9. **The interview against Wrong person and Scam.** "She" is the same person on both days, and nobody feels sorry for a scammer.

**How it plays in cross-examination.** Each collision becomes a question with no good answer, such as "Which is it: you didn't know it was her, or you knew and thought she was 23?" After two or three of those, the panel stops listening to the parts that are true and that help him: the refusal, and a history that involved adults only.

**The way out.** One account, and it has to be the true one. A workable test: it can be told in three or four sentences; it matches every text, every phone record and the video; and every exhibit the defense offers supports it. Anything that does not fit is dropped, however appealing on its own. Because the interview statement came first, the account has to begin with it, not go around it. Section 19.2 records his objection that more than one thing can be true at once. One account can have several parts. It has to be the same account each time.

### 8.4 What this changes

1. **The risk on Specs 1 and 2 is higher than the first draft assessed.** The first draft treated "did he know who he was texting" as open. The relayed statement goes a long way toward closing it against him.
2. **The decision to testify is harder.** If he knew it was the same person, he cannot say otherwise on the stand, and the statement can be used against any testimony that contradicts it.
3. **The phone evidence is better used for a narrower purpose.** It supports "the investigators' summary is incomplete" and "he had reason to doubt she was 15." It does not support "he did not know who she was."
4. **Negotiation deserves a serious conversation with counsel.** The PHO recommended a bad-conduct discharge with no confinement. The refusal, the tone of the interview and the "not minors" finding carry weight in that setting. Which specifications a plea would cover, sex-offender registration and the Texas case all belong in that conversation. The decision is his. A guilty plea to Spec 1 or 2 in the court-martial would require him to admit under oath that he believed she was under 16 (section 11.5, point 3).
5. **The first step is unchanged.** Watch the interview with counsel before committing to any account.

## 9. Addendum 2, 21 Nov 2026: the two-day timeline and "Ok. Where at?"

**What this covers.** The two timeline images supplied after section 8 (April 30, items 1 to 9; May 1, items 10 to 27), and the defense position that "Ok. Where at?" answered "we have a room" and did not accept an offer.

**Short version**

- The timeline corrects the earlier timing errors and adds useful context. Its grey entries, which include every message to another person, are assumptions until the forensic examiner confirms them.
- It gives the government as much as it gives the defense. The UC was contacted last, after five others. The texting app was installed seven minutes before the first UC message. The apps were removed about three minutes after he was asked what car he was in.
- Reading "Ok. Where at?" as a question about the room is arguable, and its meaning is for the fact-finder. It is a low-value fight, because "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra" show agreement to pay without it.
- The hotel search supports only the part nobody disputes, that "where" was a real question about location. It is also a step in planning the trip.

### 9.1 The May 1 afternoon, timeline and UC log together

| Time | Event | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 13:11 | Texting app reinstalled | Assumed |
| 13:48 to 15:30 | Messages to five other profiles (Jane Does 1 to 5) | Assumed |
| 14:04 | Search for a new texting app | Verified |
| 15:45 to 15:50 | Gas and food stop, E Stan Schlueter Loop | Shown as verified |
| 15:49:15 | "Are you still around?" to the UC | UC log |
| 15:53:50 | "Ok. Where at?" | UC log |
| 15:57 | Searches the Belton hotel | Verified |
| 15:58:25 | "Its 20 min away from me" | UC log |
| 16:13 to 16:23 | Walmart, Harker Heights | Shown as verified |
| 16:48 | On scene; the door photo with the room arrives at 16:48:45 | Shown as verified; UC log |
| 16:49 | Tries to park at the La Quinta | Shown as verified |
| 16:51:08 | "Cash right?" | UC log |
| 16:58:19 | "I have tinted windows if you can carplay. Ill give extra" | UC log |
| 16:58:57 | UC asks what car he is in; he answers "Black suv" | UC log |
| 17:02 | Texting apps uninstalled | Shown as verified |
| 17:02 | Arrest, by the jail card (section 22.6) | Evidence packet |

On April 30 the timeline shows the app installed at 16:26, the first UC message at 16:33, the conversation ending at 16:43, the image search at 16:54, class work from 18:30, and the phone off until 22:10 (assumed).

### 9.2 What it adds for the defense

1. **The times now match the exhibits.** The search is at 16:54, after the chat ended at 16:43, and the re-contact is at 15:49.
2. **April 30 ended with ordinary obligations.** After the search he went to a class presentation, and there was no further contact with the UC that day.
3. **May 1 looks like an ordinary day with a round of contacts.** Rent, a tire appointment, Walmart, and six profiles messaged, of which the UC was one. Two of the others (Jane Does 1 and 2) were also people he had messaged the day before. If confirmed, going back to the previous day's numbers was his routine, which supports "Are you still around?" as a stock line.
4. **It separates what is verified from what is assumed.** That is the right habit, and everything the defense produces should follow it.

### 9.3 What it adds for the government

1. **The UC was contacted last.** Five other profiles between 13:48 and 15:30, then the UC at 15:49. The government's reading is that he tried everyone else first and went back to the one he had refused when nothing else worked. That fits the interview statement and the deck's own "compulsion" account. The order that shows "one of many" also shows "last resort." Section 19.2 notes another reading: her conversation may simply have been the next one down the list.
2. **Going back to yesterday's numbers means he kept track of them.** To message Jane Does 1 and 2 again he needed their numbers or threads from April 30. The prosecutor will ask why the third April 30 contact was the only one he did not recognize.
3. **The app was installed at 16:26 and the UC was messaged at 16:33.** That suggests the app was set up for this contact, which reads as planning and concealment. It fits a habit of installing the app for each use just as well (sections 13.1 and 14.3).
4. **The number was the same on both days.** The UC log is one thread, so the May 1 messages came from the April 30 account. Many texting apps keep conversations on their servers and restore them at login. If this one does, reinstalling at 13:11 would have brought the April 30 exchange back, unless he had deleted that thread. The examiner should identify the app and how it behaves. Section 12.2 takes this further. The provider's help pages have since answered both questions (section 14.2).
5. **A search for a new texting app at 14:04.** It is verified, and the timeline does not explain it. Expect to be asked why he wanted another number that afternoon. He has since given a reason (section 13.5).
6. **Steady movement toward Belton.** Gas stop, hotel search, "20 min away," Walmart, on scene. That is a deliberate trip, and it undercuts "I did not intend to meet anyone." Expect to be asked what he bought at Walmart and whether he took out cash. Receipts and bank records will show it either way. He says there was no plan, and the records before 15:58 fit that (section 19.3).
7. **The La Quinta at 16:49.** Trying to park at another hotel a minute after arriving reads as watching for police or a set-up. That fits caution about any illegal meeting and says nothing about age. It is not what someone does who has decided not to meet. A federal appeals court has counted watching the meeting place among the signs that an accused knew the persona's age (section 20.4).
8. **The uninstall at 17:02.** It came about three minutes after the UC asked what car he was in and he answered. The government will call that destroying evidence while waiting for her to come down. "Disengaging" is more persuasive if he was driving away. The arrest report and video will show the time of arrest and whether the vehicle was moving. Section 13.1 covers the habit he says this was part of. Section 14.3 covers the record of it. The jail card gives the arrest as 5:02 pm, the same minute (section 22.6). Section 24 takes the question in full.

### 9.4 Gaps and inconsistencies to fix

1. **Slide 4 against the timeline.** Slide 4 says four other recipients were "verified" at 13:49, 13:56, 15:18 and 15:45. The timeline shows five, at 13:48, 14:06, 14:58, 15:18 and 15:30, all marked as assumptions. The 13:56 message in slide 4's screenshot, sent from the regular messaging app, is not on the timeline.
2. **The basis for the grey entries.** They are inferred from hotel searches. A hotel search shows he was looking at hotels. It does not show a message, a recipient, or that the recipient was an adult. Until the examiner or the app provider's records confirm each one, the defense should not describe them as texts.
3. **Grey entries before the install.** Items 1 and 2 are assumed to be texting-app messages at 12:22 and 15:17 on April 30, but the app is shown as installed at 16:26. Either there was an earlier install or those messages went another way.
4. **The sting search is missing.** Slide 8 refers to a "pre-arrival internet search specifically looking for signs of a sting operation." It is not on the timeline. Its time and exact wording matter.
5. **Unexplained or untimed entries.** Item 24, "text from unknown" at 16:23; the peace-sign request; "Black suv"; the arrest.
6. **Each Jane Doe entry is also an account of another attempt to buy sex.** Counsel should weigh that before any of it is disclosed, because it invites questions about every one of them.

### 9.5 "Ok. Where at?"

The exchange, from the UC log:

| Time | Sender | Message |
|---|---|---|
| 15:51:36 | Him | "Can I send a Uber for you?" |
| 15:52:10 | UC | "no im workin wit my girl tonight" |
| 15:52:55 | UC | "we have a room" |
| 15:53:32 | UC | "2 for 1 tonight 100" |
| 15:53:50 | Him | "Ok. Where at?" |
| 15:56:39 | UC | "comfort inn belton" |
| 15:57 | Him | Searches the Belton hotel (Google activity) |
| 15:58:25 | Him | "Its 20 min away from me" |

**The defense reading at its best.** He had asked whether he could send an Uber. She answered no, they had a room. "Ok. Where at?" completes that exchange: fine, where is it? On this reading "Ok" answers her reply to his own question, and the price line is an upsell he did not respond to. That is a coherent reading, and what a short text meant is a question of fact for the panel or jury.

**Why most fact-finders will still read it as going along with the offer**

1. It came 18 seconds after the price, which is long enough to read it. The 2-second gap before "Yes" does real work. This gap does not.
2. He did not question or refuse the price or the second girl, then or later.
3. What followed points the same way: "Just you….then both," which slide 7 says answered "2 for 1 tonight 100"; "Do guys have pictures?"; "Cash right?"; "Ill give extra."
4. He had paid for sex before (PHO finding 4g), so slide 5's claim that "2 for 1 didn't make sense until later" will not be believed.

**What the hotel search adds.** It shows that "where" was a real question about location, which nobody disputes. It does not help decide what "Ok" answered, because on both readings he wanted to know where the room was. It also works for the government: looking up the hotel, saying "Its 20 min away from me" and driving there is the planning of the trip. And it sits badly with slide 7, which says the address is what made him think it was a scam.

**What winning the point would be worth.** Little.

- It would correct PHO finding 4d, that he agreed to pay $100 for both, as to the moment of 15:53:50.
- It would not remove agreement to pay. "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra" show that on their own, and "extra" assumes a price already understood. Spec 3 and the Texas charge do not need "Ok."
- It does not touch Specs 1 and 2, which turn on who he believed she was.
- The reading concedes that he wanted to know where the room was. The next question is "Why?", and the answer collides with "I did not intend to meet anyone."

**How to use it.** As a narrow, accurate correction: the message is ambiguous, and the PHO treated it as a firm agreement to a price. Say it once and move on. It should not be paired with "I didn't understand 2 for 1," and it should not be offered as one of three misreadings (section 8.3, point 7). If the Spec 3 conduct is conceded for credibility (section 5, point 1), this point may not be worth raising at all. He has since paired it with a reading of "100" as a time (section 16.1).

### 9.6 Additions for the forensic examiner, through counsel

1. Which texting apps were used; install, uninstall and login events on both days; whether the app restores history at login; whether the UC thread was deleted, and when.
2. Each grey entry: recipient, time, app, content, and the ad it answered.
3. How the 15:49 message was started: from an ad page, typed, or from an existing thread.
4. The source and precision of the location entries.
5. The texting provider's own records for the account.
6. The arrest time and the vehicle's position, from DPS records and video.

He should keep following counsel's instruction not to reinstall the app or open the account through the cloud. Any change to the account now could be read as tampering. Section 11.1 covers how the records can be obtained without his touching it.

## 10. Addendum 3, 21 Nov 2026: the defense's single account of May 1

**What this covers.** The account supplied after section 9, set out as given and then tested against the texts, the phone records and the interview statement. I have not seen the second photo, the other May 1 conversations or the video.

**The account as given**

1. "Are you still around?" was meant for a woman he had been texting earlier on May 1. It went to the UC's number by accident. He never meant to contact the person from April 30.
2. The reply gave a location different from the one he expected, so he suspected a scam and asked for a picture. He thought the picture was fake. "Im convinced lol" was sarcasm, and he asked for another picture with a peace sign.
3. The second picture resembled the woman on the Twitter profile. He compared it with that profile, or possibly with a picture on the ad site. He did not realize it belonged to the person from the day before.
4. Together, these partly explain why what he said in the interview that evening was inaccurate.

**Short version**

- Settling on one version of how the May 1 message was sent is an improvement. Step 1 is also the most valuable thing the defense could prove, because it would take away the government's best fact, the deliberate return.
- Step 1 has a practical problem. The easiest way to text the wrong person is to open the wrong conversation, and if the UC conversation was open, the April 30 refusal was on the screen.
- Step 3 hurts as stated. He found the Twitter profile only by searching the April 30 person's photo. Checking a May 1 photo against it means he linked the two.
- The account stops at the second photo. It does not say what he believed when he arrived, asked "Cash right?" and offered extra, which is what Specs 1 and 2 turn on.
- The three steps do not explain the interview statement. They describe the opposite of it. What might explain it is what the agents had told him before he made it, and the video will show that.

### 10.1 What the account improves

1. **One version of the re-contact.** The deck had two (section 8.1, point 5). This keeps the one on slide 3, which was already in the deck. Slide 2(e) should be withdrawn.
2. **It can be checked.** The earlier conversation is on the phone or in the provider's records, or it is not. An account that records can prove is worth more than one resting on his word.
3. **It fits his strongest facts.** The one time age came up, he refused within 37 seconds, and the PHO found his history involved adults, "not minors." An accidental contact is consistent with both.
4. **It fits some of the texts.**
   - "Are you still around?" is a natural next line in a conversation from earlier the same afternoon (slide 3 points to one at about 15:18). He used similar lines with other numbers on April 14 and April 25 (slide 4).
   - "Can I send a Uber for you?", sent before any location was given, reads more naturally as the next step in an arrangement already under way than as an opening offer to someone he had turned away the day before.
   - The 3:18 PM lookups of hotels in Round Rock and Georgetown suggest the woman he had been texting was somewhere other than Belton.
   - "Like temple? Or killeen?" can be read as a man trying to place a town he was not expecting.
5. **If it is true, most of the deck's other explanations are not needed.** A man who believed he was texting an adult he had spoken to earlier that afternoon has no need for the three misreadings, the "bluff," or "I did not intend to meet anyone." Each of those that stays in gives the cross-examiner material.

### 10.2 Step 1: the accident

**What it would and would not do.** It answers the question the government most wants asked: why go back to her? It does not answer the charges by itself. The messages and acts charged came later (15:59 to 16:00, the drive, 16:58), so what counts is what he believed at those times.

**How the number was chosen.** For the message to reach the UC, the UC's number had to be selected. There are three ways that could have happened, and each should leave a trace.

| How the number was chosen | What follows |
|---|---|
| He opened the existing UC conversation from the app's list | The April 30 exchange, including "i will be 16 in june" and his refusal, was on the screen above the box he typed in, and would have stayed in view for the first few May 1 messages |
| The old conversation had been deleted, and he picked the number from a list of recent numbers or a suggestion | Possible in some apps. The examiner would have to show that this app does it and that the conversation was deleted before 15:49 |
| He typed or pasted the number | He took it from somewhere, and the examiner should be able to say where. If the UC's ad was open on his phone, that is the slide 2(e) version, not an accident |

The account needs two things that pull against each other. The UC's number had to be easy to pick by mistake, and the April 30 conversation had to be gone. The easier the number was to pick, the more likely the conversation was there to be read. The defense has since said which row applies: the first (section 12.2). Section 14.2 sets out what the provider says about deleted conversations. His later answers leave the first two rows open (section 15.2).

**What the texts do not show.** If he thought he was talking to a woman from earlier that afternoon, some replies should have seemed wrong. She said she was working with a friend, quoted a price, said she was not from the area and named a town other than the one he says he expected. He never wrote "I thought you were somewhere else" or "who is this?" The prosecutor will go through each reply and ask why not.

**What he has to be able to say.** Which woman he meant to text, what that conversation said, and why "Are you still around?" was the next thing to say to her. If he does not remember, he cannot testify to it, and the records have to make the point without him. He has since said that he does not know how the message came to be sent (section 19.2).

### 10.3 Step 2: the scam suspicion

1. **The sequence is longer than the account suggests.** He was told "belton" at 15:54:04 and "comfort inn belton" at 15:56:39. He did not ask for pictures until 16:01:35. In between he sent "Lol ok.", looked up the hotel, wrote "Its 20 min away from me", answered "Yes" and wrote "Just you….then both." His first response to the location was to work out the distance.
2. **One text in that stretch helps.** "Lol ok.", sent 11 seconds after "comfort inn belton," can be read as doubt. The defense is entitled to say so.
3. **"Do guys have pictures?" follows the two-girl exchange.** It came 53 seconds after "both if u want" and asks about both of them. The natural reading is interest in the offer. Checking for a fake is a possible reading, and the later peace-sign request supports it.
4. **The sarcasm point is fair but small.** The peace-sign request shows the first photo did not convince him. It came about seven minutes or more after "Im convinced lol", after "let me know when u pull up," and it begins "Ok." He was agreeing to come and asking for proof in the same message.
5. **Scam or sting.** Slide 8 says he searched before arriving for signs of a sting operation. A scam is someone after a deposit. A sting is the police. A search about police shows he was worried about being caught, which fits any illegal meeting. It does not fit a belief that nobody real was there.
6. **It still does not fit what he did.** After Walmart in Harker Heights he drove to Belton, asked "Cash right?" and offered extra (section 8.3, point 4). The account has to say whether the suspicion ended, and when.

### 10.4 Step 3: the second photo

As stated, this step does the most damage. Section 11.2 assesses a later detail about what he remembered of the profile. The packet shows the photo itself: a peace sign held across the face, with no face visible, followed by his "Awesome" (section 22.2).

1. **The Twitter profile is in this case for one reason.** He found it at 16:54 on April 30 by searching the photo of the person who had told him, 14 minutes earlier, that she would be 16 in June. If on May 1 he checked a new photo against that profile, he had linked the person he was texting with the April 30 ad. The account says he recognized the face without remembering where he knew it from. Section 8.3 called this remembering the answer and forgetting the question. The new detail makes it concrete.
2. **It puts the link before the acts that matter most.** From the excerpts, the second photo appears to have arrived after the peace-sign request and before the door photo at 16:48:45. Unless the comparison was made later than that, "Cash right?" and the carplay offer came after it. On the defense's own account, the government can point to a moment when he had reason to know who she was, and to what he did next.
3. **The government's reading accounts for every fact.** She said 15. He refused, looked her up and found an adult's profile. The next day he went back, asked for pictures, demanded a peace-sign photo and checked it against that profile. On that reading he knew who she was and was testing whether she was really the adult in the photos. What follows from that depends on the question in section 8.2: whether going ahead while unsure of her age is enough. That reading is simpler than the three-step account, and a fact-finder offered a simple story and a complicated one usually takes the simple one.
4. **The two alternatives lead to different places.** If he compared the photo with the Twitter profile, the profile was in his mind or on his phone on May 1. If he compared it with a picture on the ad site, the question is which ad. The ad of the woman he meant to text would match only if it used the same woman's photos as the UC's. The UC's own ad is the April 30 ad.
5. **There is no record of it yet.** The May 1 timeline lists no image search, profile view or ad page between 16:02 and 16:48.
   - If the phone recorded a fresh search on May 1 that led to the profile, that is better evidence of an adult belief than the April 30 search, because it is from the day of the charged acts. It also means he landed on the same profile twice in 24 hours.
   - If the phone recorded nothing, the comparison was made from memory, and a memory of that profile is a memory of the April 30 search.
6. **Then or now.** If he noticed the resemblance on May 1, it is evidence of what he knew that day. If it was noticed since, on reviewing the evidence, it says nothing about May 1 and does not belong in the account.
7. **Which account these facts fit.** A verification photo that matches an adult's profile is a reason to believe the person was an adult. That fits "I knew who she was and came to believe she was 23" more easily than "I did not know who she was." Only he knows what he believed. This is an observation about fit, not a suggestion about what to say.

**What the account leaves out.** The table sets what he did against what the account says he believed.

| Time on May 1 | What he did | What the account says he believed |
|---|---|---|
| 15:49:15 | "Are you still around?" | He was texting a woman from earlier that day |
| 15:53:50 | "Ok. Where at?" | The same |
| 15:54 to 15:58 | Told Belton; looks up the hotel; "Its 20 min away from me" | He began to suspect a scam |
| 15:59 to 16:00 | "Yes"; "Just you….then both" | Not stated |
| 16:01 to after 16:09 | Asks for pictures; "Im convinced lol"; peace-sign request | The first photo was fake |
| Apparently before 16:48:45 | Receives the second photo | It resembled the adult profile; he did not connect it to April 30 |
| 16:48 to 16:59 | Arrives; "Cash right?"; carplay offer; "Black suv" | Not stated |
| 17:02 | Removes the texting apps | Not stated |

The rows marked "Not stated" are the ones the charges rest on. Section 19.2 records his objection that a person need not hold one belief at each of these moments.

### 10.5 The interview

1. **The three steps do not explain the statement.** They describe a man who did not know who he was texting. The statement describes a man who did: "I refused and thought the next day she just needed money." A confusing afternoon can explain a vague answer. This one is specific. It has a refusal, a reason and a plan.
2. **One half of the statement agrees with the deck.** Slide 9 says that when signs pointed to a real person he "thought maybe I should only give money and leave." The interview has him thinking "about just giving money and leaving." That thought looks like a real memory. What the account still has to explain is the other half: who "she" was, and why she "just needed money."
3. **What might explain it.** By the time he said it he had been arrested and was being questioned. If the agents had already told him he had been texting the girl from April 30, he may have retold the afternoon using what he had just learned. That is a known way for an account to go wrong, and it is more specific than "poor recall." It can also be checked, because the video shows what he was told and in what order. Counsel can say whether an expert is needed to make the point.
4. **The account implies a moment the recordings should contain.** If he did not know until he was told, there was a moment when he found out. His reaction then, on the interview video or the arrest recording, is the best evidence available in either direction. Surprise would support the account. If he speaks of her as the same person before anyone tells him, the account cannot stand.
5. **The government has one fixed version, the interview.** Every later version will be compared with it. The account has changed again during this review, and each written version that differs from the last is material for cross-examination if the government obtains it. Drafts kept outside the attorney-client relationship may be obtainable, and that may include this document and the conversation that produced it. He should ask counsel before adding to either. Section 13.6 lists the points on which more than one version has now been given.

### 10.6 Questions the account will draw

No answer shapes are offered. Each answer has to be what he remembers. Questions 5 to 8 have no good answer unless the records and the video supply one.

1. "Which woman did you mean to text? Where is that conversation?"
2. "How did this number get into the message? Did you open the conversation from the day before?"
3. "She told you Belton. You did not say you thought she was somewhere else. You asked whether that was near Temple or Killeen."
4. "You say the location told you it was a scam. Your next messages were 'Its 20 min away from me,' 'Yes' and 'Just you….then both.'"
5. "You checked her photo against a Twitter profile. That is the profile you found the day before, 14 minutes after she told you she was 15?"
6. "So you remembered the profile, but not why you had looked it up?"
7. "After the photo matched, who did you think you were driving to meet?"
8. "That evening you told the agent she just needed money. Where on the recording do you say you texted the wrong person?"
9. "When did you first tell anyone that this was a mistake?"

### 10.7 What would prove or disprove it

**For the forensic examiner, through counsel** (in addition to sections 7 and 9.6)

1. The conversation he meant to continue: the number, the app, every message, whether "Are you still around?" or any follow-up was ever sent to it, and whether anything in it leads up to "Can I send a Uber for you?"
2. Whether the UC conversation existed on the phone at 15:49, what it displayed, and how the 15:49 message was started. Section 12.2 sets out tests for this.
3. All browser, Google and app activity from 16:02 to 16:48 on May 1: image searches, X or Twitter views, ad pages.
4. The second photo: what it shows, whether it includes a peace sign, when it arrived, and what he sent next.
5. The search for signs of a sting: its time and exact words.

**From discovery.** Every ad posted in the operation, with its photos and phone number. If an ad he answered on May 1 used the same woman's photos as the April 30 ad, that would help explain the confusion. If none did, the resemblance in step 3 points only to April 30.

**From the recordings.** The items listed in section 8.2. Above all, whether he was told who he had been texting before he made the "needed money" statement, and how he reacted.

### 10.8 What this changes

1. **Step 1 is worth pursuing hard, through the examiner.** If the records show the other conversation and an empty UC conversation at 15:49, the position on Specs 1 and 2 improves considerably, though the interview statement would still need explaining. If they show the April 30 conversation was on the screen, step 1 should be dropped.
2. **Step 3 should not be put forward in its present form.** As written, it supplies the recognition the government would otherwise have to infer. It needs the records first, and it has to be what he remembers. Section 11.2 assesses the later detail that he remembered the profile only as that of a 23-year-old nursing student.
3. **The account needs an ending.** It has to say what he believed on arrival, and that has to fit "Cash right?", the carplay offer, the removal of the apps and the interview. The later detail supplies one for the period after the second photo (section 11.2).
4. **Texas.** If he did not know he was dealing with the person who had said she was 15, the question is whether § 43.021(b)(2)(B), "represented to the actor as being younger than 18," requires that he knew. The text does not say. Texas counsel should answer that before the accident account is relied on there.
5. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third. Committing to this account before the first two are in hand risks having to change it again.

## 11. Addendum 4, 21 Nov 2026: three further points and the "counter-disclosure" question

**What this covers.** Three points supplied after section 10, and one question. The points are recorded as relayed. I have not seen the second photo, the advertisement or the video.

1. He did not mean to contact the April 30 person again. I take that as his sincere position.
2. Counsel has told him not to reinstall the texting app or open the account through the cloud.
3. The second photo links to April 30 on paper. In his memory it did not. From the April 30 search he kept only the picture, the age, and that the profile belonged to a nursing college student.
4. The question: if the government treats "i will be 16 in june" as proof that he knew her age, why is a profile showing the same picture with an adult's age not treated as a disclosure that she was an adult, or at least as cancelling the first?

**Short version**

- The search is evidence, and in the court-martial it is a real reason to doubt that he believed she was under 16. Nobody has weighed it yet, because it has not been put in front of anyone who decides. The defense presented no evidence at the preliminary hearing.
- Both are express statements of age. They differ in whose age they give. In the text, the person he was dealing with stated her own. The profile states its owner's. Getting from one to the other takes a belief that the profile's owner was the person texting.
- Neither cancels the other as a matter of law. Both are evidence of what he believed, and a fact-finder weighs them. Courts have upheld convictions where a profile gave an adult age and a text gave a younger one.
- In Texas the search may not bear on guilt, because the offense can rest on what he was told.
- Point 3 gives the account something it lacked, a belief at the time he arrived. It depends on his having kept the profile in mind while losing the reason he looked it up.
- Two steps are urgent because the evidence can disappear: securing the texting provider's records, and capturing the profile.

### 11.1 Points 1 and 2: his intent, and counsel's instruction

1. **His intent.** Whether he meant to contact her again is a fact about his own mind. This analysis cannot test it and does not dispute it. What it can do is say how a fact-finder would test it and what would prove it. Section 10.2 does that, and it stands.
2. **Counsel's instruction is sound, and it has a consequence.** He cannot check his memory against the account, so proof of the accident has to come from elsewhere:
   - the government's forensic copy of his phone, if one was made on or near May 1;
   - the texting provider's own records;
   - the undercover side's records.
3. **The phone may no longer hold the conversations.** Removing an app from an Android phone normally removes the data it stored there, and the timeline shows the apps removed at 17:02 on May 1. The conversation he says he meant to continue may now exist only with the provider, or in a copy the government made.
4. **Time matters.** Nearly seven months have passed. Services of this kind commonly delete inactive accounts and reassign their numbers. This provider says a number lapses after 30 days without use (section 14.2). Two questions for counsel:
   - Has a preservation letter gone to the provider?
   - Can the account's records be requested with his written consent? The Stored Communications Act lets a provider release the contents of a message with the consent of its sender or recipient (18 U.S.C. § 2702(b)(3)). Whether to do that, or to go through the court-martial or the Texas court, is counsel's decision. Sections 25.3 and 25.7 add how the provider can be reached and what to ask it for.

### 11.2 Point 3: what he remembered of the profile

**What it adds**

1. **A belief for the last part of the afternoon.** On this detail, from the second photo onward he took the person he was texting to be the woman on the profile, an adult. That covers the drive, the arrival, "Cash right?" and the carplay message. On my understanding of attempt law, an adult belief held at those times answers the charges that rest on them. Whether it has to be reasonable as well as genuine is an open question (end of 11.3). His later answers say that at the hotel he still expected a scam (section 16.4). The first sentence of this point was my inference from what he had said about the second photo. He has since called it misleading, because the recipients were unknown to him. It is withdrawn (section 19.2).
2. **It does not reach the earlier messages.** "Yes" and "Just you….then both" were sent at 15:59 to 16:00, before either photo. For those, the account still depends on step 1, that he thought he was texting a woman from earlier that day.
3. **It supplies the ending** that section 10.8 said the account needed. Section 19.2 restates the ending in his terms.
4. **It fits texts the deck struggled with.** A man who believes he is about to meet an adult whose photo he has checked would ask "Cash right?" and might offer extra for the car. No misreading or bluff is needed to explain them. He has since explained both texts in other ways (section 16.1).

**What it costs**

1. **The split memory.** He has to have kept the picture, the age and the nursing detail for 23 hours, and lost the reason he looked the profile up: the person using that picture had just told him she was 15. Memory does fail in this way. People keep what they learned and lose where or why they learned it, which psychologists call a source-memory error. But this was not idle browsing. The search came 11 minutes after an exchange striking enough that he told her to find a different way of life. A fact-finder will find the split hard to accept on his word alone.
2. **Rhode Island.** The profile's one-line description gives the nursing ambition, the age and the state together. Expect: "You remembered 'nurse' and '23.' The next words are 'Rhode Island.' Why did you think she was in a hotel in Belton?" The college email is a second item placing her there (section 12.1). Section 13.4 records his answer.
3. **Recognition before arrival.** Section 10.4 stands. If he recognized the woman on the profile, the government will say he recognized the ad.
4. **It conflicts with other parts of the deck.** Slide 8 says he had identified the photo as fake. Slide 9 says he did not intend to meet anyone and took "friend" as a code word for a handler. A man who believed he was meeting a 23-year-old nursing student did not hold those beliefs at the same time.
5. **The interview.** If he believed that evening that she was a 23-year-old nursing student, that is what he would be expected to tell the agent. As relayed, he said she needed money.

**What would support it**

1. **The second photo.** Does it show a peace sign, and is it the same woman as the first photo and the profile? A verification photo of the same woman, produced on request, would give anyone a reason to think she was real. Its time is not in the excerpts, and it decides which acts the belief covers. The packet answers this: it arrived at 16:11:48 and shows a peace sign held across the face (section 22.2).
2. **A record of the comparison.** A search or profile view on his phone on May 1 between 16:02 and 16:48.
3. **The video.** Any mention of "nurse," "23," or looking her up.
4. **Whether the ad's picture is the profile's picture.** A reverse image search returns look-alikes as well as copies. Once the ad is produced, the examiner can say which this was.
5. **A memory expert,** if counsel thinks one would be admitted and would help. Section 18.6 sets out what such an expert could do, and how that differs from a hypnotist.

### 11.3 The question: why the search is not treated as a disclosure that she was an adult

**The answer in three parts**

1. **It has not been treated as anything, because it is not yet in any record.** The PHO's findings rest on the government's packet. The defense offered no evidence at the hearing. As far as the relayed interview shows, he did not mention the search to the agents. Before it can be weighed it has to reach the people who decide: the prosecutors, in negotiation, and the fact-finder, through the forensic examiner. When and how is for counsel.
2. **"Knew" is not the test in the court-martial, and that helps him.** Being told is easy to prove. But the charges are attempts, and an attempt is judged on the circumstances as the accused believed them to be (Manual for Courts-Martial, pt. IV, ¶ 4.c.(3)). In sting cases the specification usually reads "a person he believed to be a child" (for example *United States v. Axe*, N-M. Ct. Crim. App. 2020). The charge sheet here should be checked for that wording. So, on my understanding, the government has to prove more than that he was told. It has to prove beyond a reasonable doubt that on May 1 he believed she was under 16. The text is evidence that he did. The search is evidence that he did not. Neither is the belief itself.
3. **So the search does not have to cancel the text. It has to leave a reasonable doubt.** That is a lower bar than the question sets, and it is the better way to put the point.

**Why a fact-finder will not weigh the two equally**

| Point of comparison | The text | The profile |
|---|---|---|
| Form | An express, written statement of age | An express, written statement of age, supported by a dated college record |
| Who is speaking | The person he was dealing with | A third party with no part in the exchange |
| About whom | Herself | The profile's owner, the woman in the ad's photograph |
| What has to be assumed before it gives the texter's age | Nothing | That the person texting was the profile's owner |
| What he did on receiving it | Refused within 37 seconds, which shows he believed it | Nothing yet shown: no message, note or statement |
| When | 16:40 on April 30 | 16:54 on April 30, 11 minutes after the conversation ended |

1. **The row on what has to be assumed is the weak point.** When a photograph in an online ad traces to a private person's account in another state, the ordinary conclusion is that the photograph was borrowed. A borrowed photograph says nothing about the age of whoever placed the ad. His own conduct points to that conclusion: the next day he asked for a picture "so I know real," and slide 8 says he had identified the first photo as fake.
2. **The government will concede that the profile's owner is an adult.** It costs them nothing. The profile states an age, dates from July 2015 and carries a college record from 2021 (section 12.1). Sting operations commonly use pictures of adults. Their case is about the age the person texting stated, not about the woman in the pictures. Section 13.3 takes up the point that the profile states an age and is not only a picture.
3. **Courts have upheld convictions on this pattern.**
   - *United States v. Fulsom* (N-M. Ct. Crim. App. 2026): the persona was listed as 18 on an adult dating app and said by text that she was "almos 15." Convictions for attempted sexual assault and attempted sexual abuse of a child were affirmed.
   - *Perales v. State*, 622 S.W.3d 575 (Tex. App. 2021): the profile said 18, the defendant said the photos made him think she was about 23, and she told him by text that she was 16. The court held the jury could infer that he believed the text.
4. **Both cases differ from this one in a way that helps him.** In each, the accused was told the younger age and carried on with the conversation. In *Fulsom* the agents also gave him chances to back out, which he did not take. Here he took the chance on April 30, and the excerpts show no mention of age on May 1. That contrast is the defense's best point on belief, and it does not depend on the search.

**What would give the search more weight**

1. The second photo, if it is a verification photo of the same woman (11.2).
2. Any record made before the arrest that shows what he concluded from the search.
3. The advertisement. If it gave an adult age, the government's own statements to him conflicted. That is a stronger form of the argument than a third party's profile.
4. The video, if he mentions the search.

**Its limits**

1. **It has force only if the profile was in his mind on May 1.** There are three versions of that day.

   | Version | Did he know who she was? | What the search does |
   |---|---|---|
   | The government's | Yes, and he remembered "15" | Nothing. He went ahead anyway |
   | He knew, and the search had changed his mind | Yes, but he had concluded she was 23 | It is the reason for an adult belief |
   | His account, with point 3 | No, but he recognized the woman on the profile | It is the only age he connected with her |

   In a version where he neither knew who she was nor recognized the picture, the search plays no part. So "the search negated the disclosure" and "I did not know it was her" can be said together only in the third version, which rests on the split memory.
2. **Counsel can argue in the alternative. He cannot testify in the alternative.** A lawyer may tell the fact-finder: "He did not know it was her. If you think he did, the last thing he had learned about her was that she was 23." From the witness stand he can give only one account. The search itself can come in through the examiner without his testimony. What he concluded from it can come only from him.
3. **Doubt about her age may not be enough.** If the search left him unsure, the government may ask for a "deliberate avoidance" instruction: a person who suspects a fact and deliberately avoids confirming it can be treated as knowing it. Military courts recognize the instruction but limit when it can be given. *United States v. Brown*, 50 M.J. 262 (C.A.A.F. 1999), found it was error to give it on the facts there. Slide 1's point that he asked no questions on May 1 invites it. Whether it is available on the belief element of an attempt is a question for counsel.
4. **Genuine or reasonable, and whose burden.** For the completed offense, a mistake about age is a defense only if it was reasonable, and the accused has to prove it (Article 120b(d)(2); R.C.M. 916(j)(2)). For elements that require specific intent, R.C.M. 916(j)(1) says a mistake "need only have existed in the mind of the accused." Under the first rule he would have to prove that an adult belief was reasonable. Under the second, a genuine belief is enough and the government has to disprove it. I could not confirm which a military judge would apply to an attempt where there was no child. The search supports a genuine belief more easily than a reasonable one, so counsel should settle this first. Section 12.1 cites a case stating the general rule.

### 11.4 Texas

1. **The statute has three branches.** Texas Penal Code § 43.021(b)(2) applies if the person was (A) younger than 18, (B) "represented to the actor as being younger than 18," or (C) "believed by the actor to be younger than 18." The State can charge (B). A 2026 court of appeals decision upheld the evidence on a charge worded that way (*Brown v. State*, No. 06-25-00151-CR, Tex. App., Texarkana).
2. **On branch (B), the search probably changes nothing.** Under that branch the text is not evidence of what he knew. It is the thing the statute asks about: what was represented to him. The statement was made, and the person who made it never withdrew it. What he later concluded from another source goes to branch (C), which the State does not need.
3. **Two arguments remain for Texas counsel.**
   - The advertisement. If it gave an adult age, was she "represented" as under 18 when the representations conflicted?
   - Identity. If he did not know he was dealing with the person who had made the statement, does branch (B) still apply (section 10.8, point 4)?
4. **On belief, a Texas jury is free to reject this kind of argument,** as *Perales* shows.
5. **In Texas the search may be worth more for negotiation and punishment than for guilt.**

### 11.5 What this changes

1. **How to use the search.** Not as a disclosure that cancels the text. As a dated, pre-arrest reason to doubt that he believed she was under 16, proved by the examiner and argued by counsel.
2. **The belief defense has a core that depends on neither step.** The one time he believed she was a minor, he refused. Everything he was shown afterwards was an adult. Age was not mentioned on May 1. The accident, if proved, adds an explanation for the whole afternoon. Point 3, if it is what he remembers, adds one for the period after the second photo.
3. **A plea.** A military judge will accept a guilty plea only if the accused admits under oath the facts that make him guilty. There is no plea that maintains innocence (Article 45, UCMJ; R.C.M. 910). If his position is that he never believed she was under 16, he cannot plead guilty to Spec 1 or 2. Spec 3 is different, because age is not part of it. So the disposition the PHO suggested depends on which specifications it would cover. Section 21.5 notes what a plea to Spec 3 would involve. Texas does allow a no-contest plea, which is a matter for Texas counsel.
4. **Two urgent steps, both through counsel.**
   - The texting provider's records (11.1).
   - A capture of the profile as it stands, and any archived copy from about April 30. Profiles are edited and deleted. He should not contact the account holder.
5. **Discovery.** The advertisement, and where its photographs came from. Whether the operation's plan required the agent to restate the age or offer a way out, and whether either was done on May 1.
6. **The written record.** A federal court ruled in February 2026 that a defendant's exchanges with an AI tool, made on his own initiative, were protected by neither attorney-client privilege nor the work-product doctrine (*United States v. Heppner*, S.D.N.Y.). That is one court, and it does not bind a court-martial or a Texas court. But it describes this document and the conversation behind it. Counsel should be told that both exist.

### 11.6 Questions these points will draw

No answer shapes are offered.

1. "The profile said Rhode Island. Why did you think she was at a hotel in Belton?"
2. "You remembered her age and that she wanted to be a nurse. You did not remember that you looked her up because she had just said she was 15?"
3. "If you believed she was 23, why did you ask for proof that the picture was real?"
4. "When the second picture came, did you open the profile again? Where is that on your phone?"
5. "That evening, did you tell the agent you had looked her up and she was 23?"
6. "If you believed she was a 23-year-old adult, why did you think about giving her money and leaving?"

## 12. Addendum 5, 21 Nov 2026: the college email and the unsaved number

**What this covers.** Two details supplied after section 11, and one image. They are recorded as relayed.

1. On the profile he viewed more than the profile picture. He also viewed a posted photo of a college email, which adds to the grounds for taking the profile's owner to be an adult.
2. Whatever else is in doubt, the May 1 message was not meant for the undercover number. That number was not saved as a contact. In the texting app it appeared as an unsaved number, one of several conversations listed by number only.

**The image.** A photo of a computer screen showing an email dated Sunday 9 May 2021, sent from an address at ric.edu, the domain of Rhode Island College. The email tells a student her score on a lecture component and her average, a B. The poster's caption says she had thought she would fail anatomy. The student is addressed by a first name that matches the profile's. The image also shows an instructor's name and email address. Names are left out of this document.

**Short version**

- The email is good evidence for a point the government was unlikely to contest: the profile's owner is an adult.
- Its better uses are narrower. It matches what he says he remembered of the profile, and it matters if his belief has to be reasonable as well as genuine.
- It also helps the government. It is a second item placing the owner in Rhode Island, which supports the conclusion that the ad's pictures were borrowed.
- The unsaved-number detail gives the mistake an ordinary mechanism and answers one prosecution argument.
- It also settles a question against him. He opened the existing conversation, so the April 30 exchange was there to be seen unless the app did not display it. That conflicts with slide 3, which says the conversation may have been deleted. Section 15.2 reopens this.
- The same things decide the case as before. This section adds tests the examiner can run on the accident.

### 12.1 The college email

**What it adds**

1. **A dated document in place of a self-description.** The age in the profile's description is whatever the owner typed, and it carries no date. The email is a record from a third party showing the owner in a college course five years before these events. On any reading she was an adult by April 2026.
2. **The two items agree.** Someone who was 23 in 2026 was about 18 in May 2021, the age of a first-year student.
3. **It matches what he says he remembered.** Section 11 recorded that he kept three things from the search: the picture, the age, and that she was a nursing college student. Nursing is in the profile's description. College is in this email. A memory that matches the record in its details is easier to believe than a bare claim.
4. **It shows he looked at more than one item from the account.** That supports a settled impression that the woman in the pictures was an adult. How he reached the email, through the search results or by going through the profile, is for the phone's records to show.
5. **It matters most if reasonableness is required.** See below.

**What it does not add**

1. **The owner's adulthood was not going to be disputed** (section 11.3). The email makes more certain a point the government would concede.
2. **The gap is where it was.** Who owns the profile and who was texting are different questions. The email says nothing about who was on the other end of the conversation.
3. **Rhode Island, twice.** The description says Rhode Island, and the email comes from a college there. The more convincingly the profile belongs to a real student in Rhode Island, the more natural the conclusion that her pictures were taken for an ad in central Texas. A prosecutor will say the email shows the photos were borrowed, and that he knew it, because he asked for proof that the picture was real. Section 13.4 records his answer on this.
4. **When he saw it has to be shown.** The Google activity on slide 1 lists three image views at 4:54 PM on April 30. I cannot tell from the thumbnails whether the email is one of them. The copy supplied to me carries no embedded date or device information. If the email was first seen after the arrest, it is evidence about the profile and not about what he believed.
5. **It is more time spent on her, minutes after the refusal.** Section 3 noted the argument that he was checking whether she was really 15. More viewing gives that argument more to work with. The answer has to be his real reason for looking.

**Where reasonableness comes in**

- Section 11.3 left open whether an adult belief has to be reasonable or only genuine. *United States v. Binegar*, 55 M.J. 1 (C.A.A.F. 2001), states the general rule: a mistake about an element requiring specific intent need only be honest, and a mistake about a general-intent element must be honest and reasonable. I have not found a decision applying that rule to an attempted Article 120b offense where there was no child. It remains a question for counsel. Three service courts have since been found that treat the belief as the government's to prove in such a case (section 23.5).
- If the belief has to be reasonable, the email is among the most useful items the defense has, because it is a document and not his word. But reasonableness is judged on everything he knew. That includes what she had said about her age and where the profile placed its owner.
- If a genuine belief is enough, reasonableness still matters in practice. A fact-finder decides whether a belief was really held partly by asking whether a sensible person could have held it.

**Handling the image**

- It shows a private person's first name and an instructor's name and email address. Neither has any part in this case. It should go to counsel and the examiner only, with those details redacted in anything shared more widely.
- The examiner should capture the post from the live profile with its date, and show from the phone's records when it was viewed.
- He should not contact the account's owner (section 11.5).

### 12.2 The unsaved number

**What it adds**

1. **A mechanism.** Section 10.2 asked how the undercover number came to be selected. The answer now given is the first row of that table: he opened a conversation from the app's list. Each entry in the list was a bare number, with no name. Picking the wrong one is an ordinary mistake, and most people who text have made it.
2. **It answers one prosecution argument.** Section 3 and cross-examination question 4 turned on his failing to notice the number. With no names attached there was nothing to notice but ten digits.
3. **Not saving the number fits not meaning to use it again.** It shows little by itself, because he saved none of them.
4. **It fits the first two messages** (section 10.1).

**What it settles against him**

1. **He opened the existing conversation.** The April 30 exchange was part of it. It was short, and it ended with four lines: her statement of her age, his refusal, his "Not in rude way.", and her "k i get it." Unless the app did not display earlier messages, those lines were directly above the box where he typed "Are you still around?" They would have stayed in view while the first replies arrived. His later answers bear on this (section 13.4). He has since said the conversation was possibly deleted (section 15.2).
2. **A conversation list usually shows more than a number.** Most apps show the last message and its date beside each entry, and put the newest at the top. For this conversation the last message was "k i get it," which says nothing about age, but its date was the day before. The conversations he had used that afternoon would have carried that day's times and sat above it.
3. **It conflicts with slide 3.** Slide 3 says the conversation was possibly deleted. A deleted conversation is not in the list to be opened. Both can be true only if the app brought the conversation back when he reinstalled at 13:11, and the examiner can test that. If it did, that would help explain the mistake, because he would have had no reason to expect a conversation from the day before in the list. If it did not, slide 3 and this detail cannot both be right. The provider's help pages say that removing the app loses no conversations and that deleting one is permanent (section 14.2). He has since said the conversation was possibly deleted, and section 15.2 takes up the conflict.
4. **The accident explains the first message, not the hour that followed.** Sections 10.2 and 11.2 cover what he believed after it.

**Tests for the examiner, through counsel**

1. **The screen.** What the app displayed when that conversation was opened at 15:49, after a reinstall at 13:11: whether earlier messages appeared, how many fit above the keyboard, and whether a date line separated the two days.
2. **The other conversation.** Slide 3 points to a conversation at about 15:18, Jane Doe 4 on the timeline, as the one he meant to continue. What was last said in it, and whether "Are you still around?" follows from it. Above all, what he sent to her after 15:49. If he went quiet in that conversation at the moment he began in this one, that supports the mistake. If he went on messaging both, he could tell them apart.
3. **The list.** Which conversations were in the app at 15:49, in what order, and what each entry showed. Slide 4 shows at least one May 1 message sent from the phone's ordinary messaging app, and the timeline refers to texting apps in the plural, so the conversations may not all have been in one list. If the two conversations sat next to each other, a slip of the finger is believable. If they were far apart, the mistake was one of recognition, taking one number for another, and how alike the two numbers looked becomes relevant.
4. **Deletion.** Whether deleting a conversation in this app removes it from the provider's servers, and whether a reinstall restores it. The provider's published answers are in section 14.2. The examiner should confirm them for the version he used.

### 12.3 What this changes

1. **The account is more coherent than at any earlier point.** Each step now has a mechanism, and most of the deck's other explanations can be dropped (section 10.1).
2. **Slide 3 has to be reconciled with it or withdrawn** on the question of deletion.
3. **The linchpin has moved.** Section 3 said the unproven point was whether the conversation had been deleted. On this account it had not been, or it had come back. The central forensic questions are now what the screen showed and what he did in the other conversation.
4. **The email should be used for what it proves:** that the profile's owner is an adult, and that his memory of the profile matches the record. It should not be offered as proof of who was texting, and the Rhode Island point should be expected.
5. **Section 1 lists three things the account asks a fact-finder to accept on his word.** These details remove none of them. The examiner's tests can remove the first.
6. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third.

### 12.4 Questions these details will draw

No answer shapes are offered. Section 13.4 assesses the answers since given.

1. "The conversation you opened had your own words in it from the day before. Were they on the screen when you typed?"
2. "Every other conversation in that list was from that afternoon. This one was dated the day before. Did you look at the date?"
3. "After 15:49, what did you send to the woman you say you meant to text?"
4. "Your slide says you may have deleted that conversation. Now you say you opened it by mistake. Which is it?"
5. "She had just told you she was 15. Why were you still looking through her pictures?"
6. "The email is from a college in Rhode Island, and the profile says Rhode Island. Why did you think she was in Belton?"

## 13. Addendum 6, 23 Nov 2026: his responses to points in this analysis

**What this covers.** Responses supplied after section 12 to two cells of the table in section 4, to section 11.3, and to the six questions in section 12.4. They are recorded as relayed. I have not seen the video, the forensic report, the texting app or the other conversations.

1. The habit of deleting conversations and removing the texting app predates May 1.
2. The interview statement "extrapolated usual trend, without included core details."
3. The Google activity shows that the adult profile was worked out before the arrest, so "worked out afterwards" was wrong.
4. On section 11.3: what the search found was an adult's profile that displays an age, and not only an adult's picture.
5. Answers to the six questions in section 12.4. Section 13.4 sets them out.
6. Within those answers, a new detail. He had meant to block the undercover number. The app unblocks a number once a message is sent to it. He did not want to message it by accident, so he looked at other texting apps, and stayed with the one he had.

**Short version**

- Two corrections are right, and the document has been changed. The profile states an age in writing (section 11.3). The records of it were made before the arrest (the table in section 4).
- The habit helps if records show it from before April 30. May 1 is too late a date to measure from. He has since said they do (section 14.3).
- His explanation of the interview describes something that does happen. The words as relayed are specific, though, and his response accepts that the statement left out the details his account depends on.
- Of the six answers, two are "I don't recall." If true, they are the right answers. The government's points are about what the app showed, though, and those answers leave them standing. One corrects a premise of mine. One is a fair answer. Two cannot be relied on until the records are in.
- The intention to block her number is the most important new detail, and it cuts both ways. It gives the app search an innocent meaning. It also means that, on his own account, he had singled out her number that afternoon. The provider's help pages have since confirmed what he said about the app (section 14.1).
- On the points that matter most, he recalls what helps and does not recall what would hurt. That may be how memory works, but a cross-examiner will make it the theme. The remedy is records.

### 13.1 The habit

1. **What it would do.** If removing the app and clearing conversations was his routine, the removal at 17:02 and anything he deleted on April 30 stop looking like a reaction to what she told him. The government's concealment evidence would then show only that he hid buying sex, which he does not dispute.
2. **The date that matters is April 30, not May 1.** The first thing the government can call a reaction to her is whatever he did after 16:40 on April 30. A habit that can be shown only from that evening onward began with her. The records need to show the same cycle on earlier occasions. Slide 4 refers to messages to other numbers on April 14 and April 25, so installs and removals around those dates are the place to look. He has since said the Google activity shows this (section 14.3).
3. **The timeline already fits a cycle.** The app was installed at 16:26 on April 30 and, on the timeline's assumption, reinstalled at 13:11 on May 1, so it was removed in between. Section 9.3 read the 16:26 install as preparation for this contact. Installing the app for each session explains it equally well.
4. **It has to be proved by records.** The Google account's Play Store history and the phone extraction should show each install and removal with its date. His saying so adds little.
5. **The rule.** M.R.E. 406 allows evidence of a person's habit to show that he acted in accordance with it on a particular occasion. As I understand it, a habit is a regular response to a repeated situation, and a few instances may not be enough. Here the act is not disputed, only the reason for it, so counsel may treat this as a question of ordinary relevance. Either way it is for counsel.
6. **The removal at 17:02 still needs its own explanation.** It came about three minutes after he described his car, while he was waiting. If his routine is to remove the app when he has finished, then at 17:02 he had finished something. The government will say it was the arranging. The defense has called it disengaging. Removing the app cut off his only line to her, which fits the defense reading better. The arrest time and the car's position will show which it was (section 9.3, point 8). Section 24.4 qualifies this: by 17:01:20 nothing was left to arrange, so he no longer needed the line for the meeting.
7. **The habit and the mistake pull against each other.** If he cleared conversations every time, the April 30 conversation should not have been in the list at 15:49. He now says conversations from the previous day were in the list (13.4). The provider's help pages, supplied since, say that removing the app loses no conversations and that deleting one is permanent (section 14.2). So the habit that fits his answers is removing the app, not deleting conversations. Section 15.2 covers his later answer that hers was possibly deleted.

### 13.2 The interview, and "worked out afterwards"

**The correction.** He is right about the records. The profile views were logged on April 30, and the look at other texting apps at 14:01 to 14:04 on May 1. Both were made before the arrest. Section 8.1 already called the first the hardest defense evidence to dismiss as an invention, and the table in section 4 now says so. What that cell was aiming at is narrower, and it stands.

1. **A record shows what he did, not what he concluded.** The log shows the profile was on his screen. It does not show what he made of it, or that he had it in mind on May 1.
2. **The explanation of the records came after the arrest.** As far as the relayed statement shows, he did not mention the search, the profile or a mistake to the agents. The account in sections 10 to 13 was given months later.
3. **Why the order matters.** By the time of the arrest he had every reason to explain himself, so what he has said since does little to support his own testimony. As I understand M.R.E. 801(d)(1)(B), an earlier consistent statement answers a charge of recent fabrication only if it was made before the motive to fabricate arose. What he did before the arrest is not open to that objection, which is why the two records are worth so much.
4. **A third record would be worth more than either.** Any trace on the phone, between the second photo and his arrival on May 1, of the profile being opened again (section 10.4, point 5).

**The explanation of the statement.** I read "extrapolated usual trend, without included core details" to mean this: when the agents asked, he described what he would usually think or do, and left out what had happened that afternoon, which was the wrong conversation, the scam suspicion and the photo. If he meant something else, the rest of this subsection may not apply. He has since put it in his own words: a generalized version, stated inaccurately (section 16.5).

1. **It describes something that happens.** People asked why they did something often answer from a general idea of themselves, and do not notice that they are not remembering. The best-known study is Nisbett and Wilson, *Psychological Review* (1977). Arrest and questioning make it more likely, and so does a question that already contains the premise.
2. **The words as relayed are not general.** "I refused" is an event. "The next day" is a date. "She" is one person. "I thought about just giving money and leaving" reports a thought. A general answer sounds like "I usually" or "I would have." The video will show which this was. Hedges such as "I guess" or "I must have" would support him. So would an agent supplying "the next day" or "she" before he used them.
3. **If paying and leaving is something he has thought of or done before, with adults, that fact matters more than the word "extrapolated."** Section 8.2 said the thought fits best with someone he believed he should not touch. That inference weakens if he has had the same thought about adults. It can be used only if it is true, and it needs support from something other than his word. It would also bring his history into the trial, which counsel has to weigh (section 7, point 5).
4. **Slide 9 has to be read with it.** Slide 9 presents "give money and leave" as a thought he had at the hotel, and section 10.5 treated it as a real memory. If the interview was a generalization, the defense has to say whether slide 9 is one too. He has since said he took the question as hypothetical, which is a third description of the same words (section 16.5).
5. **"Without the core details" is the government's point.** His response accepts that the statement left out the mistake and the profile. On his account, by the time he arrived he believed he was meeting the woman on the profile, and not the woman he had set out to text. Question 15 in section 6 asks why he told the agents neither. The explanation now offered answers a different question: why what he did say was inaccurate.
6. **There are two possibilities, and they lead to different testimony.**
   - He knew that evening that he had texted the wrong person, and did not say so. Then the omission needs a reason, and the video may supply one (section 10.5, point 3).
   - He did not understand how it had happened until he saw his records. That is an honest position and can be said plainly. It means the account of 15:49 is partly a reconstruction, and he can testify only to the parts he remembers. Section 13.6 separates the two.

### 13.3 The profile's stated age

**The correction.** He is right, and the table in section 11.3 now has a row for it. The text and the profile are both express, written statements of age. The profile has more behind it than a typed number.

1. **It cannot belong to a 15-year-old.** It shows a joining date of July 2015. Someone who would be 16 in June 2026 was five years old then.
2. **The age can be out of date in one direction only.** If "23" was written some time ago, the owner is older now.
3. **The college email is a third party's record from 2021** (section 12.1).

So if he took the person texting to be the profile's owner, he took her to be an adult. Nothing on the profile leaves room for anything else. He has since said that he did not identify her in that way (section 19.2).

**How it compares with the cases.** In *Fulsom* and *Perales* (section 11.3) the adult age was on the persona's own profile, it was 18, and the persona contradicted it in the same conversation. Here the adult age is specific, has documents behind it, and does not come from the person texting. On what the profile says, his position is stronger than in those cases. On the connection it is weaker. There the profile was the persona's own. Here it belongs to someone else.

**What the correction does not change**

1. **The row on what has to be assumed.** The profile gives its owner's age. It gives the texter's age only if he believed they were the same person.
2. **His own account dates that belief.** He says the first photo looked fake and that he asked for a second to be sure. On that account the belief began with the second photo. The profile's age covers what he did after it. The earlier messages still rest on the mistake (section 11.2).
3. **How he came to know the profile.** He found it by searching the ad's photo, during or just after the April 30 conversation (13.4). The three versions in section 11.3 stand.
4. **Texas.** Branch (B) asks what was represented to him (section 11.4).

**The argument in its strongest form, for counsel.** Nobody mentioned age on May 1. He asked for proof that the woman in the pictures was real, and was sent a picture he matched to a profile that gave her age as 23, dated from 2015 and carried a college record from 2021. On any version of events, that profile was the most recent thing he had seen about the age of the woman in those pictures.

### 13.4 The six answers

The questions are those in section 12.4. Each answer is recorded as relayed and then assessed for how it would be received. No wording is suggested.

**1. Were the April 30 messages on the screen when he typed?**

*As relayed: he does not recall seeing his words.*

- This is an answer about memory. If it is true, it is the only honest one. "I did not see them" claims more, and he should say it only if that is what he remembers.
- It does not meet the question. The government's point is what the screen showed, and the examiner can establish that without him. A fact-finder will then assume he saw what was in front of him, unless there is a reason he did not.
- The claim is a narrow one. New messages would probably have pushed the last lines of the April 30 exchange (section 12.2) off the screen within a few minutes. So what he has to be believed about is the first few minutes after 15:49. The timeline puts him at a gas and food stop until 15:50, and counsel can use that for the first message. It helps less with each message after it.

**2. Did he look at the date of the conversation?**

*As relayed: some of the other conversations were from the previous day as well.*

- This corrects the premise of the question. I had assumed the other conversations were all from that afternoon. If several were from the day before, hers did not stand out for that reason.
- It also concedes something. Conversations from April 30 were in the list on May 1, so the app kept them or brought them back. If hers was among them, its April 30 messages were there too (13.1, point 7). He has since said hers was possibly deleted (section 15.2).
- The order of the list is still open. Most apps put the most recently used conversation at the top. The one he meant to continue was from that afternoon. Hers had last been used the day before. The examiner's reconstruction of the list (section 12.2) will show how far apart they were.
- Section 9.3 made a related point. He messaged two of the previous day's contacts again on May 1, so he could tell those conversations apart.

**3. What did he send the other woman after 15:49?**

*As relayed: something like "I was on the way."*

The answer can be read three ways.

- *He sent it in the undercover conversation, believing it was hers.* Then he sent nothing in her own conversation after 15:49. Section 12.2 said that would support the mistake.
- *He sent it in her own conversation after 15:49.* Then he was using both conversations and could tell them apart. It also raises a question: on the way where? From 15:58 he was arranging to go to Belton.
- *He sent it to her before 15:49.* If he had told her he was coming and she had gone quiet, "Are you still around?" is the natural next message. That would help him.

His memory should not be what settles this. The provider's records will show which it was. An answer from memory that the records contradict costs more than "I don't recall."

**4. Deleted, or opened by mistake?**

*As relayed: he does not recall. Counsel has told him not to open the app or the messages. He does remember meaning to block the number.*

- "I don't recall" is consistent with slide 3, which says he cannot definitely remember deleting it.
- It leaves slide 3 and section 12.2 unreconciled, and only the examiner can reconcile them. His answer to question 2 shows that the previous day's conversations were kept. Whether hers was among them is still open (section 15.2).
- The rest of the answer is new. Section 13.5 deals with it.

**5. Why was he still looking through her pictures?**

*As relayed: the search was made at the same time as the conversation. He put the phone down briefly and then went back to it.*

- This says when. The question was why.
- The time of the search has now been given three ways: 16:42 to 16:50 on slide 2, 16:54 on the two-day timeline, and now during the conversation. These may be parts of one sequence, with the search started at about 16:42 and the results opened at 16:54. The records will show the first entry. The defense should give the time once, from the records.
- When it began changes what it means.

  | The search began | What that suggests |
  |---|---|
  | Before 16:40:09 | A check on the ad, begun before age came up |
  | Between 16:40 and 16:43 | A check on what she had just said. That ties the profile to her statement, which his account says he did not connect |
  | After 16:43 | Interest in her that continued after the refusal |

- On any of these, he went back to it. The profile was viewed at 16:54 and the account's handle was searched at 16:58, both after the refusal. The question still applies to those minutes, and the answer has to be his real reason.
- If the records show him searching the photos in other ads before April 30, that is a routine, and the question loses most of its force. He has since said he reverse-searches photos often (section 16.1).

**6. Why did he think a woman from Rhode Island was in Belton?**

*As relayed: his own profile gives New York and Cambridge and shows no activity since 2022, though he logs in often.*

- This is a fair answer, and most people would accept it. Locations on profiles go out of date.
- The government's own exhibit supports it. On May 1 the UC wrote "im not from here."
- The 2021 date on the email helps too. Five years is long enough to have moved.
