- Using his own account as the example invites questions about that account. The same point can be made from the exhibits, if counsel makes it (section 19.1).
- Location was the smaller half of the argument. The larger half is his own doubt that the pictures were real (section 11.3). This answer does not reach it. On his account the second photo does.

### 13.5 The number he meant to block

**The detail, as relayed.** He remembers that he was going to block the undercover number. The app unblocks a number automatically once a message is sent to it. He did not want to message it by accident, so he thought of moving to a different app. He looked at others and stayed with the one he had.

**When.** He gave no time. The phone records a Play Store search for "2nd number" and visits to the pages of three second-number apps at 14:01 to 14:04 on May 1. Nothing else supplied matches "looking at other apps," so this subsection assumes that was the occasion. If it was April 30, the first two costs below lose most of their force, and the May 1 search is left without this explanation.

**What it gives him**

1. **A reason for the app search that has nothing to do with wanting her.** Section 3 listed the search among the exhibits that needed explaining. Wanting to be rid of her number is the best explanation it could have.
2. **Conduct before the arrest that matches the refusal.** He refused her on April 30. Wanting her number blocked the next day is the same attitude.
3. **He passed her over.** The timeline puts the search between messages to two people he had also messaged the day before (both entries are assumptions). If the records confirm that, he came to her number while going through the previous day's contacts, and did not text it.
4. **The worry supports the mechanism.** A man worries about texting a number by accident only if the numbers are hard to tell apart. That is what section 12.2 describes.
5. **It is partly against his interest.** An invented detail would not put her number in his mind that afternoon. Counsel can say that this is a mark of a real memory. The government will answer that it was offered to explain a record the deck had already disclosed.
6. **It can be checked** (below).

**What it costs**

1. **It puts her number in his mind about an hour and three-quarters before he texted it.** The account's first step is that he opened her conversation without knowing whose it was. On this detail he had picked that conversation out of the list the same afternoon.
2. **He foresaw the mistake and then made it.** The government will not accept that as coincidence. It will say that a man does not fear texting a number by accident, and that what he feared was wanting to. That reading fits "compulsion" (section 3), the order of contacts, in which she came last after five others (section 9.3), and the interview.
3. **The simplest step was not taken.** Deleting the conversation takes it out of the list. He does not recall whether he did. The provider says deletion is permanent, so a conversation that was in the list at 15:49 had not been deleted (section 14.2). He will be asked why not. He has since said that deleting seemed more effective than blocking, as I read him, and that the conversation was possibly deleted (sections 15.1 and 15.2).
4. **He stayed with the same app.** Having seen the risk and looked at the alternatives, he changed nothing.
5. **He searched for a second number.** Any texting app would have given him a list without her in it. The government will say a new number is what a man looks for when he is hiding.
6. **The legal-help app.** The same Play Store session includes two visits at 2:02 PM to the page of an AI legal-help app. On this account the session was about her number, so he will be asked what legal question he had in mind. It may have been no more than a suggested result.
7. **It is a new reason for a record the deck had already explained.** As I noted it, slide 3 presents the app searches as part of removing and replacing these apps out of shame. I no longer have the deck to check the wording. If the reasons differ, the slide has to be reconciled with this or withdrawn (13.6).
8. **It strains the rest of the account.** At about 14:00 he remembered the April 30 exchange well enough to want her blocked. Later that afternoon he matched a photo to the profile he had found during that exchange and, on his account, did not connect the two. The second item on section 1's list of things to accept gets harder.

**What would support it**

1. **How the app behaves.** When this section was first written I could not confirm that any texting app lifts a block automatically when the blocked number is messaged. He has since supplied the provider's help page, and it says that this one does (section 14.1). The examiner should still test the version he used.
2. **A block that was actually set.** He says he was going to block the number, not that he did. If he did, the provider may have logged it, and the message at 15:49 would have lifted it. A block on her number, followed by an unblock at 15:49, would be strong support. He has since said he had blocked numbers in the app before (section 15.1).
3. **How he learned what the app does.** A notice on the block screen, a help page or a search would each leave a trace. A search or page visit about blocking, made before 15:49, would turn this from an explanation into evidence. Section 14.1 explains why it matters which page. He has since said he read the provider's page weeks or months before April 30 (section 15.1).
4. **The conversation list at about 14:00.** Where her conversation sat, and what he did in the app between 13:48 and 14:06.

**Questions it will draw.** No answer shapes are offered.

1. "At two o'clock you knew which number was hers. At 3:49 you texted it. What changed in between?"
2. "Why did you think you might text her by accident?"
3. "You were worried about that conversation. Did you delete it?"
4. "You looked at three other apps and installed none of them. Why?"
5. "What were you looking for in a legal-help app at 2:02?"
6. "When did you first tell anyone that you had meant to block her?"

### 13.6 What he remembers, what he does not, and what is inferred

Sorted from everything relayed so far.

| He says he remembers | He says he does not recall, or did not notice at the time | Inferred from the records, by him or by this analysis |
|---|---|---|
| Refusing her on April 30 | Seeing the April 30 messages when he typed at 15:49 | How her number came to be selected at 15:49 |
| The profile: the picture, the age, a nursing student, the college email | Why he had looked the profile up, when he matched the second photo to it | Which conversation he meant to continue (slide 3 points to the one at about 15:18) |
| Starting the search during the conversation, putting the phone down and going back | Whether he deleted the conversation | The times in grey on the timeline, including every message to another person |
| Meaning to block her number; looking at other apps; staying with the same one | Parts of what he said to the agents (slide 10) | That the app search at 14:01 to 14:04 was when he meant to block her |
| Meaning to text another woman at 15:49 | Which picture he compared the second photo with | |
| Suspecting a scam once he had the location; meaning "Im convinced lol" as sarcasm | | |
| That the second photo matched the woman on the profile | | |
| That some conversations in the list were from the previous day | | |
| Sending the other woman something like "I was on the way" | | |
| Writing "nah.....just one...maybe two ..later" on May 1 (section 25.5) | | That this was the message the log has at 16:00:22 as "Just you....then both" |

1. **The pattern.** Most of the first column helps him. Most of the second would hurt if he remembered it the other way. A cross-examiner will say that his memory fails where he needs it to.
2. **It may be how memory works.** People remember what they meant to do and what struck them. They do not remember what was on a screen, or whether they pressed delete. A fact-finder can accept that. It is easier to accept from a witness who also remembers things that do not help him, and the intention to block her is one.
3. **The remedy is records.** Each item in the second column has a record that can answer it. Where the record goes against him, "I don't recall" was the safe answer. Where it goes for him, the record makes the point better than he could. Section 17.2 sorts every point he has made by the record behind it.
4. **The third column should stay where it is.** If he gives an inference as a memory and the records disagree, he loses the parts that are true as well. Section 19.2 moves one entry into it: he now says he does not know how the message at 15:49 came to be sent, so "meaning to text another woman" no longer belongs in the first column.

**Points that have been given more than one way**

| Point | Earlier | Now |
|---|---|---|
| How the May 1 message came to be sent | Slide 2(e): he saw the ad's picture again and contacted it. Slide 3: it was meant for someone else | Sections 10 and 12: meant for someone else; he opened the wrong conversation in a list of unsaved numbers. Section 15.2: if her conversation had been deleted, her number was entered some other way. Now: he does not know. He thinks it more likely that he answered her ad or messaged several recent conversations (section 19.2) |
| The April 30 conversation | Slide 3: possibly deleted. Section 12: in the app as one of several unsaved numbers | He does not recall deleting it (13.4). Possibly deleted, with many others present (section 15.2) |
| The app search at 14:01 to 14:04 | Timeline: no reason given. Slide 3, as I noted it: removing and replacing these apps out of shame | To avoid messaging her number by accident (13.5). "For the same reason" as his nervousness, a scam (section 16.1) |
| When the image search was made | Slide 2: 16:42 to 16:50. Two-day timeline: 16:54, after the conversation | During the conversation, with a break |
| "Give money and leave" | Slide 9: a thought he had at the hotel | The interview statement was a generalization (13.2). An answer to a question he took as hypothetical (section 16.5) |
| "2 for 1 tonight 100" | Slide 5: "2 for 1" did not make sense until later | The same position, as he explains it: the offer was not overtly accepted. The reading of "100" as a time was added in section 16.1 (section 19.2) |
| "Cash right?" | The deck, as section 4 recorded it: scam testing | Meant for someone else (section 16.1). Section 32.3 records an argument about what the message shows if it is read as a question to her. That is an argument for counsel, and not a further account of what he meant |
| The words of one message on May 1 | Section 16.1: the line at 16:00:22 was his answer to the repeated proposition. Its words were not disputed | He recalls writing "nah.....just one...maybe two ..later", which I take to be that line (section 25.5) |
| What he believed at the hotel | Slide 9: he did not intend to meet anyone. Section 11.2 added that he took her for the woman on the profile. That was my inference, and he says it is misleading | Several things at once: a scam suspected, nothing decided, the recipient unknown, no thought of a minor (section 19.2) |

Some of these are refinements and not contradictions, and the newer version is usually the more specific and the easier to check. Each is still a "which is it?" question, and the deck is a written record of the earlier versions. Section 8.3 explains the cost. Counsel should settle which slides are withdrawn before anything more is disclosed.

### 13.7 Records to look for, through counsel

These add to the lists in sections 7, 9.6, 10.7 and 12.2. Section 14 adds the provider's log of blocks, a dated copy of its help page, and whether the government obtained the provider's records. Section 15.5 adds further items.

1. **Installs and removals of the texting apps before April 30,** with dates (13.1).
2. **Earlier reverse image searches** on the photos in other ads (13.4, answer 5).
3. **Anything about blocking before 15:49 on May 1:** a search, a help page or the app's block screen, and the provider's log of blocks and unblocks on the account (13.5).
4. **The April 30 image search:** its first entry and each later view, with times (13.4, answer 5).
5. **The other woman's conversation:** the last message before 15:49, anything sent after it, and any "on the way" message in either conversation (13.4, answer 3).
6. **The conversation list at about 14:00 and at 15:49:** the order, and the date shown on each entry (13.4, answer 2, and 13.5).
7. **Any view of the profile on May 1** between the second photo and 16:48 (13.2).
8. **The video:** what he was told before the "needed money" statement, whether he hedged, and whether a mistake, a search or a profile is mentioned anywhere (13.2).
9. **The Google account's activity.** It is the source of the two best defense records, and he controls the account. Nothing in it should be deleted or changed. Counsel should say how to preserve it. A complete export through Google Takeout, made once, is the usual way. If the account is set to delete activity automatically after a period, counsel should know now. The account can also send commands to the seized phone, and none should be sent (section 26.1).

### 13.8 What this changes

1. **Two corrections are accepted,** in the table in section 4 and in section 11.3. They make the document fairer to his position. They do not change the assessment of the charges.
2. **The account is narrower and more definite.** It no longer needs a deleted conversation. It needs a few minutes of inattention at 15:49, and then a mistaken identification from a photo.
3. **The new detail raises the stakes on the first step.** If records support the intention to block her, the app search becomes defense evidence. If they do not, the detail gives the government her place in his mind that afternoon, in his own words.
4. **The interview is still the first thing to resolve.** His response accepts that the statement left out what the account depends on.
5. **Section 1 still lists three things to accept on his word.** The first is harder on its face, and easier to test.
6. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third.
7. **The written record.** This section sets out what he does and does not recall on six questions a prosecutor would ask. Sections 10.5 and 11.5 explain why a record like this may be obtainable. A practice cross-examination with counsel is the protected place for this work, and counsel should be told that this document now contains it.

## 14. Addendum 7, 23 Nov 2026: the provider's help pages and the record of the habit

**What this covers.** Two items supplied after section 13, recorded as relayed. I have not seen the Google activity. I could not open the provider's support site from here, so what follows about its pages rests on what a web search returns from them.

1. A passage from the texting provider's support site, under the heading "How do I unblock a number on TextFree Web?" It says that a user who has blocked someone need only send them a message, and that "Sending a message to a blocked contact will automatically remove the block."
2. The Google activity shows that the habit predates April 30.

**Short version**

- He was right about the app, and section 13.5 has been corrected. In this app a block would not have stopped the message at 15:49.
- The page shows what the app does. It does not show when he learned it, or that it was the reason for the app search on May 1. A record from before 15:49 would show that, and it matters which page the record is of.
- The app is now identified, and the provider's other pages settle two open points. Removing the app loses no conversations. Deleting a conversation is permanent. So a conversation that had been deleted was not in the list to be opened. He has since said hers was possibly deleted (section 15.2).
- The provider also says that a number lapses after 30 days without use and that message history may not survive long inactivity. The record that would best prove the mistake may no longer exist with the provider. Counsel needs to find out now.
- If the Google activity shows the same cycle on earlier dates, the removals stop being evidence about her. The record can show removal of the app. It cannot show deleted conversations, and on his own answers many of the April 30 conversations were not deleted. He has since said hers possibly was (section 15.2).

### 14.1 The provider's page on blocking

**What it confirms**

1. **The app behaves as he said.** Section 13.5, as first written, said I could not confirm this. The search index lists an article with the heading he quotes on the TextFree support site. The provider's general article on how to block a number, whose steps read as those of the phone app, ends with the same point: "You can lift a block at any time by sending a message to the number/contact you've blocked." So the behavior is not limited to the web version.
2. **A block would not have prevented the message.** In this app a block stops what comes in. The same article says that messages from a blocked number are undeliverable. A block does not stop what goes out. Had he blocked her number, "Are you still around?" would still have been sent, and would have lifted the block. That section also said the prosecutor might argue that blocking would have prevented this. On the provider's own description that argument is not available.
3. **His reasoning holds together.** He said he looked past blocking because it would not protect him from his own mistake. That is what the pages say.
4. **He was right about an obscure detail before it was checked.** That counts with a fact-finder.

**What it does not show**

1. **When he learned it.** The page is public and can be read today. It proves what the app does. It does not prove that he knew it on May 1, or that it was why he looked at other apps at 14:01 to 14:04. He will be asked when he first read it. He has since said weeks or months before April 30 (section 15.1).
2. **Where the knowledge came from, if not from a page.** If he had blocked numbers in this app before, the provider may have a log of the blocks and of their being lifted.
3. **It does not reach the two hardest questions in section 13.5:** why her conversation was still in his list, and why he stayed with the same app. Section 14.2 makes the first one sharper.

**A record from before 15:49 on May 1**

- Section 13.5 said that a search or a help page about blocking, opened before 15:49, would turn the detail from an explanation into evidence. That stands, and his Google activity is where it would be. On his later answer he read the page well before April 30, so the record to look for is earlier (section 15.1).
- It matters which page. The provider's article on how to block a number carries the point in its last line. A visit to that article before 15:49 would be strong support.
- The page he has quoted is headed as instructions for unblocking. It tells the reader that the way to unblock a number is to message it. If the records show that page opened before 15:49, the defense will say he was finding out what a block would do. The government will say he was finding out how to undo one, and that at 15:49 he did what the page says.
- Whether a block was ever set on her number decides between those readings. He says he was going to block it, not that he did. If none was set, there was nothing to undo. The provider's records may show which it was.
- Visits made recently are neutral. They will appear in his Google activity with their dates, and nothing should be deleted to tidy the record.

**Handling**

- TextFree Web is the browser version of the account. Reading a help page about it is not logging in to it. Counsel's instruction not to reinstall the app or open the account still applies, and counsel should be told which pages he has read.
- The page should go to counsel as a saved copy that shows its address and the date it was saved. Help pages are edited. Counsel or the examiner can look for an archived copy from about April 2026 to show what it said then.
- A help page is the provider's description. The examiner's test of the version he used is the proof. How either is put in evidence is for counsel.

### 14.2 What the provider's other pages say

As relayed, the provider is TextFree, which is made by Pinger. That allows three open points to be checked against what it has published. Each statement is the provider's description, and the examiner should confirm it for the version he used.

| Question | What the provider says | What follows |
|---|---|---|
| Does removing and reinstalling the app lose conversations? | No. "Reinstalling the app will not cause you to lose your number or any messages." The user logs back in. One page adds that messages may be lost "dependent on account usage" | After the reinstall on May 1 the conversations from April 30 were back in the list. That supports his answer that some conversations in the list were from the day before (13.4), and the mechanism in section 12.2. It also means the April 30 exchange was in her conversation, unless that conversation had been deleted |
| Can a deleted conversation come back? | No. Deleting messages or conversations "is a permanent action". Neither the user nor the provider's support staff can recover them | If he had deleted her conversation, it was not in the list to be opened by mistake. If it was in the list, he had not deleted it |
| How long does an unused account last? | The number expires after 30 days in which no message is sent and no call is made. Call and message history "may not be available after any long period of inactivity" | The points under "The expiry point" below |

**The deletion point**

1. **Slide 3 and section 12.2 cannot both be right, with one exception.** Section 12.2 said they could be reconciled only if the app brought a deleted conversation back when it was reinstalled. On the provider's statement it does not. As first written, this point went on to say that his own answers put the conversation in the list, so it had not been deleted and slide 3 should be withdrawn. That went further than his answers did. He has since said the conversation was possibly deleted. Section 15.2 sets out what follows either way, and the exception.
2. **"I don't recall whether I deleted it" is still his position.** The records have to answer it (section 15.2).
3. **If the conversation was in the list, the April 30 exchange was in it.** That is what section 12.2 said, and the provider's pages bear it out.
4. **It bears on the habit.** A habit of deleting conversations does not fit a list that still held the previous day's. A habit of removing the app does, because removal leaves the conversations with the provider (14.3).
5. **It sharpens a question in section 13.5.** Deleting her conversation would have taken her number out of the list for good, in one step. On his account he saw the risk and looked for a way to remove it. For a number that is not a saved contact, the provider's article describes the block as set from the conversation itself, by swiping on it. If he got that far, her conversation was in front of him. If it was still in the list at 15:49, he will be asked why. No answer shape is offered.
6. **A later correction.** The provider's page on saving conversations says that the result of a Personal Information Request "may include deleted messages, if they are still available." So a deletion removes a conversation from what the user sees, and not necessarily from the provider's systems. Section 25.1 takes this up.

**The expiry point**

1. **The number has almost certainly lapsed.** The apps were removed at 17:02 on May 1, and he has rightly not logged in since. On the provider's stated policy the number expired about a month later, unless a paid plan kept it. He has since said that a paid plan did keep it, and that the number expired on 26 July 2026 (section 27.1).
2. **The conversations may have gone with it.** The provider warns that history may not be available after long inactivity. That statement is about what a user sees. What the provider keeps on its own systems, and for how long, is not published. Only a request through counsel will find out. A later correction: its privacy policy does give a period for message content, which is six months on inactive accounts (section 26.4).
3. **Why this matters more than anything else in this section.** The conversation with the other woman is held only by his account and by hers, and he should not try to reach her. The undercover side holds only its own conversation. Removing the app normally removes what it stored on the phone (section 11.1). If the provider's copy is gone, the mistake may never be shown by the messages themselves. It would then rest on indirect records, such as the hotel searches, and on his word.
4. **What to ask counsel now.**
   - Whether a preservation request went to the provider, and when (section 11.1).
   - Whether the government obtained the provider's records for his account. It had both numbers from May 1. If it did, the other conversations should be in discovery.
   - Whether the government's extraction of the phone recovered anything from the app.
5. **He should not log in to see what is left.** Counsel's instruction stands. Logging in would add activity to the account's record, and I do not know what else it would change. Section 25.4 sets out what has since been found about that, and what a look by him would cost.

### 14.3 The record of the habit

*As relayed: the Google activity shows that the habit predates April 30. I have not seen it.*

1. **That is the date section 13.1 asked for.** If the activity shows the same cycle on earlier dates, with the app installed, used and gone again before the next install, then the removal after the April 30 conversation and the removal at 17:02 on May 1 stop being evidence of a reaction to her. They remain evidence that he hid buying sex, which he does not dispute.
2. **It gives the install at 16:26 on April 30 an ordinary explanation.** An install followed seven minutes later by a first message is what a session looks like for someone who installs the app each time (section 9.3, point 3).
3. **What Google's records show well, and what they may not.** They record searches and page visits in the Play Store, and when an app was used. The Play Store data in a Google export lists installs with their times. I could not confirm that any of these lists removals. Repeated installs of the same app imply the removals in between, which is enough for this point. The timeline marks the 17:02 removal as verified, so some record of removals exists. The examiner should identify it and say how far back it goes.
4. **What the record cannot show.** Deleting a conversation happens inside the app, and Google does not record it. On his own answers the April 30 conversations were still in the list on May 1 (14.2). So the habit the records can prove is removing the app. The wider claim, that he also deleted conversations as a matter of routine, is not supported for April 30 and should not be made for that day. He has since said hers was possibly deleted while many others were present. That describes one deletion, not a routine (section 15.2).
5. **The timing at 17:02 still needs its own explanation** (13.1, point 6). The earlier cycles may help. They will show at what point in a session he usually removed the app. If it was when he had finished for the day, then 17:02 marks a decision that he had finished, and the argument is about what he had finished. Section 24.5 sets out what each kind of routine would show.
6. **The same records test the blocking detail.** They will show whether looking at other texting apps was part of the routine.
   - If on earlier dates he reinstalled the same app each time and looked at no others, May 1 was different, and his reason (13.5) explains the difference.
   - If he had looked at other apps before, the search at 14:01 to 14:04 was routine. It then needs no special reason, and the government will say he has chosen the one that helps.
7. **A regular cycle means he had seen old conversations come back before.** If he logged back in to the same account each time, as he did on May 1, each reinstall brought back the list. That supports the mechanism: a list of old, unsaved numbers after every reinstall. It supports the worry in 13.5 as well. It also means he had no reason to expect a clean list on May 1, and had reason to know her conversation would be in it unless he had deleted it. He has since said that a paid plan kept the number (section 27.1).
8. **Each earlier cycle is another occasion.** The record that proves the habit also shows how often he did this. Counsel has to weigh that against the rules on other acts and against the Texas case before it is disclosed (section 7, point 5, and section 9.4, point 6).
9. **The rule.** With a run of dated cycles, a habit argument under M.R.E. 406 has something to rest on (13.1, point 5). Whether to make it is for counsel.

### 14.4 Questions these items will draw

No answer shapes are offered. Section 15 assesses the answers since given.

1. "When did you first read that help page?"
2. "Before May 1, had you ever blocked a number in that app?"
3. "Deleting her conversation would have removed it for good. Why was it still in your list?"
4. "Every time you reinstalled, your old conversations came back. So you knew hers would be there?"
5. "You say you delete these conversations. The ones from the day before were all still there, weren't they?"
6. If a block was ever set on her number: "The page says the way to unblock a number is to message it. Is that what you did at 3:49?"

### 14.5 What this changes

1. **One correction is accepted.** The app behaves as he said, and section 13.5 now says so.
2. **The blocking detail is more credible as a description of the app.** It still needs a record that ties it to May 1.
3. **The deletion question is open again.** As first written, this point said that slide 3's deletion should be withdrawn. He has since said the conversation was possibly deleted (section 15.2).
4. **The removals lose most of their force as evidence about her,** if the record is as relayed. The timing at 17:02 remains.
5. **Preservation is the urgent item.** The best evidence for the mistake may no longer exist with the provider. Counsel should be asked now.
6. **Section 1's three points to accept on his word are unchanged.** Neither item reaches what he saw at 15:49, why he remembered the profile but not the search, or what he told the agents.
7. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third.

## 15. Addendum 8, 23 Nov 2026: his answers to the questions in 14.4

**What this covers.** Answers supplied after section 14 to the six questions in section 14.4. They are recorded as relayed. Three of them can be read more than one way, and section 15.1 says how I have read each. I have not seen the Google activity, the provider's records or the app.

**Short version**

- One answer can be checked, and would help if a record bears it out. He says he read the provider's page weeks or months before April 30. A dated visit from then would show that he knew how blocking works in this app before she had any reason to matter.
- Two answers reopen a question that section 14 treated as closed. He now says her conversation was possibly deleted. Sections 14.2 and 14.5 said slide 3's deletion should be withdrawn. That went further than his answers did, and those passages have been changed.
- Deleted and not deleted lead to different accounts of the first message. If the conversation was in the list, he opened it, and the April 30 exchange was on the screen. If it had been deleted, the screen was blank, but he entered her number some other way, and the obvious source is her ad. Only the records can say which.
- He has now named three ways of keeping clear of her number: blocking it, changing apps and deleting her conversation. On his present memory he cannot say that he did any of them.
- Four of the six answers do not say what he did or saw. They give what made sense, what was possible or what he would have done. A prosecutor will press that difference, and his own explanation of the interview supplies the reason.
- This section adds no new list of questions. The next round is better done with counsel, with the records in hand.

### 15.1 The six answers

Each answer is recorded as relayed and then assessed for how it would be received. No wording is suggested.

**1. When did he first read the help page?**

*As relayed: weeks or months before April 30.*

- This helps if a record shows it. A dated visit to the provider's page before April 30 would be a record made before the arrest, and before she had any reason to matter. It would show that what he knew about blocking was not learned because of her.
- It removes one reading set out in section 14.1. That section said a visit to the unblocking page before 15:49 on May 1 could be read as finding out how to undo a block on her. On this answer the visit was long before her.
- "Weeks or months" is an estimate. The record will give the date, and he should not put one on it from memory. If the Google activity shows no visit before April 30, the answer rests on his word, and the only visits on record will be recent ones.
- The page he quoted is about unblocking. Expect: "Why were you reading how to unblock a number?" The ordinary reason is that a person has blocked someone and wants to reach them again. Answer 2 makes that question more pointed.
- The page he quoted is for TextFree Web, which the provider describes as the version used from a desktop or laptop. If he ever used that version, the browser history on that computer is another place the examiner can look (15.5). It is not something for him to open.
- It does not tie the knowledge to the afternoon of May 1. Section 14.1 asked for a record from before 15:49 that day. On this answer there will not be one of the page. Nothing dated May 1 yet shows that blocking her was on his mind when he looked at other apps.
- If he had known this about the app for months, what was new on May 1 was not the knowledge. It was her number. Both sides will say so. The defense will say he took what she had told him seriously. The government will say she was on his mind.

**2. Had he blocked a number in the app before May 1?**

*As relayed: "sure, frequently texted individuals."*

I read this as yes: he had blocked numbers before, in the course of texting many people. It may mean more narrowly that the numbers he blocked belonged to people he had texted often.

- It makes the intention to block her ordinary. Blocking was something he did, so wanting to block her number needs no special explanation.
- It explains how he knew what a block does.
- The provider may hold a record of the blocks. I could not confirm that it keeps a list or a history. Its own description says only that a user can unblock a number later.
- It draws a hard question. "You blocked numbers often. A girl told you she was 15. Did you block her that day?" On his account he was going to. The moment to do it was 16:40 on April 30. What the records show next is the search on her photo.
- If the numbers he blocked were those of people he had been texting, the prosecutor will ask what the blocks were for. A block in this app stops the other person's messages and not his own. If the answer is that they were to stop himself going back, the government has its reading of section 13.5 from his own practice: he wanted her blocked because he thought he might go back to her.
- With answer 1 it draws another. He had blocked people, and he had read how a block is lifted. "So you had blocked someone and then gone back to them?" If that is true, it is the pattern the government alleges here. If it is not, a record of his blocks would show it.

**3. Why was her conversation still in the list?**

*As relayed: "it seemed more effective than blocking."*

I read "it" as deleting. On that reading he is saying that deleting was the step he thought would work, which is the answer of a man who believes he probably deleted the conversation. Answer 5 points the same way. "It" could instead be the change of app described in section 13.5. Then the answer explains why he did not block her, and not why he did not delete her conversation.

- On my reading the answer disputes the premise of the question. He is not saying why the conversation was still there. He is saying it may not have been. Section 15.2 takes that up.
- It reports what he thought, not what he did. The next question is "So did you delete it?" On his own answers that is "I don't recall."
- It pulls against the reason he gave for looking at other apps. That reason was fear of messaging her by accident. Once her conversation was deleted, the only way to do that was to enter her number afresh, for example from her ad, and a different app would not prevent that. The two fit most easily in one order: he looked at other apps, stayed with this one, and then deleted her conversation. That would put the deletion after 14:04 on May 1.

**4. Did he know her conversation would come back when he reinstalled?**

*As relayed: reinstalling had nothing to do with any one individual.*

- This is consistent with the records so far, and worth establishing. On the timeline, the reinstall at 13:11 was followed by messages to five other people before her, though those entries are still assumptions. On the record he describes, reinstalling was routine.
- It answers a different question, which is why he reinstalled. The question asked was whether old conversations came back, and so whether he knew hers would be among them. That has a factual answer, and the examiner can give it.
- A witness who answers a different question is asked the same one again, and the fact-finder notices. A responsive answer starts with yes, no or "I don't remember." The explanation comes after it.
- His answer 5 supplies part of it. He says many other conversations were present on May 1. So he saw that old conversations were in the list. Whether hers was among them is the question in 15.2.

**5. Were the previous day's conversations all still there?**

*As relayed: possibly deleted, many others were present.*

I read this as: her conversation was possibly deleted, and many other conversations were in the list.

- "Possibly" is not a memory. A witness testifies to what he knows. As I understand M.R.E. 602, which follows the federal rule, that means matters of which he has personal knowledge. On the stand this answer becomes "I don't remember whether I deleted it," which is what he said in section 13.4.
- "Many others were present" confirms section 14.3, point 4. He did not delete conversations as a routine. If hers was deleted, it was one deletion.
- One deletion would help him in a way a routine would not. If the records show that on April 30 he deleted her conversation and kept the others, that is something he did before the arrest that matches the refusal. It would be the third such record, and the first that concerns her alone.
- It would also change how the message came to be sent (15.2).

**6. If a block had been set on her number, was messaging her at 15:49 the way he lifted it?**

*As relayed: he would not message a number in order to unblock it. If he had blocked it, the reason would have been to prevent contact.*

- The question arises only if a block was set. On his account in section 13.5 he was going to block her number. Nothing says he did. If none was set, that fact answers the question, and it is better than any argument. The provider's records may show it.
- The answer given is an argument about what he would do. It is a reasonable one. It is not a statement of what happened, and the prosecutor will say so: "I am not asking what you would do."
- The argument has two weak points. People do unblock numbers, and on answer 1 he had read how. And by his own account a block in this app does not prevent his contact with her. It prevents hers with him, and she never wrote first. So "to prevent contact" invites "whose?" That leads back to the reading under answer 2.

### 15.2 The deletion question, reopened

**The correction.** Section 12.2 read his description of the number, as one of several unsaved numbers in the app, to mean that he opened her existing conversation. Sections 13.4 and 14.2 built on that reading. Section 14.2 concluded that the conversation had not been deleted and that slide 3 should be withdrawn. His answers 3 and 5 do not accept that. The passages have been changed, and the question is open.

**What follows from each possibility.** The provider says that a deleted conversation cannot be recovered and that reinstalling loses nothing (section 14.2). On that description there are three cases.

| Her conversation was | In the list at 15:49 | On the screen as he typed | How her number got the message |
|---|---|---|---|
| Never deleted | Her conversation, with the April 30 exchange in it | The end of that exchange, above the box | He opened it from the list (section 12.2) |
| Deleted after her last message at 16:43:04 on April 30, or on May 1 before 15:49 | Nothing for her number | A new, empty conversation | He entered her number some other way: typed, pasted, picked from a suggestion or taken from an ad (section 10.2) |
| Deleted in part: before her last message arrived, or only some of its messages | In most apps, an entry holding what was left, at least "k i get it." | What was left | He opened it from the list |

1. **The first case is section 12.2.** The mistake is the ordinary one, and the refusal was in front of him.
2. **The second case clears the screen and changes the mistake.** Nothing on the screen would have told him who she was, which fits his not recalling his own words. But it was not a wrong conversation opened from a list. Her number had to be entered, and it was not a saved contact. The obvious source is her ad. That is slide 2(e): he saw the ad again and answered it. The defense set that version aside in section 10 in favor of a message meant for another woman. "Are you still around?" sent to a number taken from an ad also reads as one of his usual opening lines (slide 4), and not as a message meant for someone else.
3. **The third case is the exception noted in section 14.2.** It is the only one in which slide 3 and section 12.2 are both right. If he deleted the conversation after his "Not in rude way." at 16:42:25 and before her "k i get it." at 16:43:04, her reply would in most apps have started a fresh entry holding that one line. Something similar would follow if he deleted some messages and not the whole conversation, which the provider's pages suggest is possible. Her number would then have been in the list with nothing about age in it, though still under the previous day's date. The window is 39 seconds, and I could not confirm how this app treats a message that arrives after a deletion.
4. **He cannot testify to any of them.** He does not recall deleting the conversation. So the third case is a test for the examiner, and not something for him to adopt. If he says it and the records show otherwise, it is one more version.
5. **These are two different accounts of the first message, and the choice between them is not his to make.** In the first and third cases he opened the wrong conversation. In the second he sent a message to a number he had just entered. Only one happened. Until the records show which, "wrong conversation" and "possibly deleted" should not be put forward together. He has since said that he does not know which, and thinks an ad or a round of messages more likely (section 19.2).
6. **Where the answer is.**
   - The provider's copy of his account, if it survives (section 14.2): what it held for her number, and any record of a deletion.
   - The government's extraction of his phone, and whatever it recovered from the app.
   - His Google activity for the minutes before 15:49 on May 1: which page or app was open. Section 7 has asked for this since the first draft. If her ad was open, it was the second case.
   - The examiner's tests of the app: what a message arriving after a deletion produces, and whether the app suggests numbers that are no longer in the list.
   - Section 25 sets out what each possible finding would be worth, and why it matters how it is found.

### 15.3 What he did about her number

He has now named three ways of making sure he did not text her.

| Measure | What he has said | What the records show so far |
|---|---|---|
| Block her number | He was going to. A block would not have held against his own message | Nothing |
| Change to another app | He looked at others and stayed with this one | The Play Store visits at 14:01 to 14:04 on May 1 |
| Delete her conversation | It seemed more effective than blocking. Possibly done | Nothing |

1. **On his present memory he cannot say that he carried out any of them.** The prosecutor will put it in one question: "You thought of three ways to make sure you never texted her, and you can't tell us you did one of them?"
2. **The government's reading.** He kept returning to the problem because he did not trust himself, and each time he left the way open.
3. **The defense reading.** A man who meant to go back to her would not have thought about any of this. That he did shows what he wanted, and it matches the refusal.
4. **One record would change the balance.** A deletion of her conversation, dated before 15:49, turns the third row from something he thought about into something he did. It also brings in the second case in 15.2.

### 15.4 Memory and reasoning in these answers

| Answer | What it gives |
|---|---|
| 1. Weeks or months before April 30 | A memory, with an estimated date |
| 2. He had blocked numbers before | A memory |
| 3. It seemed more effective than blocking | What he thought was the better course |
| 4. Reinstalling had nothing to do with any one person | Why he reinstalled |
| 5. Possibly deleted | A possibility |
| 6. He would not message a number to unblock it | What he would have done |

1. **Four of the six do not say what he did or saw.** Answers 3 and 6 give his reasoning, answer 5 gives a possibility, and answer 4 answers a different question. That is natural. These are points on which he has no memory, and a person asked about something he does not remember will reason his way to an answer.
2. **It is what a prosecutor presses hardest.** "I did not ask what you would have done. What did you do?" After two or three such exchanges the fact-finder hears a man arguing his case, and not a man remembering.
3. **His own account of the interview gives the prosecutor the reason.** Section 13.2 records his explanation that what he told the agents came from his usual pattern and not from what he remembered, and that it was wrong. A fact-finder who accepts that will listen for the same thing in his testimony. He has more reason than most witnesses to keep the two apart.
4. **Reasoned answers produce new versions.** This round produced one. Section 12 had her number in the app as one of several unsaved numbers. Section 13.4 had "I don't recall" on deletion. This round has "possibly deleted." The table in section 13.6 has been updated.
5. **The remedy is the one in section 13.6.** Where he remembers, he says so. Where he does not, "I don't remember" is the answer, and the records make the point. On these six questions the records can make most of them.

### 15.5 Records to look for, through counsel

These add to section 13.7.

1. **The visit to the provider's page before April 30:** its date, which page it was, and what led to it. It will be in the Google activity if his settings recorded browsing. Nothing in that activity should be deleted.
2. **Blocks on his account:** any list or history the provider keeps, whether her number was ever blocked, and whether any number was blocked and later messaged.
3. **Her conversation:** whether it was deleted and when, and what the account held for her number at 15:49 on May 1 (15.2).
4. **How her number was entered at 15:49,** and which page or app was open in the minutes before.
5. **The app's behavior** in the two respects listed at the end of 15.2.
6. **The browser version.** Whether he ever used TextFree in a browser, and on which computer. If he did, its browser history may show sessions and their dates. The examiner should collect it. He should not log in. Section 26.5 sets out how such a device should be kept in the meantime.

### 15.6 What this changes

1. **One correction, to the previous section.** Sections 14.2 and 14.5 treated the deletion as settled and said slide 3 should be withdrawn. On his latest answers it is not settled. Neither slide 3 nor section 12.2 should be relied on until the records show which is right.
2. **One answer is worth pursuing.** If the Google activity shows the provider's page read before April 30, his knowledge of the app is proved by a record that has nothing to do with her.
3. **The practice of blocking helps and hurts.** It makes wanting to block her ordinary. It also gives the government, from his own habits, its reading of why he wanted her blocked.
4. **The first step of the account now has two forms that do not share a mechanism.** That is a larger problem than any single answer, and it is the one to take to counsel first.
5. **Section 1's three points to accept on his word remain,** with the first reworded to cover both forms.
6. **Where the next round belongs.** These answers were given without the records, and one of them moved the account. Each written version can later be set beside the next (sections 10.5 and 11.5). The questions noted in 15.1 are better answered with counsel, after the video and the forensic report, than here.
7. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third.

## 16. Addendum 9, 23 Nov 2026: his answers to the cross-examination questions in section 6

**What this covers.** Answers supplied after section 15 to questions 4 to 18 of section 6, and to the rebuttals listed under them. They are recorded as relayed. Questions 1 to 3 were not answered. Question 3, where the number came from on May 1, is the one section 15.2 left open. Several answers keep the wording of section 6, and 16.6 says which. I have not seen the video, the forensic report or the other conversations.

**Short version**

- The answers that work best are the plainest. He never believed on May 1 that he was texting a minor. The two days' conversations read differently. The one time a minor came up, he refused. His "Yes" was sent two seconds after the line it is said to answer. The last three rest on exhibits.
- Most of the others explain one message each, and the explanations are of different kinds. This round adds a second message meant for someone else ("Cash right?") and a fourth misreading ("100" as a time). Section 16.2 puts them in one table. Nearly every message that hurts now has its own explanation, which is the problem section 8.3 described.
- Most of that effort goes into denying that he agreed to pay for sex. That is Spec 3, which does not depend on age, and his other answers concede the conduct. It spends credibility he needs for the one question that decides Specs 1 and 2.
- Two answers say that at the hotel he still expected a scam. Section 11.2 recorded that from the second photo he took her to be the woman on the profile. Whether both can be true depends on what he means by a scam.
- On the interview there are two new details, and the video can check both. He says he told the agents to look at his phone. He says he took one question as hypothetical. He also says he never told them she was 23.
- "These details were unlikely to be instantly recalled" is given three times. It can explain forgotten details. The two things missing from the interview are not details. And the explanations he now gives are about what he was thinking, which no record shows.
- One sentence, as written, says the opposite of what he appears to mean (question 5).

### 16.1 The answers

The numbers are those of section 6. Each answer is recorded as relayed and then assessed for how it would be received. No wording is suggested.

**4. He checks photos and asks for verification, but did not notice the number?**

*As relayed: numbers can be changed, like the one he was using. He frequently reverse-searches images, with results.*

- The first half is a real reason. In this setting a number identifies nobody. The provider's own pages say a user may be able to change his number. A man who knows that checks pictures and not numbers.
- It moves the question from the number to the picture, and that is where the difficulty lies. If pictures are how he tells people apart, then on May 1 he identified her by her pictures. On his account he matched the second one to the profile he had found by searching the April 30 ad (section 10.4).
- His own number did not change between the two days. The undercover log is one conversation with one sender.
- The second half is new and useful. If he reverse-searched the photos in ads as a routine, the search on April 30 was routine too, and not a check on what she had just said (section 13.4, answer 5). His Google activity recorded the April 30 search, so it should show the earlier ones.
- It draws two questions. What did he do when a search showed that an ad's photo belonged to someone else? And if he searched as a rule, where is the search on the photos she sent on May 1? The timeline shows none (section 10.4, point 5).

**5. He never asked her age on May 1.**

*As relayed: "I had no reason to think it was different from posted age; so there was reason to address age discrepancy the way i asked for second photo when i thought the first was fake."*

- As written, the second half says there was a reason to address an age discrepancy. I take it that a "no" is missing and that he means this: he saw no discrepancy about age, so he raised none, and when he did see one, in the first photo, he challenged it.
- Read as written, the sentence says that age was on his mind on May 1 and that the photo request was how he dealt with it. That is the government's case in his own words. The sentence should not be left in this form in anything he keeps.
- On my reading it is slide 1's argument, and it is a fair one. His conduct shows that he questions what looks wrong. He questioned the photo. He did not question her age.
- The government's answer is that he checked everything except the one thing he did not want answered (section 11.3, on deliberate avoidance).
- "Posted age" has to be pinned down. Posted where?
  - On the ad of the woman he meant to text. That fits the mistake, up to the second photo.
  - On the profile. That fits the time after the second photo, and the profile came from the April 30 search.
  - On the undercover ad. Then he knew on May 1 which ad he was answering, which is slide 2(e).
- If the undercover ad gave an adult age, that matters for other reasons (sections 11.3 and 11.4). The ad is still not in evidence.
- "No reason to think" assumes he did not know whose number it was. A man who knew had been given a reason the day before. So this answer stands or falls with the first step of the account.

**6. "2 for 1 tonight 100." "Ok. Where at?"**

*As relayed: no. He was answering "we have a room." Later in the conversation the two-girl proposition was repeated, which he took to be because he had not answered it the first time.*

- This is the reading in section 9.5. The new point is a fair one: she raised it again, which suggests she had not taken "Ok" as his answer.
- In the excerpts I have seen, the only later mention of two before his reply is "on both of us lol" at 15:59:19. If that is the repeat he means, then "Just you….then both" at 16:00:22 was his answer to it. So on his own account he answered the proposition six and a half minutes after "Ok," and the answer was not no.
- It also shows that he read the first line as a proposition for two. That bears on the next answer.

*To the rebuttal that he read the price and never refused it. As relayed: he had not asked for a price, so he read "100" as a time, 0100, because "tonight" suggests later.*

- This is the answer most likely to cost him. Section 9.5 said the "Ok" point should not be paired with a claim not to have understood the offer. It is also a fourth misreading (section 8.3, point 7).
- His conduct does not fit it. He had just offered to send a car. He asked where, checked the distance, and by 16:13 was at a Walmart on the road to Belton. He arrived at 16:48. Nobody sets off at four in the afternoon for an appointment he believes is at one in the morning.
- His own later text does not fit it. "Ill give extra" means extra on top of a price. The only figure in the May 1 conversation is "100."
- The day before, in the same conversation, a bare number was a price: "50."
- "2 for 1" is how a price is put. He has done this before, and a panel will know how these messages read.
- The sentence is reasoning. He had not asked for a price, so it read as a time. It is not put as a memory of how he read the line then (section 15.4).
- One part is true and can be said without the rest. At 15:53 in the afternoon, "tonight" does not mean now.

*To the follow-up, why he wanted to know where the room was. As relayed: it is habitual to ask where, and then to check the distance.*

- The records bear this out. He asked where, searched the hotel at 15:57, and wrote "Its 20 min away from me" at 15:58. He had looked up hotels in two other towns at 15:18.
- It describes what he does. The question was why, and the habit is that of a man deciding whether to go. It fits meeting an adult. It does not fit "I did not intend to meet anyone," and it sits badly with a meeting at 0100.

**7. "u gonna cum" / "on both of us lol" / "Yes"**

*As relayed: the answer in section 6, word for word. To the rebuttal that he then wrote "Just you….then both": as earlier, he had not replied to the original two-person proposition.*

- The timing is the strongest point he has on any single message. It is in the log, and it does not depend on his word. As relayed, Spec 1 rests on this message (section 19.1).
- The reply does not meet the rebuttal, which was that he never corrected "Yes." It says what "Just you….then both" was: his answer to the proposition. That concedes that by 16:00:22 he understood what was offered and answered it.
- Whom "you" meant is then the question, and it is the question of Specs 1 and 2.
- He has since said that he recalls writing "nah.....just one...maybe two ..later". I take that to be this line (section 25.5).

**8. He drove 20 minutes and asked "Cash right?"**

*As relayed: the actual duration was 36 minutes, and not directly to the location. "Cash" stems from a separate conversation he had. In a later answer: "Cash right?" was intended for someone else.* The complete log has an earlier exchange about cash with this number, on April 30 (section 22.2). He has since made a point about what the message shows if it is read as a question to her: that a man who had been told would not have needed to ask. That is an argument, and not a further account of what he meant. Section 32.3 sets out how it sits with these answers.

- "20 minutes" is his own figure, from his text at 15:58. I cannot match 36 minutes to the timeline with certainty. It may be the two legs of the drive. The timeline has him leaving the Walmart at 16:23 and on scene at 16:48, which is 25 minutes. The figure should come from the records.
- The correction helps the prosecutor. A longer trip with a stop gave him more time to turn back. The gas stop was in Killeen, as I understand the address, and Harker Heights lies on the main road from there to Belton. A map is likely to show that the stop was on the way.
- The stop brings back a question from section 9.3: what he bought, and whether he took out cash. His bank records and the arrest inventory will show it. If he drew cash between 16:13 and 16:23, "Cash right?" at 16:51 was about that cash.
- "Meant for someone else" can be read two ways.
  - *He typed it into the wrong conversation.* That is a second message sent to her by mistake within about an hour. It came less than three minutes after she sent the photo of the door, while he was parked at her hotel. It also means he was arranging payment with someone else at that moment. He will be asked who, and where they were to meet.
  - *The subject came from the other conversation.* Then it was a question to her about how he would pay, and it concedes payment.
- The log decides part of this. If she answered "Cash right?" and he carried on without saying it was meant for someone else, he did not treat it as a mistake. The other conversation decides the rest: whether payment was being discussed in it at 16:51.
- A second wrong message weakens the first. One is a familiar mistake (section 12.2). Two, each falling on a message that hurts, is a pattern a fact-finder will not accept without the other conversation in evidence.

*If he says he was testing for a scam: why drive there, and why offer extra? As relayed: it seemed harmless at the time. Offering extra was an attempt to expose a scam.*

- "Harmless" does not sit with the next answer, that he was nervous because he expected a scam, or with "paralyzed" (16.4).
- The test has a logic. A real person will come down to the car, and a scammer will make excuses. The money is what makes it worth her while.
- On that logic she passed. She wrote "ok will be down what car u in," and he answered "Black suv." So the test told him that someone real was coming. Section 6 asked what follows: "And when she agreed, you described your car." No answer has been given to that. It is the part that matters, because it goes to what he intended at 16:59.
- Section 4 said a bluff would not be believed and that fear of going into the room might be. That is still my assessment. "Can you walk me upstairs?" and the request to come to the car both fit a man who wanted to see who was there before he went in.
- In Texas the offer may be the offense whether or not he meant to keep it (section 20.3).

**9. "Im a little nervous."**

*As relayed: nervous because he expected a scam. To the rebuttal about the searches for stings and second-number apps: for the same reason.*

- Section 6 had a different answer here: nervous because buying sex is illegal, could end his career, and might be a robbery or the police. That answer explained his caution without any reference to age. It was easy to believe because it admitted what he was doing. The new answer drops it.
- People do not usually tell a suspected scammer that they are nervous. They tell the person they are about to meet.
- "For the same reason" was written for the earlier answer. With the new one it says that he searched for stings and for second-number apps because he expected a scam. Neither fits.
  - The app search was at 14:01 to 14:04. On his account the suspicion began when she named Belton, after 15:54.
  - He has given the app search a different reason, her number (section 13.5). This is the third.
  - A sting is the police, and a scam is not (section 10.3, point 5). A search for signs of a police operation shows that he thought the police might be at the other end. The government will say that a man who thinks so and goes anyway has decided to take the chance.
- Section 20 takes up his further point, that nervousness at the door does not fit a settled agreement, and gives the reported cases found.

**10. "You offered a 15-year-old extra money for sex in a car with tinted windows."**

*As relayed: not true. Not once on May 1 did he believe he was texting a supposed minor. The dialogue is drastically different when he thought he was talking to an adult as opposed to a minor.*

- This is the center of his defense, and the best answer in the set. It is a direct statement of what he believed, and only he can give it.
- The comparison is one a fact-finder can make from the exhibits. On April 30, once age was mentioned, he refused and told her to change her life. Nothing like that appears on May 1. Counsel can make the point from the log without his testimony, so it does not depend on whether he is believed. Someone has to make it (section 19.1).
- The government's answer: the May 1 conversation reads like April 30 before she gave her age. On its theory that is the point. He went back to treating her as he had before she told him.
- The answer does not take up the car or the money, and it does not need to. What he believed is the issue. If he is believed on that, the car and the money are Spec 3.

**11. He removed the app in the parking lot.**

*As relayed: he deletes that app and those threads out of shame, and had many times before, because he felt out of control. To "But you didn't drive away": he felt paralyzed.*

- "Those threads" is still in the answer. On his own answers many of the April 30 conversations were still there on May 1 (sections 14.3 and 15.2). The records support removing the app as a routine. They do not yet support deleting conversations as one.
- "Out of control" is honest, and it will matter at sentencing. On the findings it is also the government's theme: the urge that overrode Thursday's refusal (section 3).
- "Paralyzed" has to answer one question: by what? He has said it seemed harmless, that he expected a scam, and that he believed she was an adult. None of those is a reason to freeze. The government will supply one. If his reason was shame at what he was doing at all, that is his answer, and only he can give it.
- The time of the arrest may make this unnecessary. If he was arrested a minute or two after 17:02, he had no time to drive away. The arrest time is still not in anything I have seen (section 9.1). It is now known: 5:02 pm (section 22.6).
- Shame is the one reason in this answer that could fit the rule on abandonment, and "many times before" is the part that makes the removal a routine. Section 24 takes both readings, and explains why the reason for the removal should go to counsel first.

**12. "I refused and thought the next day she just needed money."**

*As relayed: the details had become convoluted in his head, and he gave an inaccurate, generalized version. What he said was to just "look at my phone." Asked where on the recording he says she was 23: at no time was that stated, only "look at my phone."*

Section 16.5 deals with this.

**13. If he thought about giving her money and leaving, why "Just you….then both," "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra"?**

*As relayed: those are different questions at different times. The first was his reply after not replying earlier. "Cash right?" was intended for someone else. "Ill give extra" was intended for a purported scammer. These details were unlikely to be instantly recalled during the interview. To the follow-up, why give money and leave at all: he understood the question as hypothetical.*

- The question was not what each text meant. It was that none of the three fits a man who meant to hand over money and leave. Three separate explanations do not answer that. They show that each text needs one.
- His answer to question 12 goes further than these three explanations. If the interview statement was inaccurate, giving money and leaving was not something he had settled on, and the three texts have nothing to contradict. Slide 9 cannot then stand beside it, because it gives the same thought as one he had at the hotel. He has since said that there was never a plan of any kind (section 19.3).

**14 and 15. His memory that evening, and what he did not tell the agents.**

*As relayed, to both: these details were unlikely to be instantly recalled during the interview. He keeps part of the answer from section 6: he is pointing to the phone records, not to his memory.*

Section 16.5 deals with these.

**16. He asked for a peace sign "so I know real," so the photos told him nothing.**

No answer was given. His answers to questions 8 and 9 make this one harder (16.4).

**17. How many times has he paid for sex?**

*As relayed: the answer in section 6, unchanged.*

- His other answers already tell the fact-finder that it was often: "habitual," "frequently," "many times before." Counsel may be able to keep his history out (section 7, point 5). Explaining his conduct by his habits brings it in. That is counsel's decision, and it should be made before he gives any of these answers.

**18. Addiction.**

*As relayed: the answer in section 6, with an addition. The one time he read that a minor was involved, he refused.*

- The addition is his best fact, used where it belongs.
- "Careless about who I was texting" fits the mistake. A prosecutor will try to turn "careless" into "did not care," which is slide 8's "indifferent" (section 11.3, on deliberate avoidance).

### 16.2 One explanation for each message

| On May 1 | How it reads | His explanation | Kind |
|---|---|---|---|
| "Are you still around?" | A return to her | Meant for another woman | Wrong recipient |
| "Ok. Where at?" after "2 for 1 tonight 100" | Going along with the offer | He was answering "we have a room"; "100" read as a time | Reading of the text |
| "Yes" | Agreeing to the act | He was answering "are you coming?" | Reading of the text, supported by the timing |
| "Just you….then both" | Choosing her, then both | His answer to the repeated proposition | Not disputed at the time. He has since recalled different words, which I take to be this line (section 25.5) |
| "Im convinced lol" | Satisfied by the photo | Sarcasm | Tone |
| "Cash right?" | Confirming how he would pay | Meant for someone else | Wrong recipient |
| "Im a little nervous" | Said to the person he was about to meet | He expected a scam | Scam |
| "I have tinted windows if you can carplay. Ill give extra" | More money for sex in the car | A test to expose a scam | Scam |
| "Black suv" | Telling her where to find him | None given | |
| Removing the apps at 17:02 | Getting rid of evidence | Habit and shame | Habit |
| Staying in the car park | Waiting for her | He felt paralyzed | State of mind |
| The interview that evening | He knew who she was | A generalized, inaccurate version; a hypothetical question | Memory |

1. **Taken one at a time, several of these are possible.** The timing on "Yes" is good. Sarcasm is plausible. Removing the app as a habit can be proved.
2. **Taken together they are the problem.** Ten of the twelve are given a meaning other than the natural one, for seven kinds of reason. A fact-finder does not weigh them one by one. He asks how likely it is that so many messages meant something other than what they say, each for a different reason. The government needs one explanation for all twelve. Section 19.2 separates the explanations that sit beside the natural meaning from those that replace it. Only the second kind carry this cost, and they are fewer.
3. **This is the list section 8.3 warned about, and it is longer.** The single account in section 10 was an improvement because it let most of these go. These answers bring them back and add two.
4. **A witness with an answer for everything is believed less than one who concedes what is plain.** The statements that have added to his credibility in this review are the ones that cost him something: that he pays for sex, that he meant to block her, that he felt out of control. He has since pointed to habit as the common explanation (section 17.3).

### 16.3 Which charge the answers defend

1. **Most of the answers deny that he agreed to pay for sex.** "Ok" answered the room. "100" was a time. "Cash right?" was for someone else. The extra was a test. He expected a scam.
2. **That is Spec 3.** It is the charge the government can prove without her age, and this analysis has called it strong from the first draft. Section 21 goes through its elements. If every one of these answers were accepted, Specs 1 and 2 would still turn on the same question: whom did he believe he was dealing with?
3. **His other answers concede the conduct.** He asks where and checks the distance as a habit. "Just you….then both" was his answer to the proposition. He has paid adults for sex. He drove there, waited and described his car.
4. **The cost falls on the charges that matter.** The only evidence that he believed she was an adult is his own word (section 8.3). A fact-finder who decides he is not being straight about "100" or "Cash right?" will not then take his word on the one point where his word is all there is.
5. **Section 5 put it the other way round,** and that is still my assessment. If it is true that he went to Belton to pay an adult for sex, that one fact explains "Ok. Where at?", "Just you….then both", "Cash right?", the nervousness and the extra. It needs no misreading and no second mistake, and it leaves his credibility for the question of age. Habit as the explanation leads the same way (section 17.3).
6. **If it is not true, he cannot say it.** Then he should expect the scam account to be tested against the drive, the wait and "Black suv." Most fact-finders will not accept it.
7. **Conceding the Spec 3 conduct has consequences of its own,** in the court-martial and in Texas. That decision is his and counsel's (section 11.5).

### 16.4 The scam and the adult belief

**The collision.** Section 11.2 recorded that from the second photo he took her to be the woman on the profile, a real adult. His answers now say that at 16:53 he was nervous because he expected a scam, and that at 16:58 he offered extra to expose one. As far as the excerpts show, both came after the second photo. The first of these was my inference, and he has since said it is misleading (section 19.2).

**Why it matters.** The profile's age tells him her age only if he believed the pictures were hers (sections 11.3 and 13.3). If at the hotel he still thought they were not, then on his own answers the profile told him nothing about who was in the room. Question 16 asks exactly this, and it is the one he left unanswered.

**What "scam" means decides it.** The word has been used for at least two different things.

| Meaning | Where it appears | Does it fit the adult belief? |
|---|---|---|
| Nobody real is there, and the pictures are not hers | Slide 8 (the photo was fake); the peace-sign request; "a test to expose a scam" | No. Pictures that are not hers say nothing about her age |
| A real woman, but a set-up: a robbery, or money taken for nothing | Slide 9 (a "friend" as a handler); "Can you walk me upstairs?"; asking her to come to the car | Yes. He could believe she was the adult in the pictures and still fear what was waiting |

1. **Only he knows which he meant, and when.** It has to be the same thing each time he uses the word. This is an observation about fit, not a suggestion about what to say. He has since said that the suspicion and the rest were true together (section 19.2).
2. **Neither meaning involves a minor.** That is the one thing all his answers agree on. If what he remembers is that he was unsure what was waiting but never thought of a child, that is one state of mind. Given as "I expected a scam" in one answer and "I believed she was an adult" in another, it is heard as two stories.
3. **His state at the hotel has been described four ways:** it seemed harmless, he was nervous, he felt out of control, and he felt paralyzed. A fact-finder can accept that a man felt several things in ten minutes. "Harmless" is the one that does not belong with the rest.

### 16.5 The interview

**"Look at my phone"**

1. **If it is on the video, it is worth having.** A man who tells the agents to look at his phone is not behaving like a man who thinks it will convict him. The record of the search and the profile could be reached from it.
2. **It has an obvious answer.** He had removed the texting apps from that phone at 17:02. The prosecutor will say that he invited them to look once the conversations were off it.
3. **It is not an account.** Asked what had happened, he sent them to the phone. As relayed, he did not say what they would find.
4. **What to look for on the video:** the exact words, the question that drew them, whether he said what the phone would show, and whether he consented to a search. If he consented, the government has an extraction, and counsel should have it (section 14.2). He has since said that he put the phone in airplane mode at an official's request after the interview (section 26.1).
5. **It was said after the arrest,** so it does little to support his later account as a statement (section 13.2, on M.R.E. 801(d)(1)(B)). Its value is as conduct: he invited scrutiny.

**What he did not say.** He now says that at no time did he tell the agents she was 23. Section 6 said of question 15 that the video controls, and that if he said neither thing it may be the hardest question in the case. On his own recollection the video will not contain it. His recollection of the interview is, on his own account, poor, so the video still has to be watched for it.

**"Hypothetical"**

1. **I read this as being about the agent's question.** He took it as asking what he would do, and giving money and leaving was his answer to that. If he means the cross-examination question, this point does not apply.
2. **The video can settle it.** Section 8.2 listed the question asked just before the statement as the first thing to look for. If the agent asked what he would have done, or what he was going to do, the statement loses much of its force as a report of what he thought.
3. **The words as relayed are not hypothetical in form.** "The next day I thought about just giving money and leaving" reports a thought, in the past tense.
4. **It is the third description of the same words.** Slide 9 gives it as a thought he had at the hotel. Section 13.2 records it as a generalization. Now it is an answer to a hypothetical. The table in section 13.6 has been updated.

**"Unlikely to be instantly recalled"**

1. **It is given three times, to three different questions.** In testimony a repeated sentence sounds prepared, and each question asked for something different. Section 19.2 records the fuller explanation he has since given.
2. **It can explain forgetting details.** That "Cash right?" was meant for someone else is a detail.
3. **The two things missing from the interview are not details.** That he had texted the wrong person, and that he believed she was an adult, are the whole of his account. A man arrested for soliciting a minor, who believed he had been dealing with an adult, does not have to recall that. It is what he would be expected to say first.
4. **It turns round.** If the afternoon was too confused in his head to describe that evening, the prosecutor will ask how he can now say what he was thinking as he sent each text.
5. **"I am pointing to the phone records, not to my memory" does not cover these answers.** Records show what he did and when. They cannot show that he read "100" as a time, whom "Cash right?" was for, or what the extra was meant to do. Those are statements about his mind, and only memory can supply them. If seeing the records brought a memory back, he can say so. If he worked it out from the records, it is an inference (sections 13.6 and 15.4).

### 16.6 The wording, and this document
