- I found no reported case that matches his facts, and none in which nervousness on arrival was accepted as showing that the accused lacked intent. In the cases I found where a court discussed hesitation in a sting, it was read against the accused: as fear of being caught, and in one case as knowledge of the persona's age. In that case the accused had said so himself.
- That says less than it seems to. Acquittals leave no written opinions. In one case on the question of what the accused believed, the first jury could not agree.
- The argument does have a place in the law. It is the point section 19.3 called resolve. A military appeals court held in 2025 that arriving at the meeting place was not enough to show a firm intent. That case turned on what the accused had with him, not on what he felt.
- For Specs 1 and 2 the nervousness is neutral. The question is still who he believed she was.

### 20.1 What is right in the point

**The room was her proposal.** His first suggestion, at 15:51:36, was to send a car to bring her to him. She declined, and wrote "we have a room" at 15:52:55, less than four minutes after his first message. Section 9.5 sets out the exchange.

**The wariness was not sudden.** The log and the timeline show it building for almost an hour.

| Time on May 1 | What the log or the timeline shows | Note |
|---|---|---|
| 15:51:36 | He offers to send a car for her | Sent before any room or hotel was mentioned |
| 15:52:55 | She answers "we have a room" | The room is her proposal |
| 15:56:39 | She names the hotel | From the log |
| 15:56:50 | "Lol ok." | Eleven seconds later. He says his suspicion came from an earlier experience at the same hotel (section 19.2) |
| 16:01:35 to 16:02:46 | He asks for pictures, then writes "Im convinced lol" | He says the second was sarcasm (section 10) |
| After 16:09:41 | He asks for a photo with a peace sign "so I know real" | From the log |
| Before he arrived | A search for signs of a sting | Slide 8. Its time and its words are not known |
| 16:48:45 | The photo of the door, with the room number, reaches him | He is on scene at 16:48 |
| 16:49 | He tries the car park of the hotel next door | From the timeline |
| 16:51:08 | "Cash right?" | He says this was meant for someone else (section 16.1) |
| 16:53:54 | "Im a little nervous" | Five minutes after the room number |
| 16:56:05 | "Can you walk me upstairs?" | From the log |
| 16:58:19 | He asks her to come to the car, and offers extra | From the log |
| 16:58:57 | She agrees and asks what car he is in. He answers "Black suv" | From the log |
| 17:02 | The texting apps are removed | The arrest time is not in anything I have seen |

**Three things follow**

1. **"Suddenly" undersells his point.** The first sign comes eleven seconds after she named the hotel. That fits what he has said about an earlier experience there. A fear that appeared only at the door would be easier for the government to explain.
2. **He tried three times to have the meeting somewhere other than the room:** the car he offered to send at the start, the request to be walked up, and his own car at the end. The first was sent before any room or hotel was mentioned, so it was not a reaction to this hotel.
3. **He had the room number from 16:48:45.** As far as the log and the timeline show, he did not go to the door in the thirteen minutes before the apps were removed. I do not know where he was when he was arrested.

**What it costs.** The same table shows a man who was wary for an hour and kept going. He drove there, stayed, and went on writing to her. His explanation is compulsion (section 19.2). The government's is that he had decided to take the risk.

### 20.2 What nervousness can and cannot show

**The argument at its best.** The government describes a man carrying out a settled agreement to pay for sex with two girls in a hotel room. The man in the log never wanted the room. He did not go to it when he had the number, he said he was nervous, and he asked twice for her to come to him. A settled arrangement does not look like that.

**Why a fact-finder is unlikely to take it as far as he does**

1. **People are nervous about what they are about to do.** Nobody is nervous about a meeting he has decided not to attend, or about a person he thinks does not exist. The message assumes that someone was there and that he might go up. Section 16.1 made a related point: people do not tell a suspected scammer that they are nervous.
2. **Nervousness has several possible causes, and the message names none.**

   | Cause | Does it fit an intent to go ahead? | Does it bear on her age? |
   |---|---|---|
   | He believed she was 15 (the government's reading) | Yes | Yes |
   | Buying sex is illegal, and the police might be there (his answer in section 6) | Yes | No |
   | A robbery or a set-up (his answer in section 16.1) | Yes, if she turned out to be real | No |
   | Second thoughts about doing it at all | No | No |
   | Nobody real was there | No | No |

   Only he knows which it was, and more than one can be true (section 19.2). The last two are the only ones that do not fit an intent to go ahead. They are also the two that his next messages make hardest to maintain.
3. **What he did next.** Two minutes later he asked her to walk him upstairs. Two minutes after that he asked her to come to the car, and offered extra. When she agreed, he described his car. Each step moved toward a meeting, on terms he felt safer with. The room was her idea. The car was his.
4. **An agreement is not what the government has to prove in the court-martial.** It has to prove an intent and an act (20.3). So showing that nothing was firmly agreed at 15:53:50 does not answer the charge. Section 19.1 already records that the finding of an agreement at that moment says more than the log does. The log is better evidence for that than the nervousness is.
5. **The arrangement did change, and that is worth saying.** What she proposed at 15:53 was two girls in a room. What he proposed at 16:58 was a meeting in his car. Whatever he intended at the end, it was not the arrangement the government says he accepted at the start.
6. **One detail leans his way.** A man afraid of being seen with a 15-year-old is unlikely to ask her to come down and walk him through a hotel. That request fits fear of what was in the room better than it fits fear of being seen with her. It is a small point, and it is for counsel.

**Which charge it reaches**

| Charge | What the government must show | What the nervousness does |
|---|---|---|
| Spec 3 | An intent to obtain a sexual act for money, and an act that strongly corroborates it | It goes to how firm the intent was. It does not remove an intent that depended on her being real (section 19.3). The message about the car is a new offer |
| Spec 2 | An intent to commit a sexual act with her, an act that strongly corroborates it, and a belief that she was under 16 | The same on firmness. Nothing on what he believed, unless the reason for the nervousness is shown |
| Spec 1 | That he sent indecent words to someone he believed was under 16 | Nothing on the words. The message about the car is one of those relied on (section 19.1) |
| Texas | A knowing offer or agreement to pay for sexual conduct | Nothing, if an offer or agreement was made (20.3) |

### 20.3 Where the argument fits in the law

These are my understanding of the rules, for counsel to confirm.

**The court-martial: how firm the intent was**

1. **The elements of an attempt.** An overt act; a specific intent to commit the offense; an act that is more than preparation; and an act that apparently tends to bring the offense about (*United States v. Payne*, 73 M.J. 19 (C.A.A.F. 2013), as quoted in *Penaloza*, below). No agreement is required.
2. **The act has to show a firm intent.** It must be "strongly corroborative of the firmness of the defendant's criminal intent" (*United States v. Byrd*, 24 M.J. 286 (C.M.A. 1987)). It must "unequivocally" show that the crime will take place unless independent circumstances interrupt it (*United States v. Winckelmann*, 70 M.J. 403 (C.A.A.F. 2011)). This is the legal form of his point. Evidence that he would not go to the room is evidence about firmness.
3. **Travel usually satisfies it.** *Winckelmann* said that courts agree that travel is a substantial step in enticement cases. *United States v. Hale*, 78 M.J. 268 (C.A.A.F. 2019), said that a substantial step can be something as ordinary as travel, arranging a meeting or booking a hotel. Service courts have treated the journey to meet the persona as enough for attempted sexual assault of a child (20.4).
4. **But not always.** In *Byrd* the accused took money to buy marijuana for an undercover agent, rode in a taxi to the place where it was sold, and bought liquor there. The court held that the ride was not strongly corroborative of a firm intent. In *Penaloza* the Army court held in 2025 that arriving at the meeting place was not a substantial step where the accused had no cash, and cash was the only payment the persona would accept (20.4).
5. **What that means here.** *Penaloza* turned on what the accused had with him. On the same reasoning the arrest inventory matters more than the message about being nervous. Cash in the amount discussed would point one way, as "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra" already do. No cash, or too little, would point the other. Section 7 already asks for the inventory, and section 16.1 asks whether he drew cash on the way. Section 21.3 applies this to Spec 3.
6. **The specification may not name the act relied on.** An earlier version of this point said that it would, and section 21.1 corrects it. If the act is the drive, the question is what he intended as he drove. What he did on arrival is evidence of that, in both directions. He can say that for thirteen minutes nothing outside him stopped him from going to the room, and he did not go. The government can say that by 16:59 she was on her way down to his car. The government may also rely on what he did at the hotel as an act in itself. Section 24.2 notes a limit on this argument, from *Rios*: intent is judged at the time of the act, and a later loss of nerve does not undo it.

**The court-martial: voluntary abandonment**

1. **Military law allows the defense even after a substantial step.** "It is a defense to an attempt offense that the person voluntarily and completely abandoned the intended crime, solely because of the person's own sense that it was wrong, prior to the completion of the crime" (Manual for Courts-Martial, pt. IV, ¶ 4.c.(4); *United States v. Rios*, 33 M.J. 436 (C.M.A. 1991)). At least one federal appeals court does not allow it once the attempt is complete (*United States v. Young*, 20.4).
2. **The reason has to be the right one.** The same paragraph says the defense "is not allowed if the abandonment results, in whole or in part, from other reasons, for example, the person feared detection or apprehension, decided to await a better opportunity for success, was unable to complete the crime, or encountered unanticipated difficulties or unexpected resistance."
3. **Fear of a scam is not that reason.** It is a difficulty, and not a sense that the thing was wrong. So the nervousness he describes cannot support this defense. *Rios* is the authority (section 24.2).
4. **It has to be complete.** In *Rios* the accused gave up one robbery and went on to try another, and the defense was not available. Here the only act that points away from the meeting is the removal of the apps at 17:02, and he stayed where he was (section 16.1). The arrest time decides how much that shows. Section 24.2 takes the removal through the rule's conditions, with the cases.
5. **It pulls against his account.** A man who says he gave something up is saying that there was something to give up. Counsel can argue in the alternative. Whether he had decided anything at 17:02 is a fact only he knows. This is a statement of what the rule requires, and not a suggestion about what to say. Section 3 called this defense weak, and that is still my assessment.

**Who proposed the room.** It matters for what "Ok" answered (section 9.5). It is not entrapment. Offering a place is an opportunity, and he had opened the conversation.

**Texas: the offer is the offense**

1. **The statute.** A person commits solicitation of prostitution if he "knowingly offers or agrees to pay a fee to another person for the purpose of engaging in sexual conduct with that person or another" (Penal Code § 43.021(a)). Nothing more has to happen. If an offer or agreement was made, what he felt afterwards does not undo it.
2. **The message about the car came after the nervousness.** On its face it is an offer.
3. **A test may not be an answer there.** Under the earlier prostitution statute, the Court of Criminal Appeals held that a person can knowingly offer or agree without intending to go through with it, and that intent to consummate is not an element (*Mattias v. State*, 731 S.W.2d 936 (Tex. Crim. App. 1987)). That case concerned the seller. The buyer's statute has the words "for the purpose of engaging in sexual conduct," and whether an offer made as a test is such an offer is a question for his Texas counsel. Until it is answered, his statement that the extra was offered to expose a scam (section 16.1) should be treated as one that could admit the Texas offense.
4. **Texas has a renunciation defense for attempts,** with a similar limit on motive (Penal Code § 15.04). The charge as described is not an attempt.

### 20.4 The reported cases

**What I looked for.** A decision in which the accused drove to the meeting place and then said he was nervous, on arriving or on being given a room number, and in which the court said what that showed.

**What I found.** None that matches. Section 23.3 has since added a court-martial in which the accused stayed in his car near the meeting place, and section 23.5 says what it shows. The cases below are the nearest. In the sting cases among them the accused was arrested as he arrived, or after he had met the agent. None has what the log shows here: a period of messages sent from the car park, without an approach to the room.

**Cases in which hesitation or nervousness was discussed**

| Case | Facts | How the court read it | How this case differs |
|---|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Puff*, No. 23-4457 (4th Cir. 17 Mar. 2025) | He answered an ad on a prostitution site, using a number from a second-number app. He was told by text that the girl was 13. He asked more than once whether she was the police. He watched the hotel before the meeting. He paid an agent $60. He deleted the app and at first lied about it. He told agents that he knew she was 13, that he was nervous because of her age, and that he had not been nervous in that way with adults. Section 24.6 adds what the full opinion shows: he had refused from one number and gone back on the other, and he testified that he did not know it was the same conversation | Convictions affirmed. The questions about the police, the watching, the deletion and his own account of his nervousness were evidence that he knew her age | He said it himself. He was told the age twice, the second time about 20 minutes before he went back. He paid |
| *United States v. Fulsom*, No. 202500166 (N-M. Ct. Crim. App. 29 May 2026) | The persona was listed as 18 and said by text that she was "almos 15." He refused at first and came back within minutes. He spoke of the risk to his career. He asked for a photo "at the moment" and for video calls. He traveled 45 minutes with condoms | Convictions affirmed. His hesitation "stemmed from fear of apprehension, not the lack of predisposition." The requests for proof were counted as showing that he went on knowingly. The journey was a "clear" substantial step | His refusal ended the conversation. Nothing was said about age the next day and, as far as the excerpts show, he was offered no way out then (section 11.3) |
| *United States v. Petersen*, No. 25-13589 (11th Cir. 13 Nov. 2026) | He answered an ad for a 19-year-old and was told she was 14. He asked to meet the next day instead. The agent wrote "u playing." He drove to a gas station and was arrested | Conviction affirmed. His messages showed that he "only wished to avoid detection by police" | The issue was an entrapment instruction. He had been told the age and went on |

**Cases on arriving at the place**

| Case | Facts | Result |
|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Penaloza*, ARMY 20230473 (Army Ct. Crim. App. 28 Feb. 2025) | He arranged to meet a persona he believed was under 18, and to pay cash. He was arrested on arriving. No cash was found on him or in the car | The court held the evidence insufficient. Travel "must be accompanied by evidence of a firm intent to complete the offense," and there was "a fair and rational hypothesis" that he did not intend to pay. I did not see the final order, or check for any later appeal |
| *United States v. Byrd*, 24 M.J. 286 (C.M.A. 1987) | Set out in 20.3 | Riding to the place of sale was not strongly corroborative of a firm intent |
| *State v. Duke*, 709 So. 2d 580 (Fla. Dist. Ct. App. 1998), and *State v. Kemp*, 753 N.E.2d 47 (Ind. Ct. App. 2001) | Each accused arranged sex online with a supposed child and drove to the car park agreed. One flashed his lights as arranged. The other brought condoms | Not enough for an attempt at the sexual offense itself. These are older state decisions and the minority view. They bind no court that will hear this case, and I did not check whether they are still followed |
| *United States v. Ozbirn*, No. ACM 39556 (A.F. Ct. Crim. App.), affirmed on another point, 81 M.J. 38 (C.A.A.F. 2021) | He arranged sex with a supposed 12-year-old and drove to meet her | The journey was a substantial step toward sexual assault of a child |
| *United States v. Rutgerson*, 822 F.3d 1223 (11th Cir. 2016) | He answered an ad and was told she was 15. He tried first to have her come to him. He then drove to her hotel and was arrested | Conviction affirmed. The court noted that he did not hesitate after learning her age |
| *United States v. Young*, 613 F.3d 735 (8th Cir. 2010) | He drove to a motel to meet a supposed minor | Travel was a substantial step. "A defendant cannot abandon an attempt once it has been completed" |
| *State v. Dickerson*, No. 20230751-CA (Utah Ct. App. 2025) | He drove to the place agreed. He argued that many steps remained before any contact | The argument was rejected and the convictions affirmed |

**Two cases close to his in other ways**

| Case | Facts | Result |
|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Medina*, No. 24-1609 (1st Cir. 9 Sept. 2025) | He answered an ad for commercial sex. The agent's texts described girls of 14 and 12. He came to the hotel with $200 and condoms and was given a room key. At booking he was surprised by the charge and denied speaking to any minor. His defense was that his English was limited and that he believed he was arranging to meet an adult | The first jury could not agree. The second convicted. Affirmed |
| *United States v. Munn*, ARMY 20250252 (Army Ct. Crim. App. 2026) | A website sting. He agreed a fee with an officer posing as a sex worker and was arrested on arriving at the hotel. He was charged with attempted patronizing and attempted sexual assault of a child | By agreement with the special trial counsel the case went to a special court-martial. He pleaded guilty to attempted patronizing, and the child specification was dismissed |

**What the cases show**

1. **Courts ask what the accused was afraid of.** In *Fulsom* and *Petersen* the answer was the police. In *Puff* it was her age, in his own words. In each the accused had just been told the age, so a fear of being caught was evidence that he knew what he could be caught for.
2. **His record differs on both points.** Nothing was said about age on May 1. When it was said on April 30 he did not hesitate. He refused. And most of his checks were on whether she was real and whether the room was safe: the pictures, the sign, the request to be walked up. On the deck's own account a check for the police is among them too (slide 8).
3. **The government has a pattern to follow, and *Puff* is that pattern.** A second-number app, a check for police, a look at the hotel, nervousness, an app removed. Each has a counterpart in this case (sections 2 and 9.3). Section 24.6 adds what the full opinion shows.
4. **The answer to *Puff* is in his records.** That accused said he had not been nervous with adults. If his earlier conversations with adults show the same caution, with the pictures, the request to come down, the remark about being nervous and the app removed afterwards, then the caution was his routine and says nothing about her age. Section 17.3 called this the test. The earlier occasion at the same hotel would do the same for the reason he gives (section 19.5). Both are records of other occasions, with the cost noted in section 9.4.
5. **What he said that night matters more than what is said now.** *Puff* was decided on the accused's own statements, and in *Medina* the jury heard what he said at booking. If the video has him giving a reason for being nervous, that is the reason a fact-finder will use.
6. **Requests for proof have been read as going ahead with open eyes.** That is how *Fulsom* treated the photo "at the moment." The request for a photo with a sign will be put the same way. His answer is what he says he compared it with (section 10.4).
7. **Asking the persona to come out to the car has not been treated as drawing back.** *Rutgerson* began with the accused trying to have her come to him.
8. **Reported decisions are a one-sided sample.** An acquittal cannot be appealed and leaves no opinion, and most cases end in pleas. So the absence of a favorable case does not show that the argument fails at trial. *Medina*'s first jury is the nearest evidence. On the question of what he believed, it could not agree.
9. ***Munn* shows one way such a case has ended.** The same pair of charges, from the same kind of operation, ended in a plea to attempted patronizing. The opinion does not say what age the persona gave, or why the government agreed. As I understand it, an attempted sexual assault of a child can be tried only by a general court-martial (Article 18(c)), and a conviction carries a dishonorable discharge unless a plea agreement provides otherwise (Articles 56(b) and 53a). If that is right, the outcome the hearing officer recommended, a bad-conduct discharge with no confinement, is not open to the court on a conviction of Spec 2 after a contested trial. Counsel should confirm this. It belongs in the conversation section 8.4 describes.

### 20.5 Records to add, through counsel

These add to section 19.5.

1. **The arrest record:** the time, where he was, whether the car had moved, and the inventory of what he had with him, including any cash.
2. **The operation's own record of the minutes from 16:48:** what the team saw him do, and when and why it moved.
3. **The log pages between about 16:09 and 16:48,** and her replies between 16:51 and 16:59. These are now in section 22.2.
4. **His earlier conversations with adults,** for the same cautions (section 17.3).
5. **The earlier occasion at the same hotel** (section 19.5).
6. **The search for signs of a sting:** its time and its words (section 9.4).
7. **Bank records for that afternoon** (section 16.1).
8. **The video:** whether he gave a reason for being nervous, and what he said about the car.
9. **The charge sheet:** the act each specification relies on, if it names one (section 21.1).

### 20.6 What this changes

1. **The facts in the point are accepted.** The room was her proposal. He was wary from the moment the hotel was named, and nothing I have seen shows him going to the room. Sections 1 and 4 now say so.
2. **The inference is not accepted as he puts it.** Nervousness does not show that there was no intent. It shows that he was afraid of the room, and the message about the car shows what he did about it.
3. **The argument's place is firmness of intent,** and there is military authority for it. *Penaloza* makes the arrest inventory more important than it was.
4. **No reported case matches his facts.** Those that discuss hesitation went against the accused, for reasons that do not all apply to him. Reported cases are a one-sided sample.
5. ***Puff* is the pattern the government is likely to follow.** The answer is his routine with adults, from records. Section 31 takes this further.
6. **The Texas position is unchanged, with one caution.** The answer that the extra was a test needs Texas counsel's advice before it is given anywhere.
7. **Voluntary abandonment is still weak.** The rule is now set out.
8. ***Munn* is a reference for how a case with these charges has ended.**
9. **The assessment of the charges is unchanged.** Spec 3 is strong. Specs 1 and 2 turn on whether he knew whose number it was. The order of work is the same: video first, forensic report second, account third.

## 21. Addendum 14, 24 Nov 2026: Spec 3, element by element

**What this covers.** Two questions, supplied after section 20. This analysis has said repeatedly that Spec 3 is the strongest charge for the prosecution: which of its elements are strongest, and which are weakest? And what does the prosecution have to prove at a general court-martial?

I have not seen the charge sheet. The hearing officer's report said that this specification was to be amended, and I do not know how. What follows works from the offense as named, an attempt to patronize a prostitute. The elements come from the Manual for Courts-Martial as quoted in appellate opinions and in secondary sources, and from the standard instructions as reproduced online. Each statement of law is my understanding, for counsel to confirm.

**Short version**

- For any attempt the government has to prove four things: an overt act, a specific intent to commit the offense, that the act went beyond preparation, and that it tended to bring the offense about.
- The intent has to cover every element of the offense attempted. For patronizing, those are a sexual act with someone not his spouse, obtained in exchange for money, wrongfully, in circumstances that discredit the service or prejudice good order and discipline.
- The government does not have to prove her age, what he believed about it, that she existed, that anything was agreed, or that money changed hands.
- **Strongest:** the act and the money. The drive, the arrival and the messages are in the government's exhibits and are not disputed. "Ill give extra" is his own offer.
- **Weakest:** the kind of act he intended, because the law defines "sexual act" narrowly and on May 1 he named none; how firm the intent was, since he suspected a scam and did not go to the room; the terminal element, which needs evidence; and what he had with him, which I do not know. The wording of the specification is a fifth unknown.
- "Strong" still stands. A fact-finder is likely to fill the first two gaps by inference. His own account fills them as well: a routine of paying adults from ads is the intent this charge requires.
- So the weak points are for counsel to press, from the charge sheet, the arrest inventory and the government's own witnesses. His testimony cannot help with them, and would probably close them.

### 21.1 What the government has to prove

**Spec 3 has two layers.** It is charged as an attempt under Article 80. The offense attempted is patronizing a prostitute, which is one of the offenses listed under Article 134.

*The attempt* (Manual for Courts-Martial, pt. IV, ¶ 4.b; *United States v. Payne*, 73 M.J. 19 (C.A.A.F. 2013)):

1. He did a certain overt act.
2. He did it with the specific intent to commit the offense.
3. The act amounted to more than mere preparation.
4. The act apparently tended to bring the offense about.

*The offense attempted* (pt. IV, ¶ 106, as quoted in *Baldwin* (21.6) and in secondary sources):

1. He engaged in a sexual act with another person who was not his spouse.
2. He compelled, induced, enticed or procured that person to engage in it in exchange for money or other compensation.
3. The act was wrongful.
4. Under the circumstances his conduct was prejudicial to good order and discipline, or of a nature to bring discredit on the armed forces, or both. This is called the terminal element.

In an attempt the second list is not a list of things that happened. It is what he must have intended.

**How the two fit together**

1. **The standard instruction.** Members are told that the offense need not have been completed, but that "it must be proved beyond a reasonable doubt that, at the time of the act(s), the accused intended every element" of the offense attempted. The judge may describe that offense in summary and need not list each element.
2. **"Sexual act" has a defined meaning.** The paragraph on prostitution adopts the definition in Article 120(g)(1): penetration of the vulva, anus or mouth by the penis, however slight; contact between the mouth and the penis, vulva, scrotum or anus; or penetration of the vulva, penis or anus by any part of the body or any object, with an intent to arouse or gratify sexual desire, or to abuse, humiliate, harass or degrade. Other sexual touching is not a "sexual act."
3. **"More than preparation" means a substantial step.** The instruction describes it as a step that is "strongly corroborative of the accused's criminal intent" and shows his "resolve to commit the offense." Section 20.3 gives the cases.
4. **The specification may be short.** The Manual's model says only that the accused attempted the offense, described in enough detail to include every element expressly or by necessary implication. It need not set out each element of the offense attempted (*United States v. Norwood*, 71 M.J. 204 (C.A.A.F. 2012)). It need not name the overt act either: the members are told of the act or acts "alleged or raised by the evidence." So the government is not tied to one act unless it has pleaded one, or is made to say which it relies on by a bill of particulars (R.C.M. 906(b)(6)). Section 20.3 said that the specification would name the act. That was wrong, and it has been corrected.
5. **The standard and the vote.** Each element has to be proved beyond a reasonable doubt. A general court-martial has a military judge and eight members, and a finding of guilty needs three-fourths of the members, so six of eight (Articles 16 and 52). He may ask to be tried by the judge alone. In either case the judge decides any sentence, for offenses committed after 27 December 2023.

**What the government does not have to prove on Spec 3**

- Her age, or what he believed about it.
- That a real person, or a prostitute, was at the other end. Where the conduct would have been the offense if the facts had been as he believed, impossibility is not a defense (¶ 4.c.(3)).
- That anything was agreed, or that a price was fixed (section 20.2).
- That money changed hands, or that any sexual act took place.
- A plan (section 19.3).
- That anyone outside learned of it, if discredit to the service is what is alleged (21.3).

### 21.2 The elements, from strongest to weakest

| Element | What the government has | What stands against it | Assessment |
|---|---|---|---|
| An overt act | His messages in the log. The drive and the arrival, from location records and whatever the arrest team saw | Nothing | Strongest. Not disputed |
| The other person was not his spouse | The persona was a stranger | Nothing | Not disputed |
| Wrongfulness | No justification or excuse has been suggested | Nothing | Not disputed |
| The act tended to bring the offense about | He was at the hotel, in contact with her, and had described his car | That nobody real was there is not a defense | Strong |
| In exchange for money | "Ill give extra," which is his own offer. "Cash right?" Her price, followed by "Ok. Where at?" The $50 on April 30. What he said in the interview about earlier occasions, if it is admitted. Any cash found on him | He stated no price on May 1, and the proposal was hers. "Ok" may have answered the room (section 9.5). His answers on "100," "Cash right?" and the extra (section 16.1). What he had with him is not known | Strong on his own words. The arrest inventory could add to it or take from it |
| More than preparation | The drive, the arrival, about fourteen minutes on scene, the request that she come to the car, "Black suv" | He did not go to the room (section 20.1). *Penaloza* and *Byrd* (section 20.3) | Strong. Travel to the place usually satisfies it |
| The terminal element | A noncommissioned officer, a hotel in the civilian community, and an operation that the DPS report describes as joint with the Army | It has to be proved like any other element. If only prejudice to good order is alleged, the government needs evidence of an effect | Usually found where discredit to the service is alleged. Weaker for the government if only prejudice to good order is alleged |
| A specific intent to engage in a sexual act, as the law defines it, held when he did the act | The purpose of the contact. His request on April 30, in the same conversation. "Just you….then both." The message about the car. The drive itself | He named no act on May 1, and the explicit words that day were hers. He suspected a scam and did not go to the room. As relayed, he told the agents he had thought about "just giving money and leaving" | The least proved on the May 1 messages. A fact-finder is still likely to infer it |

### 21.3 The weak points

**1. The kind of act he intended**

1. **The element is an intent to engage in a "sexual act" as defined.** A sexual purpose in general is not enough.
2. **On May 1 he named no act.** His own words that day are "Yes," "Just you….then both" and the message about the car (section 2). None describes an act.
3. **Her line does not describe one within the definition either,** even on the government's reading of "Yes" (section 19.1).
4. **What fills the gap is April 30.** Before any age was given, as the complete log confirms (section 22.2), he asked for "An amazing bj" and $50 was agreed. Oral sex is within the definition. That exchange is in the same conversation, and it will be in evidence in any case, because it holds the age disclosure and the refusal.
5. **It fills the gap on either account of May 1.** If he knew whose number it was, it shows what he went back for. If he did not, it shows what he asks numbers from ads for, and the government can offer it as evidence of intent (M.R.E. 404(b), for counsel).
6. **The rest is inference from the setting:** a paid meeting in a hotel room, "2 for 1," and her line about what he would do.
7. **If the specification names a particular act, the proof has to match it.** Otherwise the fact-finder has to find the specification proved with substitutions, which is allowed only where that does not change the nature of the offense (R.C.M. 918(a)(1)). In *Munn* the fee had been negotiated for a named act (section 20.4).
8. **Assessment.** A gap on paper, and a small one in the room. Its use is to hold the government to the definition: in the instructions, in cross-examination of the agent about what was and was not discussed on May 1, and in a motion at the close of the government's case (R.C.M. 917), where the standard favors the government (section 19.1).

**2. How firm the intent was, and when**

1. **The intent has to exist when the act is done.** The government may rely on more than one act (21.1). For the drive, the question is what he intended between about 15:58 and 16:48. For the messages from the car park, it is what he intended then.
2. **For him.** He was wary from 15:56:50 (section 20.1). He did not go to the room. He had no plan (section 19.3). And, as relayed, he told the agents that he had thought about "just giving money and leaving."
3. **Against him.** An intent that depends on a condition is probably still an intent (section 19.3). If he meant to go ahead should she prove real, that is enough. At 16:58 he proposed the car and offered more, and when she agreed he described his vehicle.
4. **The interview statement has a use here, and a cost.** A thought of giving money and leaving is not an intent to obtain a sexual act. If the government plays the first half of that sentence for Specs 1 and 2, the defense can have the rest played (M.R.E. 304(h)(2), section 8.2). The government will then have put before the fact-finder a statement that denies the intent Spec 3 requires. But he has since said that the statement was a general version and not what he had thought (sections 13.2 and 16.5). It cannot be inaccurate for Specs 1 and 2 and accurate for Spec 3. This is an observation about fit, not a suggestion about what to say.
5. **The test.** He has said that the extra was offered to expose a scam (section 16.1). In the court-martial, unlike Texas (section 20.3), an offer made with no intention of going through with it would not be the intent this charge requires. The difficulty is the one section 16.1 recorded: she agreed, and he described his car.

**3. The money, and what he had with him**

1. **His own words carry this element.** "Extra" assumes a price already understood (section 9.5).
2. **The price and the proposal were hers.** The element asks whether he "induced, enticed or procured" the act in exchange for money. A federal appeals court has held, under a different statute, that offering money to someone who is already willing counts as inducing her (*Rutgerson*, section 20.4). Whether a court-martial would read these words the same way is for counsel. The message about the car is his own offer in any case.
3. ***Penaloza* is about this element.** The Army court held that arriving without the means to pay "does not prove an intent to pay" (section 20.4). So the arrest inventory matters more for Spec 3 than for any other charge. Cash in about the amount discussed supports the element. None, or very little, would give counsel that argument. The vehicle inventory in the packet lists no cash, but it is incomplete, and the jail's record is the one to get (section 22.5).
4. **Bank records for the afternoon bear on the same point** (section 16.1).

**4. The terminal element**

1. **It is an element, and it has to be proved beyond a reasonable doubt.** It cannot be conclusively presumed from the conduct (*United States v. Phillips*, 70 M.J. 161 (C.A.A.F. 2011)).
2. **Discredit to the service is the easier branch for the government.** The fact-finder may infer it from the conduct and the circumstances, and the public need not have known of it (*Phillips*; reaffirmed in *United States v. Wells*, No. 23-0219 (C.A.A.F. 2024)).
3. **Prejudice to good order and discipline needs evidence of an effect.** Where the government offered none, the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces held the evidence insufficient (*United States v. Richard*, 82 M.J. 473 (C.A.A.F. 2022)).
4. **How it works in an attempt is a question for counsel.** The instruction says that he must have "intended every element." For attempts to violate a regulation, the Manual says that the accused need not have intended to violate the regulation, only to do the prohibited thing (¶ 4.c.(7)). A court may treat the terminal element in the same way. In *Baldwin* the specifications alleged that the conduct was service discrediting (21.6).
5. **Here.** The government can point to a noncommissioned officer, a hotel in the civilian community, and a police operation that the DPS report describes as joint with the Army. A fact-finder will usually find discredit on facts like those. Counsel should see which branch the specification alleges, and what evidence the government has for it.

**5. The specification**

1. **I have not seen it.** The hearing officer's report said that it was to be amended.
2. **What to read it for.** The date or dates. Whether it names a sexual act, an amount or an overt act. Which branch of the terminal element it alleges. What was changed, and when. Before referral a specification can be changed. After it, a major change cannot be made over his objection (R.C.M. 603, section 19.1).
3. **A short specification is not defective for being short** (21.1). A bill of particulars is the way to make the government commit.
4. **If the dates include April 30.** That day's exchange had no travel and no meeting arranged. The Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces has held that a chat with neither was not a substantial step (*Winckelmann*, section 20.3). He also ended it himself, with a refusal that reads as a moral one, which is the reason the abandonment rule requires (section 20.3). So April 30 is unlikely to support this charge on its own. Its use to the government is as evidence of what he intended on May 1.

### 21.4 Why it has been called strong

1. **It does not depend on her age.** Everything in sections 8 to 20 about what he believed is beside the point for this charge.
2. **Most of its proof is his own words and movements,** in the government's own exhibits.
3. **His own account supplies the intent.** He has said that he texts numbers from adult ads, that he has paid adults for sex for years, and that an urge took him to the hotel (sections 17.3 and 19.2). The exhibits the defense has built for Specs 1 and 2 show the same purpose: the other numbers messaged that day, the hotel searches and the stock lines. The better the routine is proved, the better this element is proved. That is true of a routine that ends in a paid meeting. Section 31.2 considers one that ends in the car park.
4. **The defenses are weak.**
   - Impossibility is not one (21.1).
   - Voluntary abandonment requires a reason he does not give (section 20.3).
   - Entrapment requires that the suggestion came from the government and that he had no predisposition (R.C.M. 916(g)). He opened the May 1 conversation. Raising the defense would also invite the government to prove predisposition with the earlier occasions.
5. **What "strong" meant.** It was a comparison with Specs 1 and 2, and it was made without the charge sheet, the inventory or the video. It meant that the charge is likely to be proved, and that nothing in his account about her age answers it. It did not mean that every element is proved on the papers. Section 21.3 is the more exact statement.

### 21.5 How it can be contested, and by whom

1. **The weak points are legal and documentary.** Counsel can press each one without him:
   - the wording of the specification, and a bill of particulars;
   - the arrest inventory;
   - cross-examination of the agents on what was discussed on May 1, and on the terminal element;
   - the instructions on the definition of a sexual act and on a substantial step;
   - a motion at the close of the government's case;
   - on appeal, review of whether the evidence was sufficient. The Army court may also weigh the evidence itself, and set aside a finding that it is clearly convinced was against the weight of the evidence (the statute as quoted in *Penaloza*).
2. **His testimony cannot help with them.** If he testifies and says what section 5 describes, if it is true, that he was trying to pay an adult for sex, he supplies the intent himself and the gaps in 21.3 close. If he denies the purpose, he contradicts his own routine, and he spends credibility that he needs for Specs 1 and 2 (section 16.3).
3. **He probably cannot testify about Specs 1 and 2 alone.** As I understand the rule, an accused who testifies about some offenses may not be cross-examined on his guilt of the others, unless the questions are relevant to an offense he has testified about (M.R.E. 301(c)). All three specifications rest on the same messages. Questions about the money and the meeting are relevant to what he intended and believed, so they would probably be allowed.
4. **So there are three courses.**

   | Course | What it gives up | What it costs or gains |
   |---|---|---|
   | He concedes the conduct, as section 5 suggested if it is true | The points in 21.3 | A finding of guilty on Spec 3 becomes close to certain. It gains credibility on Specs 1 and 2 |
   | He contests it with his own explanations of the messages | Nothing | It costs credibility on Specs 1 and 2, where his word is all there is (section 16.3) |
   | Counsel contests it on the elements, and he offers no explanation of the messages | Nothing | Little. If he testifies he will still be asked about the money and the meeting, and truthful answers may concede it |

5. **A plea of guilty to Spec 3 alone, with Specs 1 and 2 contested, is a form of the first course.** It would require him to admit each element under oath (section 11.5). How those admissions could be used on the contested specifications, and in Texas, is for counsel.
6. **Which course is right depends on things I have not seen:** the specification, the inventory and the video. The decision is his and counsel's.

### 21.6 The other specifications, and what Spec 3 alone would carry

**The three specifications side by side.** The four elements of an attempt are the same for each. What differs is the offense attempted.

| | Spec 1 | Spec 2 | Spec 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Offense attempted | Sexual abuse of a child by indecent language (Article 120b(c)) | Sexual assault of a child (Article 120b(b)) | Patronizing a prostitute (Article 134) |
| What he must have intended | To communicate indecent language to a child under 16, in order to arouse or gratify sexual desire, or to abuse, humiliate or degrade | A sexual act upon a child who had reached 12 but not 16 | A sexual act with a person not his spouse, obtained for money |
| A belief that she was under 16 | Required | Required | Not required |
| What it turns on | Whether he knew whose number it was. Which words are alleged, and the timing of "Yes" (section 19.1) | Whether he knew whose number it was | The inference about the act, the inventory, and the wording |

The elements of the first two are as the service courts state them in *Fulsom* and *Ozbirn* (section 20.4). Section 11.3 sets out why a belief about her age is required when no real child was involved.

1. **"Sexual act" has the same definition in Spec 2.** So the first weak point in 21.3 applies there as well. The government has to show that he intended an act within the definition, with someone he believed was under 16, and on May 1 he named none. The point about the interview statement applies there too.
2. **Spec 4** is the one the hearing officer found unsupported (section 1).
3. **Spec 2 and Spec 3 rest on the same intended meeting.** If he were found guilty of both, whether they should be treated as one for sentencing is a motion for counsel to consider.

**What Spec 3 alone would carry**

1. **The maximum is that of the offense attempted** (¶ 4.d). For patronizing, as secondary sources give it: a dishonorable discharge, forfeiture of all pay and allowances, and confinement for one year. No discharge is mandatory for this offense. The mandatory discharge applies to offenses like Spec 2 (section 20.4).
2. **The judge would decide the sentence.** As one secondary source gives it, patronizing is in the lowest sentencing category, with a confinement range of 0 to 12 months.
3. **Two reference points.**
   - *United States v. Baldwin*, ARMY 20250141 (Army Ct. Crim. App. 2026). An officer used sites associated with sex workers to arrange meetings with women advertising sexual services. A panel at a general court-martial found him guilty, against his pleas, of three attempts to patronize a prostitute and one attempt at extramarital sexual conduct. It also found him guilty of conduct unbecoming an officer, which the appeals court held duplicated the attempts. The sentence was thirty days of confinement and a reprimand. The court also held that attempted patronizing and attempted extramarital conduct each require proof of something the other does not.
   - *United States v. Munn* (section 20.4). A plea to attempted patronizing at a special court-martial. I did not see the sentence.
4. **These are single cases and predict nothing.** They say nothing about administrative consequences or about Texas. For counsel.

### 21.7 Records to add, through counsel

These add to sections 19.5 and 20.5.

1. **The charge sheet as it now stands.** For Spec 3: the dates, any sexual act, amount or overt act it names, the branch of the terminal element, and what was amended and when.
2. **The arrest inventory,** and bank records for the afternoon (sections 16.1 and 20.5).
3. **Her replies after "Cash right?"** (section 20.5).
4. **The government's evidence on the terminal element:** which witness, and what that witness will say.
5. **Any notice of other acts the government means to offer** (M.R.E. 404(b), section 7).
6. **The video:** what he said about earlier occasions, about money, and about what he meant to do at the hotel.

### 21.8 What this changes

1. **The assessment stands, and now has its reasons.** Spec 3 is the strongest charge because it does not need her age, and because most of it is proved in his own words.
2. **Its weak points are named:** the kind of act, the firmness of the intent, what he had with him, the terminal element, and the wording. None is a defense. Each is something the government has to prove and may not have covered.
3. **They are counsel's to press.** His testimony would probably close them.
4. **The arrest inventory and the charge sheet matter more than they did.**
5. **One correction.** Section 20.3 said that the specification would name the act relied on. It may not.
6. **The definition of a sexual act applies to Spec 2 as well.**
7. **The suggestion in section 5 that the Spec 3 conduct be conceded now has its price stated** (21.5).
8. **The assessment of Specs 1 and 2 is unchanged,** and so is the order of work: video first, forensic report second, account third.

## 22. Addendum 15, 24 Nov 2026: the evidence packet, with the police report, the inventory and the CID report

**What this covers.** The 26-page evidence packet supplied after section 21 (pages numbered 000001 to 000026). It is the first of the government's evidence that I have read directly. Until now the log and the reports were known to me only from the deck's excerpts and the hearing officer's summary. I read every page and looked at every photograph. People are referred to by their role and not by name. Statements of law are my understanding, for counsel to confirm. The search for comparable cases that was asked for with it is in section 23.

**Short version**

- **The log is complete,** and it is set out in full in 22.2. It confirms the times this analysis has used and fills the gaps: all of April 30, the second photo, "Awesome," "Yes" to "u getting close," her answers at the hotel, and his last message at 17:01:20.
- **April 30 is stronger for him than the summaries showed.** The age came last. He had agreed a price, settled how to pay, said "Im ready now" and asked which hotel. He was then told the age, and he refused in 37 seconds.
- **It also gives the government one new point.** On April 30 he asked "Prefer cash or cashapp?" and was told "cash." On May 1 he wrote "Cash right?" The government can say that the second looks back to the first.
- **The arrest was at 5:02 pm,** by the jail card. His timeline has the apps removed at 17:02. He had no time to drive away after his last message. Which of the two came first is for the video and the phone's own record.
- **The two reports say more than the log does.** The Army report says that he "believed" she was 15, that he "agreed to pay $100.00" for "two minor females," and that he "made statements concerning ejaculating on both minor females." The log supports none of these in those terms. No age was ever given for the second person, and the words about ejaculating were the agent's.
- **The Army report also says that he "declined to proceed ... due to their age."** The government's own investigators accept the refusal and the reason for it.
- **The DPS narrative leaves out two messages on May 1,** and with them the fact that his first proposal that day was a car for one person. It does not mention the sexual lines at all.
- **The "admission" is one sentence** by an agent who "was not present" and "was informed." It quotes nothing, and it does not say which day it refers to.
- **The inventory does not settle what he had with him.** It lists no cash, but it is incomplete on its face: it does not list the phone, which was certainly taken. By the coordinates printed on it, the car was inventoried about 1.3 km from the hotel.
- **The interview paperwork is a Texas waiver and a polygraph consent,** neither with a time on it. There is no Army rights form, and the Army report does not mention the interview that the DPS report says Army CID conducted.
- **Three documents name three different Texas offenses.** The one in the DPS report is a statute for people who run websites.
- **What Specs 1 and 2 turn on is unchanged.** The packet weakens the reports as evidence. It does not weaken the log.

### 22.1 What the packet contains

| Pages | Document | Made by, and when | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 to 3 | Army CID report of investigation, marked "Initial," with one page of narrative | A CID special agent. The report is dated 4 May 2026. The narrative page is dated 30 April | Lists one offense, under Texas law. Names DPS as the lead agency. Names the site the ad was on |
| 4 to 9 | The conversation log | Printed from the undercover messaging system | Both days in one conversation, with times to the second and a delivery status for the agent's messages |
| 10 to 12 | Bell County Jail arrest card, transfer checklist and felony screening form | The DPS case agent, by hand | The arrest time and the wording of the charge |
| 13 to 19 | DPS supplemental report | The DPS case agent. Dated 19 May 2026. Every page is marked DRAFT | A narrative in 22 paragraphs, a list of attachments, and entries for the evidence |
| 20 | DPS property inventory for the vehicle | A DPS trooper, 1 May 2026 at 17:22 | |
| 21 to 22 | DPS waiver of rights and polygraph consent | The DPS polygraph examiner, 1 May 2026, no time | Both signed |
| 23 to 26 | Four photographs | Not stated | Him at the arrest; the vehicle from behind and from the side; the vehicle seen from above |

**Not in the packet:** the advertisement; the interview video, or any report of the interview; any Army rights advisement; the polygraph charts or report; a search warrant for the phone, or anything taken from the phone; the recording of the call at 17:03:24; body-camera and in-car video; any statement by the officers who made the arrest; the operation plan; the jail's record of his property and money; the charge sheet.

**The file itself.** Its own data gives it a creation date of 11 June 2026 and a last change on 30 June 2026. The charges were preferred on 24 June. So as late as June the packet held only a draft of the DPS report, and nothing from the phone.

**The highlights.** Ten passages are highlighted. The file records that the marks were added on 30 June 2026, under a user label of the kind a military account carries. It is not his number, and I do not know whose it is. The marked passages are:

- "An amazing bj";
- "Are you still around?" and its time;
- the agent's "ok u gonna cum," "on both of us lol" and "yummy";
- "Just you….then both";
- in the DPS report, the sentences about the Army interview, the polygraph, and the admission.

"Yes" is not marked. If the marks are the government's, they show what it relies on: the act he named on April 30, the return, the sexual lines, and the interview. If they are his counsel's, they show where counsel is looking. Either way they fall on the passages that this section finds the reports have overstated (22.3 and 22.7).

Not in the excerpts supplied earlier: his opening message on April 30 and most of what followed it before the age was given; what the two photos show; the times of the peace-sign request and of the second photo; "Awesome"; everything between 16:40:46 and 16:50:47; and the agent's replies at the hotel, apart from "ok will be down what car u in."

**What the complete log shows**

1. **It confirms the messages and times used so far.** Four small differences: the log reads "Ok. Where st?", "tinted wndows" and "Just you....then both," and "Cash right?" is at 16:51:09. The meaning is the same, and the earlier sections have been left as they were.
2. **It is one conversation.** The header names two numbers. The two days run on with nothing between them but the date: "k i get it" and "Are you still around?" are next to each other on one page. That is how a panel will see it. What his own screen showed is a separate question (sections 12.2 and 15.2).
3. **Age was stated once.** "i will be 16 in june is that ok," on April 30 at 16:40:09, marked delivered and answered 37 seconds later. The number 15 was never written. Nothing about age was said on May 1, and no age was ever given for the second person. The agent had the refusal in front of him when the conversation resumed, and did not repeat the age.
4. **On April 30 the age came last.** In under seven minutes he had said what he wanted, accepted "50," asked "Prefer cash or cashapp?", asked what time, written "Im ready now" and asked which hotel. The agent answered that question with the age, and he refused.
   - For the defense: he gave up an arrangement that was already complete, at once, and told her to find a different way of life. That is strong evidence of how he acts when he believes he is dealing with a minor. One military appeals court has called a refusal of this kind the reaction of a man who is not predisposed, and then held that coming back undid it (section 23.5).
   - For the government: he read the age and understood it. And the only sexual act he ever named in this conversation is the one he asked for on April 30.
   - That act was named before any age was mentioned, and withdrawn when it was. On May 1 he named none (section 21.3). This bears on Spec 2 as well as Spec 3.
5. **His opening lines were the same on both days.** April 30: "I can come. Or Uber you to house." May 1: "Can I send a Uber for you?" April 30: "im at a hotel," then "Which hotel?" May 1: "we have a room," then "Ok. Where st?" This supports what he has said about a routine (section 17.3), and it supports his reading of "Ok. Where st?" as a question about the place (section 9.5).
6. **"Cash right?" now has an earlier exchange behind it.** On April 30 he asked "Prefer cash or cashapp?" and the agent answered "cash." Payment was not discussed on May 1 until he wrote "Cash right?" at the hotel.
   - The government can say that "right?" asks her to confirm something he already knew, and that the only place he could have learned it was the April 30 conversation. That would be evidence that he knew whose number it was.
   - The answer is the routine. If he asks every number how it wants to be paid, and most say cash, the question shows a habit and not a memory. His other conversations would show that. He has said that the question came from a separate conversation (section 16.1), and a prosecutor will say that the separate conversation was this one, the day before.
   - This is the only message on May 1 that may point back to April 30. It should be dealt with before he gives any account of "Cash right?" This is an observation about fit, not a suggestion about what to say.
   - He has since answered the first of these: a man who had been told would not have asked. That he asked is at least as easy to fit to his account as to the government's. The form of the question is still easier to fit to the government's (section 32.1).
7. **Who raised what on May 1.**

| Subject | Raised by | Message |
|---|---|---|
| Meeting that day | Him | "Are you still around?" |
| How to meet | Him | "Can I send a Uber for you?" |
| A second person | Agent | "no im workin wit my girl tonight" |
| A room | Agent | "we have a room" |
| A price | Agent | "2 for 1 tonight 100" |
| A sexual act | Agent | "ok u gonna cum," then "on both of us lol" |
| Photos | Him | "Do guys have pictures?" |
| Proof that she was real | Him | The peace-sign request |
| Payment | Him | "Cash right?" |
| Meeting in the car, and extra money | Him | The message at 16:58:19 |

   The room, the second person, the price and the explicit words were the agent's. Coming back, the payment question, the car and the extra were his. This bears on Spec 1 (section 19.1) and on whether the idea came from the government (section 23.5).
8. **The photos.** The DPS report says that troopers working undercover "were utilized for the pictures." So the two pictures he was sent on May 1 were of adults, on the government's own account. Neither shows a whole face. The first shows two women from the mouth down. In the second, the hand making the peace sign and a fringe of hair cover the face.
   - For the defense: nothing he was shown on May 1 was a child, and nothing in either picture suggests one.
   - For the government: the pictures are cropped so that no age can be judged from them, and he had been told the age the day before.
   - Section 10 recorded that the second photo "resembled" the woman on the profile. With no face visible, the likeness can only be in hair and general appearance. The prosecutor will put the photo beside the profile and ask what he compared. Sections 10.7 and 11.2 asked what the second photo shows, and this is the answer. It is an observation about fit, not a suggestion about what to say.
9. **"Awesome."** He sent it 3 minutes 51 seconds after the second photo. The government will say that he asked for proof that she was real, received it, and was satisfied. He has said that he still expected a scam when he reached the hotel (section 16.1). Both can be true, but his account has not yet dealt with this message.
10. **"Yes" at 16:42:45, and "I'm here" at 16:48:28.** The arrival came 50 minutes after "Its 20 min away from me," with the stop in Harker Heights between.
11. **Her answers at the hotel.** "dont b," and "u being silly the door is open." Each hesitation was met with encouragement. He did not go up. The DPS report says so in terms (22.4).
12. **Delivery status.** Ten of the agent's messages are marked "sent" and not "delivered": three short ones on April 30, "no im workin wit my girl tonight," "yummy," all three photos, "hello" and "X-Ray Nora Queen." He replied to several of them, so "sent" does not show that a message failed to arrive. It matters for the last two (22.6), and for what the system's times record (section 19.1).

### 22.3 The Army CID report against the log

| What the report says | What the log shows | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| He solicited an agent "who he believed was a 15-year-old minor" (summary, page 1) | Nothing on May 1 about age. The report cites no statement of his and no other source for the belief | His belief on May 1 is the contested element of Specs 1 and 2. The report states it as a fact, three days after the arrest and, as far as the packet shows, before anyone had examined the phone |
| "The UCA stated she was 15 years old but would turn 16 years old soon" | "i will be 16 in june is that ok" | The meaning is the same, and his reply shows that he understood it. The words are not. If a specification or a witness uses the paraphrase, the log corrects it |
| "Initially, S/SAINTLOTH declined to proceed with the UCA due to their age" | "No. Find a different way of life please," 37 seconds later, then "Not in rude way." | The Army's own report accepts the refusal and its reason. That helps on predisposition (section 23.5). It also shows that he read and understood the age, which the government will use |
| He "then agreed to pay $100.00 to engage in sexual activities with two minor females" | She wrote "2 for 1 tonight 100." He wrote "Ok. Where st?" He named no price on May 1. No age was given for the second person | "Agreed to pay $100" is a reading of "Ok" (sections 9.5 and 19.2). "Two minor females" has nothing behind it. The hearing officer's finding that he agreed to pay $100 for both matches this sentence and not the log |
| He "offered to pay for an Uber ... but subsequently agreed to meet at the Comfort Inn" | The offer of a car came first, at 15:51:36, before any second person, room or price was mentioned | The report puts the offer after the agreement. In the log his proposal was a car for one person, and what followed was the agent's counter-proposal |
| He "made statements concerning ejaculating on both minor females" | The agent wrote "ok u gonna cum" and "on both of us lol." He wrote "Yes" two seconds after the second line, and later "Just you….then both" | The words were the agent's. This sentence is the likely source of what Spec 1 alleges, as relayed (section 19.1) |
| He "confirmed the cash payment" | He asked "Cash right?" and the agent answered "yes" | He asked and the agent confirmed. No amount was stated |
| He "stated he was too nervous to go into the hotel and requested the UCA meet in his vehicle" | "Im a little nervous." "Can you walk me upstairs?" Then the message about the car | He did not say that he was too nervous to go in. He asked to be walked up, was told the door was open, and then proposed the car. The report leaves out "walk me upstairs" and "Ill give extra" |

Other points on the Army report:

1. **It is marked "Initial"** and lists one offense, online solicitation of a minor under Texas law. It names no offense under the UCMJ.
2. **The narrative page is dated 30 April 2026,** and it describes the events of 1 May.
3. **It says nothing about an interview.** It ends with his being taken to the DPS office. The DPS report says that Army CID interviewed him there (22.7).
4. **It does not mention "Im convinced lol," "Awesome," or the two-second gap before "Yes."**
5. **It names the site.** The ad was "posted on SkipTheGames." Nothing else in the packet says where the ad was.
6. **It calls the operation joint from April 30.** The DPS report describes an operation on May 1 only. A DPS press release of 4 May 2026 calls it a three-day operation by DPS with Army CID and the Bell County District Attorney's Office (22.8). So April 30 was part of it, and the Army's role on that day is something to ask about (22.11).

### 22.4 The DPS report against the log

1. **Paragraph 2: the pictures were of troopers.** Troopers working undercover "were utilized for the pictures and to converse via voice." This is the government's statement that the pictures he was sent were of adults (22.2).
   - It leaves a question about the ad. He says that his search of the ad's photograph led to the profile of a woman in another state (section 12.1). If the ad's photograph was of a trooper, the search should not have led there. If it was taken from the internet, the report does not say so.
   - So either the ad used a real person's photograph, or his search matched the wrong person. The ad, and the agents' evidence about where its photographs came from, will settle it. Either way the photograph was of an adult.
2. **Paragraph 6 is not about him.** It says that "it was clear that the subjects had the intent of meeting a minor to have sexual intercourse in exchange for money." It is in the plural and describes everyone in the operation. It is a conclusion, and it reads like standard wording.
3. **Paragraph 8 calls the narrative "paraphrases."**
4. **Paragraph 9: the number "was later confirmed to be being used by" him.** It does not say how (22.6).
5. **Paragraphs 10 and 11 quote April 30 accurately,** including the refusal, and add: "The conversation then ended for the night."
6. **Paragraph 12 leaves out two messages.** After "Are you still around?" it goes straight to "no im workin wit my girl tonight." The log has the agent's "yes" and his "Can I send a Uber for you?" in between. As written, the agent's "no" answers a question he did not ask, and the reader never learns that his first proposal was a car for one person.
7. **It does not say that he agreed to a price.** It quotes "2 for 1 tonight 100" and "Ok. Where st?" and leaves the reader to connect them.
8. **Paragraphs 13 to 15 skip from 15:54 to the arrival.** The narrative has none of the sexual lines, "Yes," "Just you….then both," the photos, "Im convinced lol," the peace-sign request or "Awesome." The words that Spec 1 rests on do not appear in the Texas narrative at all.
9. **Paragraph 16 confirms that he did not go up.** "With SAINTLOTH being nervous to come up to the room, SAINTLOTH was taken into custody ... in the parking lot without incident."
10. **Paragraph 18 is the "admission"** (22.7).
11. **Paragraph 20: the phone.** The case agent "secured" it and "will obtain a search warrant ... at a later date." The report is dated 19 May, and nothing in the packet shows that a warrant had been obtained by then.
12. **It is a draft, written 18 days after the arrest.** The entry for the hotel carries a comment naming another man as the person arrested there. The DPS press release lists a man of that surname among the eleven arrested. So the entry was made for someone else's case and reused. That is not an error about him. It does show that the paperwork was shared between the arrests, which is a reason to treat paragraph 6 as standard wording and to ask for the other files (22.11).
13. **The offense line cites the wrong statute** (22.8).

### 22.5 The inventory and the jail forms

**What the inventory records**

- It was made at 17:22 on May 1 by a trooper. The car went to a towing company at 17:23.
- Left in the vehicle: on the front seat, "multiple scent sprays, misc items"; on the rear seat, "military stye pouches, white paper bag, sunglasses"; in the trunk, "misc tools and objects."
- "Items Removed from Vehicle Prior to Inventory" and "Items Taken from Person" are blank.

**What is wrong with it as a record**

1. **The subject's name was never filled in.** The form still reads "Subject Name." Two other fields still hold the form's own prompts. No witness is named, and "Reason Stored" is blank.
2. **The case number on it is not the case number on the report.**
3. **It does not list the phone.** The DPS report says that the case agent secured his phone that day. A form that leaves out the one item everyone agrees was taken cannot show that nothing else was.
4. **It appears to have been made somewhere other than the hotel.** "Location Towed From" is an address whose street number is eight away from the DPS office's, on the same loop road. The coordinates printed on the form are about 1.3 km from the coordinates that the DPS report gives for the hotel. That is my calculation from the two pairs of figures, and counsel should check it on a map. The photographs show the car in a marked space at the hotel. If the figures are right, someone drove it away between 17:02 and 17:22. Nothing in the packet says who, on what authority, or whether it was searched first.
5. **The report lists the vehicle as "Impounded"** and gives no reason.

**What it means for the question of cash**

1. Sections 20.3 and 21.3 said that what he had with him matters, because of *Penaloza*. The packet does not record any cash, wallet or condoms, on this form or anywhere else.
2. That is not the same as a record that there were none. The form is incomplete (point 3 above).
3. The jail's own record should settle it. The transfer checklist in the packet has a stroke against "MONEY/CHECK" and nothing against "PROPERTY." The stroke could mean that money was handed over, or that there was none. The jail's property and money receipt will say.
4. Until then, the government has "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra" in his words, and no record of money. Whether that gap helps depends on what the jail's record shows, which he and counsel can find out and I cannot.

**Two items the government may mention.** The "white paper bag" on the rear seat: the form does not say what was in it, and section 9.3 already expected a question about what he bought on the way. The "multiple scent sprays" on the front seat: a prosecutor may call them preparation, and they fit an ordinary car just as well.

**The jail forms.** The arrest card gives the arrest as 5:02 pm on May 1 and the release to the jail as 19:03, as far as the handwriting can be read. The charge is written as "Prostitution of a minor < 18 years old," a second-degree felony.

### 22.6 The arrest and the removal of the apps

| Time | Event | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 16:48:28 | "I'm here" | Log |
| 16:59:33 | "Black suv" | Log |
| 17:00:23 | Agent: "we look out the window are yur lights on" | Log |
| 17:01:20 | "Ya," his last message | Log |
| 17:02 | The texting apps are removed | His timeline, shown as verified |
| 5:02 pm | Arrest | Jail card, in the case agent's handwriting |
| 17:03:04 | Agent sends "X-Ray Nora Queen," marked "sent" | Log |
| 17:03:24 | Agent places a recorded voice call | Log |
| 17:22 and 17:23 | Inventory, then the tow | Inventory form |
| 19:03, as far as it can be read | Released to the jail | Jail card |

1. **The arrest time is now known.** Sections 9.3, 16.1 and 20.3 said that it would decide how much the removal of the apps shows. The two are recorded in the same minute.
2. **He had no time to drive away.** His last message was at 17:01:20. That answers the question "then why didn't you drive away?" for the time after the removal. It does not answer it for the thirteen minutes before, in which the log shows him still arranging the meeting.
3. **Which came first is not in the packet.** Both times are given to the minute, and the arrest time was written on a jail card later that evening. Body-camera video will give the arrest to the second. The phone's own record will give the removal to the second.
   - If the removal came first, the government will say that he saw the arrest team coming, and the defense will point to the routine (sections 13.1 and 14.3).
   - If the phone's record puts the removal after the agents had hold of him, a different question arises: who was holding the phone.
   - Counsel should have both times before anyone says what the removal was. Section 24 sets out what each reading requires in law.
4. **The last two messages from the agent are marked "sent" and not "delivered."** That fits the app being gone by 17:03:04. It also fits a phone that was switched off or out of his hands. He has since said that the phone was put in airplane mode only after the interview. If it was switched on and connected until then, the first reading is the likelier (section 26.1).
5. **"X-Ray Nora Queen" is the name the system gave this conversation** (DPS report, paragraph 8). I read that message, and the call 20 seconds later, as the team's way of matching the phone in front of them to the conversation. If the app had already been removed, neither would have reached the phone. The report says only that the number "was later confirmed" as his. This is my reading, and the agents can be asked.
6. **Where he was.** The photograph from above shows the car in a marked space at the edge of the lot, parked nose out, with its headlights on. The photograph of him appears to have been taken beside the same fence. The DPS report says that he was taken into custody "in the parking lot without incident." So he was arrested where he had parked. He had not driven off, and he had not gone to the door.

### 22.7 The interview paperwork

1. **Two forms, both from the DPS polygraph unit.** A waiver of rights, marked page 1 of 2, and a polygraph consent, marked page 2 of 2. Both carry the name of the DPS polygraph examiner, as the person who gave the warning on the first and as the witness on the second. Both are dated May 1 and signed. Neither has a time.
2. **The waiver gives five warnings.** He may remain silent and any statement may be used at his trial. Any statement may be used as evidence in court. He may have a lawyer present before and during questioning. A lawyer will be appointed if he cannot employ one. He may stop the interview at any time. The waiver does not say what he was suspected of.
3. **The consent form does.** The examination is "regarding: ONLINE SOLICITATION OF A MINOR." It also says that "this interview and examination will be both audio and video recorded."
4. **The DPS report describes two things** (paragraph 18). "Army CID conducted an interview with SAINTLOTH." The DPS examiner "also conducted a polygraph examination." "For details of the polygraph examination, review the interview video."
5. **There is no record of the Army's part.** The packet has no Army rights form and no agent's account of the interview, and the Army report does not mention one (22.3).
6. **Why that matters, as I understand it.**
   - Article 31(b) applies to a person subject to the Code who questions a suspect in an official capacity, and to anyone knowingly acting as that person's agent, which is how civilian Army investigators are treated (M.R.E. 305(b)(1)). Before any question, the suspect must be told the nature of the accusation, that he need not make any statement, and that what he says may be used against him in a trial by court-martial. A statement taken without that warning cannot be used at a court-martial (Article 31(d)).
   - Civilian police who question a soldier for their own purposes need only meet the civilian rules (M.R.E. 305(f)(1)). The exception is where the two investigations have merged, or where the civilian is acting as an instrument of the military.
   - Here both agencies call the operation joint, Army agents were on the arrest team, and the DPS report says that Army CID did the interviewing.
   - So the questions are these: who asked the questions; what warning came before them and from whom; and in what order the interview and the polygraph came. The video answers all three.
   - Section 7 raised this as a possible motion. The packet makes it more concrete. It is for counsel.
7. **The time available.** About two hours passed between the arrest and the release to the jail. That had to cover the drive to the office, the Army interview, the polygraph with its own interview before the test, and the drive to the jail. The video will show how long each took and in what order.
8. **The sentence about an admission.** In full: the case agent "was not present during the interview but was informed that SAINTLOTH admitted to knowingly speaking to a minor."
   - The writer was not there.
   - It does not say who informed him.
   - It quotes no words.
   - It does not say which day. "Knowingly speaking to a minor" describes April 30 on his own account: he was told, and he refused. The sentence does not say that he knew on May 1.
   - It is in a draft written 18 days later.
   - The hearing officer watched the video and did not cite it for knowledge of her age (section 2).
   - At trial the report is not evidence of what he said. The video is, and so is a witness who heard it. Counsel should find out who "informed" the case agent and whether that person wrote anything down.
9. **The polygraph rule is unchanged** (section 8.2). The examination, its result and any reference to it stay out under M.R.E. 707. What he said during it can come in, if it is otherwise admissible.

### 22.8 Three names for the Texas offense

| Document | Offense named | What it requires about age, as I understand it |
|---|---|---|
