7. **The defense's route to the provider runs through his consent.** As I understand the rules, defense counsel in a court-martial cannot issue a subpoena, and a request made through the trial counsel is seen by the government. Whether the military judge can be asked directly is for counsel (25.3).
8. **Section 6 has a new question, 21, and section 7 a new item, 14.**
9. **The order of work is unchanged:** the video first, the forensic report second, the account third. The provider's records belong with the second.

## 26. Addendum 19, 25 Nov 2026: four statements about the texting account, the seized phone, and a six-month limit

**What this covers.** A new detail and four statements supplied after section 25, recorded as relayed. The source of the statements was not given.

*As relayed: the original phone was seized and is in airplane mode. Directly after the interview, another representative asked him to place the phone in airplane mode. With that in mind, how accurate are these four statements?*

*1. Accessing or handing over credentials creates a significant risk of altering metadata or local app state, which the prosecution could use to claim evidence tampering.*

*2. Subpoenaing the provider directly is premature at this stage. Most modern messaging services utilize end-to-end encryption or strict retention schedules, meaning providers rarely hold readable message bodies on their servers, only raw metadata (such as timestamps and connection logs), while the actual chat text resides exclusively on the physical devices.*

*3. [If he] accessed or synced this messaging app on a secondary device that was not seized by law enforcement (such as a tablet or desktop computer), [it] should be left offline (in Airplane Mode or powered off) and preserved in its current state without opening it.*

*4. Logging in creates risk that law enforcement would predict the move by (already having subpoenaed provider) waiting for user to login thereby establishing ownership of account (or this gives law enforcement too much credit).*

The four statements are in the words supplied, with two changes: a dash in the second has become a comma, and the words in brackets have been added to the third.

I have not seen the phone, the video or any record of the request. I do not know who made the request, or for which agency. I could not open the provider's sites from here, so what follows about its support pages and its privacy policy rests on what a web search returns from them (section 14). Counsel should read the policy itself. *United States v. Mitchell* was read in full. *Riley v. California*, the rules and statutes, and the technical guidance were read in extracts. Statements of law are my understanding, for counsel to confirm. If any of the four statements came from counsel or from an examiner, it may rest on information that I do not have, and the right course is to ask what it rests on.

**Short version**

- **First, a date.** Checking statement 2 turned up the provider's privacy policy. It says that message content is kept "Indefinitely for active account and 6 months for inactive accounts." He says that he has not used the account since May 1. If the six months ran from that day, they ended about 1 November. If they run from the lapse of his number, they end about the end of November. I cannot tell which, or whether anything is left. Today is the day before Thanksgiving. A request to preserve the account should be put to counsel today (26.4).
- **The new detail settles one thing.** Nothing done from outside can alter the seized phone while it stays offline, and the app was already off it. So the risk in a login was never to that phone. It is to the provider's record of the account.
- **Statement 1 has the right conclusion and partly the wrong reasons.** "Local app state" does not apply to the seized phone. A login would add to the provider's record, and would make later findings harder to date (section 25.4). "Handing over credentials" depends on who receives them. Given to an examiner working for counsel, they are part of the proper route. Given to the government, they may amount to consent to a search, which is a decision for counsel.
- **Statement 2 is mostly wrong for this app, and wrong on the point that matters.** It describes services like Signal. This provider's policy speaks of "message content stored on our servers," and says for how long. The app was removed from the seized phone, so that phone is the place where the text is least likely to be. A short retention period is a reason to ask now.
- **One part of statement 2 is right, for a different reason.** The defense cannot issue a subpoena, and counsel may have sound tactical reasons to hold back formal process. Neither applies to a request to preserve, or to a request for his own records made with his consent.
- **Statement 3 is right, if such a device exists.** It should go offline, unopened, and counsel should be told. A second device that last synced before the arrest could hold a copy of his conversation list with a date of its own, which is what section 25.2 said was missing. Keeping it safe is not the same as keeping it from anyone.
- **Statement 4 gives law enforcement too much credit, as he suspects, and names the wrong risk.** Nobody disputes whose account it is, and nobody needs to be waiting. A login is likely to be recorded anyway, and records of that kind can be obtained later. What it would show is access after the arrest.
- **One new caution.** Airplane mode is a setting, and it lasts until someone changes it. Nothing should be sent toward that phone from his Google account: no erase, no lock, no sign-out, no removal from the account's list of devices. A soldier who reset a seized phone remotely was convicted for it (section 25.4).
- **The request itself raises questions for counsel:** who asked, whether he had to unlock the phone, what else was done while it was in his hands, and when the government copied what the phone holds.

### 26.1 The new detail: the phone, and the request

*As relayed: the phone was seized. Directly after the interview, another representative asked him to put it in airplane mode, and he did.*

1. **What airplane mode does.** It takes the phone off the cellular network. The Supreme Court has said: "Remote wiping can be fully prevented by disconnecting a phone from the network" (*Riley v. California*, 573 U.S. 373 (2014)). The guide on mobile device forensics published by the National Institute of Standards and Technology says: "Isolating a mobile device from all radio networks (e.g. WiFi, Cellular and Bluetooth) is important to keep new traffic, such as SMS messages, from overwriting existing data" (Special Publication 800-101, revision 1). So the request was an ordinary step to preserve the phone.
2. **What it does not do.** It is a setting and not a seal. The guide names Wi-Fi and Bluetooth as well as the cellular network, and whether those were off is not known. The setting lasts until someone changes it, and the phone is in the government's hands. In *Strong* the agents had put the phone in a "Faraday-labeled bag," and the soldier was still able to reset it from outside (section 25.4).
3. **The phone was not isolated until then.** The arrest was at 5:02 pm. The request came after the interview, and he was released to the jail at about 19:03 (section 22.6). The request shows that the phone was switched on, and not yet in airplane mode, when it was made. It does not show what state the phone was in at 17:03:04, when the agent's code-name message was marked "sent" and not "delivered." Section 22.6 said that this status fitted the app being gone, and also fitted a phone that was switched off. If the phone was on and connected from the arrest until the request, the first explanation is the likelier. The video, the record of who held the phone and the phone's own data may show whether it was. It is a small point either way. What "sent" and "delivered" mean in that system is not known, and he replied to several messages that were marked "sent" (section 22.2).
4. **He handled the phone after the arrest, at someone's request.** If the phone shows activity that evening, this is one explanation for it. An examiner may also find a record of when airplane mode was switched on. Whether the phone keeps one, and for how long, I could not confirm. If it does, that gives a time for the end of the interview, and a check on what else was done in the same minutes. The guide says that switching it on "requires interaction with the mobile device using the keypad, which poses some risk," and less risk if the person "documents the actions taken (e.g., on paper or on video)." Here the person who did it was the accused. Whether anyone recorded it is a question for the video and the reports.
5. **It does not change the reasoning in section 25.2.** That section said that the app came off the phone at 17:02 and the phone was taken. He had the phone in his hands once more. The app was no longer on it, and an examination may be able to show that nothing was installed or opened in those minutes.
6. **Some of what the phone holds is cleared with age.** Two examples:
   - Android keeps a record of which app was in use, event by event. That is the record that could show what was open in the minutes before 15:49 (section 7). Android's documentation says of it: "Events are only kept by the system for a few days."
   - Google's location timeline is kept on the phone, and by default it deletes entries after three months (section 19.2).

   I could not confirm how either behaves on a phone that is offline or switched off, and an examiner has other sources. So the date on which the government copied the phone matters. If it has not been copied, the examiner should know this before the phone is next switched on.
7. **Questions for counsel.**
   - Who asked, for which agency, and on what authority the phone was then held. The DPS report says that the case agent secured it that day (section 22.5).
   - Whether he had to unlock the phone, and whether he was asked for its passcode or for consent to search it. As relayed, he told the agents to look at his phone (section 16.5).
   - Whether he had asked for a lawyer by then. In *United States v. Mitchell*, 76 M.J. 413 (C.A.A.F. 2017), agents asked a soldier to unlock his phone after he had invoked his right to counsel, and the court held that "the contents of the phone must therefore be suppressed." He signed a waiver (section 22.7), so that case may have no application. The video will show what was said.
   - Whether the phone has been kept switched on, when it was examined, and whether a copy of its contents was made soon after May 1.
8. **What the phone is good for, and how the defense gets it.** If it has stayed offline since that evening, nothing he has done since can have changed it. What the defense needs is a copy of the government's examination (section 25.9). Who holds the phone decides how that is asked for. As I understand R.C.M. 701(a)(2)(A), the defense may inspect data "within the possession, custody, or control of military authorities." If the Texas agency holds the phone, the request may have to run through the Texas case as well. For counsel. He has since said that the reason given in Texas for waiting is the crime lab's review of the data. Who holds the phone, and whether its data has been copied, is still to be confirmed (section 29.2). He has since said that DPS has held the phone since May 1 (section 30.2).
9. **What he should not do.** Nothing from his Google account that is aimed at that phone. Google's help page says that the owner of a lost Android device can "find, secure or erase it remotely." Whether a command sent while the phone is offline would take effect when it next connects is something I could not confirm, and it is not something to find out. Ordinary use of the account on another phone is a different thing from sending an erase or a lock, and he can hardly avoid it. It is not without effect. In a synced account, a change or a deletion made on one device is normally carried to the others when they next connect. Section 13.7 already says that nothing in the account should be deleted. If he has changed the account's password or its list of devices since May 1, counsel should be told, so that nothing the examiner finds comes as a surprise.
10. **One possible sign of whether the phone has been online.** His Google account has a page that lists the devices signed in to it. Google says that the times on that page "represent the last time there was communication between the device or session and Google's systems." If the seized phone is listed, the time against it is worth knowing. A time later than the evening of May 1 would suggest that the phone has been connected while in the government's hands. Its absence would prove nothing, because the page is described as showing devices signed in now or "in the last few weeks." Counsel or the examiner should confirm which entry is the seized phone, and read it with the record of who has held the phone. Reading the page sends no command. The same page can sign a device out, and that should not be used on this phone. Whether to look, and how to record what is there, is for counsel.

### 26.2 Statement 1: accessing the account, or handing over credentials

*As relayed: Accessing or handing over credentials creates a significant risk of altering metadata or local app state, which the prosecution could use to claim evidence tampering.*

**How accurate.** The conclusion is right. Two of its three reasons need correcting, and it treats two different acts as one.

1. **"Local app state" does not apply to the seized phone.** The app was removed from it at 17:02 on May 1. Android's documentation says: "When the user uninstalls your app, the files saved in app-specific storage are removed." The phone has been offline since that evening. A login on another device cannot reach it.
2. **The only app data a login would create is on the new device.** It would be made on the day of the login. It says nothing about May 1, and it is not evidence that anyone could be said to have altered.
3. **"Metadata" is the real point, and it is on the provider's side.** A login now would probably do these things to the provider's record of the account:
   - add a login, with its time, device and network address (26.6);
   - bring up the prompt for a new number, because the old one will have lapsed (section 25.4);
   - show, on the new device, whatever reached the account after 17:02 on May 1 and has never been opened. The agent's message at 17:03:04 would be one such item, if it reached the provider. Replies from the other numbers he had written to that afternoon would be others. If the provider records whether a message has been passed to a device, or read, that record bears on when the app left the phone and on who was still writing to him. I do not know whether it keeps one. A login could change it;
   - end the argument that nobody has had access since the arrest (section 25.2).
4. **"Tampering" is too strong for what a login is.** As I understand the offenses set out in sections 24.3 and 25.4, each needs an intent to obstruct the proceedings or to impair evidence, and the Texas and federal ones also need something to be altered, destroyed or concealed. A look at his own account, made without that intent and changing nothing, meets none of them. The accurate version is narrower. After a login that only he made, it may be hard to show that nothing was changed. How hard depends on what the provider logs (section 25.4). The government could raise it against any finding that helped him.
5. **"Handing over credentials" depends on who receives them.**
   - **Counsel, or an examiner working for counsel.** This is the route that sections 7 and 25.3 recommend. The examiner is "the lawyer's representative" (*Turner*, section 25.3). An examiner's login has the same effects on the account as his own would. The differences are that it is recorded, and that it is made by someone who can be questioned about it. That is why production by the provider comes first and a capture second.
   - **The government.** Giving credentials voluntarily may be treated as consent to a search of the account. It could put every conversation in the account in the government's hands, including those with other people on other days (section 9.4). Whether there is valid consent, and how far it goes, are questions for counsel. So is the answer to any request for a password, or for the phone's passcode. He is represented, and a request of that kind should reach him through counsel.
   - **Anyone else.** A friend or a relative who logs in for him is his own login with a second witness to it. Nothing said to that person is privileged.
6. **What the credential is.** The provider's page on its browser version speaks of signing in "with Apple, Google, or email." If he signed in with Google, the credential for this account may be the credential for his Google account, which holds his searches, his locations and his mail. That is one more reason for the provider to produce the records, and for a password to go only to someone who needs it.
7. **Credentials should not be written down here,** or in a message or an email. Counsel will say how to pass them, if they are needed at all.

### 26.3 Statement 2: whether asking the provider is premature

*As relayed: Subpoenaing the provider directly is premature at this stage. Most modern messaging services utilize end-to-end encryption or strict retention schedules, meaning providers rarely hold readable message bodies on their servers, only raw metadata (such as timestamps and connection logs), while the actual chat text resides exclusively on the physical devices.*

**How accurate.** For this provider it is mostly wrong, and wrong in the direction that matters. The one thing it gets right, a retention schedule, points the other way.

1. **The general statement describes other services.** Signal has answered a federal subpoena by saying that "the only information we can produce in response to a request like this is the date and time a user registered with Signal and the last date of a user's connectivity to the Signal service." WhatsApp is reported as saying that it "cannot and does not produce the content of its user's messages." Both are as quoted by other sites. For services built in that way the statement is right.
2. **This provider is not built in that way, on its own description.**
   - Its support page says: "TextFree does not support end-to-end encryption of text messages, however, our servers are encrypted."
   - Its privacy policy says that it uses "key words gathered from outbound message content stored on our servers" for advertising. A provider that can take key words from messages holds them in a form it can read.
   - "Reinstalling the app will not cause you to lose your number or any messages" (section 14.2). Messages that come back after an app has been removed were not kept only on the phone.
   - It offers a download of conversation history, and says that a data request "may include deleted messages, if they are still available" (section 25.1).
   - His own answers describe the same thing. After the reinstall at 13:11 on May 1, conversations from April 30 were in his list (section 13.4).
3. **"Exclusively on the physical devices" is the reverse of the position here.** The app was removed from the seized phone, and removal takes the app's stored data with it (26.2). That phone is the place where the text is least likely to be. The text existed in three places: the provider's systems, the undercover system, and the phones of the other people he wrote to.
4. **"Strict retention schedules" is right, and it is why the conclusion is wrong.** This provider has a schedule, and it is short (26.4). A short period is a reason to ask at once. It is not a reason to wait.
5. **"Only raw metadata" undersells what that would be worth.** A record of which numbers his account wrote to, and when, would show:
   - whether he was writing to other numbers in the minutes around 15:49, which is the center of his account of the first message (section 19.2);
   - whether his account sent a second message near 16:00 (section 25.5);
   - whether anything reached the account after 17:02;
   - whether anyone has logged in since.

   The privacy policy lists, among the data the provider collects, "information about those messages (date, duration, recipients, size, attachments)." One row of its table, whose heading the search did not return, gives "2 years account activity." So records of that kind may outlast the text. That is for counsel to confirm with the provider.
6. **"Subpoenaing" is the wrong word, for a reason the statement does not give.** As I understand it, the defense in a court-martial cannot issue a subpoena (section 25.3). The process in the Stored Communications Act is written for the government. For content that has been stored for 180 days or less, a "governmental entity" needs a warrant, and the statute gives other means for older content (18 U.S.C. § 2703(a)). A subpoena reaches records such as "records of session times and durations" (§ 2703(c)(2)). The defense's way in is his consent (section 25.7), and before that a request to preserve.
7. **What may be right about "premature."** Counsel may have reasons to hold back formal process that have nothing to do with encryption.
   - Before referral there may be no judge to ask.
   - A request through the trial counsel tells the government what the defense is looking at, and may put the answer in the government's hands (section 25.3).
   - The government may already have the provider's records. If so, they come in discovery.
   - What the defense obtains can become something it has to show. Under R.C.M. 701(b)(3), once the government has complied with a defense request for its documents, the trial counsel may ask to inspect items in the defense's hands, if "the defense intends to use the item in the defense case-in-chief at trial."

   These are reasons about formal process. None is a reason to put off a request that the provider preserve the account, or a request for his own records made with his consent. That subparagraph, as I read it, reaches only what the defense intends to use in its own case. Whether any other duty to disclose applies is for counsel.
8. **If the statement is counsel's or an examiner's,** he should ask what it rests on, and show counsel the lines of the provider's policy quoted here and in 26.4.

### 26.4 The six months

1. **What the policy says.** As a web search returns it, the provider's privacy policy has a table of how long each kind of information is kept. One row is headed "Message Content," which it describes as "Text messages you send and receive" and "Voicemails." The period given is: "Indefinitely for active account and 6 months for inactive accounts, with automated implementation of this process to be completed in Q1 of 2024." The search returned that row twice, in the same words. I could not open the page, and counsel should read it.
2. **What it does not say, in what I could see.** When an account becomes "inactive," and from what day the six months are counted. The provider's page on numbers uses "inactivity" to mean that the user has "not sent a message nor made an outgoing phone call in TextFree within 30 days." Whether the policy uses the word in the same way, I do not know.
3. **The dates.** His last message from the account was at 17:01:20 on May 1, and the app was removed a minute later. On his account, nothing has been done with it since.

| If the six months are counted from | They end about |
|---|---|
| The last use, on May 1 | 1 November 2026. That has passed |
| The lapse of the number, 30 days later | 27 to 30 November 2026. That is within days |

If the provider's process runs late, or counts in some other way, there may be more time. If a paid plan kept the number (section 14.2), the account may not count as inactive at all. I cannot tell. He has since said that a paid plan kept the number until 26 July 2026. Section 27.1 sets out the dates again. Today is Wednesday 25 November, and tomorrow is Thanksgiving.
4. **What is at stake.** The words of every conversation in the account:
   - her conversation as his account held it (section 25.2);
   - the conversations with other numbers that afternoon, which are the only direct record of whom he says he meant to write to (section 14.2);
   - the line he recalls (section 25.5).

   The undercover log would remain. So might the provider's records of activity, if "2 years account activity" covers them (26.3). The words would not.
5. **It cuts both ways.** Preserving the account keeps all of it, including what does not help him, and what is kept the government can later obtain (section 25.2). On his own account of May 1, what the account holds helps him. If he has any doubt about that, it is something to tell counsel before the request goes, and not to write here. That is one more reason for the choice to be counsel's.
6. **What earlier sections said, and what is different.** Sections 14.2 and 25 said that preservation was urgent, and that the way to deal with it was to press counsel. Both stand. What is new is a stated period, which on one reading runs out within days. Sections 14.2 and 25.1 also said that the provider does not publish how long it keeps messages. For message content that was wrong, and both have been corrected.
7. **What can be done in a day.** These are for counsel to choose among, and more than one can be done.
   - **A letter from counsel to the provider.** It would ask the provider to keep everything in the account, identify the account by its sign-in and by the number it had on May 1, and say that it is sent for the account holder. As I understand the statute, a provider is obliged to preserve only on the request of a "governmental entity," so a letter from the defense may not bind it. It is still the account holder asking the provider not to delete his own data. It costs little, it changes nothing in the account, and it does not go through the government. Either of his lawyers can send it.
   - **A preservation request from the government.** That one binds. On the request of "trial counsel, or other authorized counsel for the Government," a provider "shall take all necessary steps to preserve records and other evidence in its possession," for 90 days and for 90 more if the request is renewed (R.C.M. 703A; 18 U.S.C. § 2703(f)). The cost is that the trial counsel learns that the defense wants the account kept, and that the account is then kept for the government as well. Against that, the government already knows of the account, holds its own side of this conversation, and may have asked the provider months ago.
   - **Asking whether the government already has the records.** If it obtained them in May or since, the text is not lost (section 25.7).
   - **Making the account active again.** If the policy uses "inactive" as the page on numbers does, that would take a message sent or a call made, and, with the number lapsed, the choice of a new number first. I could not confirm that this would stop a deletion, or that anything is left for it to save. It would change the account more than a look would, with every cost in section 25.4. If it is done at all, it is done on counsel's decision, by the examiner or in counsel's presence, with each step recorded.
8. **Why the request should be in writing, with its date.** As I understand R.C.M. 703(e)(2), a party is not entitled to the production of evidence that has been destroyed or lost. There is an exception where the evidence is "of such central importance to an issue that it is essential to a fair trial" and there is "no adequate substitute." The judge then grants a continuance or other relief, or abates the proceedings, "unless the unavailability of the evidence is the fault of or could have been prevented by the requesting party." Whether that rule could apply to records that a provider deleted on its own schedule is for counsel. Either way, a dated request shows what the defense did to prevent the loss, and when.
9. **How this bears on section 25.4.** That section set the costs of a look by him against a benefit that could be had through counsel anyway. It assumed that there was time. A letter does not act as fast as a login. Someone at the provider has to read it and act on it, and it would arrive on the eve of a holiday. If the choice really were between a recorded step that kept the account alive and the loss of the text, the comparison would come out differently, because a record with a login against it is worth more than a record that no longer exists. I cannot tell from here whether that is the choice. The text may already be gone, or it may be safe for months. It is a decision for counsel, with these dates in front of counsel, and it should be made now and not after the weekend.
10. **What he should not do meanwhile.**
   - Open the account on his own. If the text has gone, that saves nothing and costs what section 25.4 says. If it has not, the step that might save it is one for counsel to decide on and to record.
   - Tell the provider anything about the events or the case. What he writes to its support desk is a statement to a third party, with none of the protection of a note to counsel (section 11.5). Point 11 describes the only kind of request that says none of that. That request has since been withdrawn unless counsel agrees to it (section 32.5).
   - Follow a link in any email from the provider. The provider says that it sends a warning "to your verified email address" when a number is near expiry. Any such email dates the lapse, and a later one may say more. He can read them without touching the account, and he should pass them to counsel. A link in one of them may act on the account.
11. **If counsel cannot be reached today.** *One part of this point has since been withdrawn. The written request of his own that it describes is not to be sent unless counsel agrees to it (section 32.5).* He should leave the message by telephone and in writing, with the line from the policy and the dates in point 3. He should keep trying both of his lawyers, and the office that his military counsel works in. A message sent today is itself a record of when he raised it. If no lawyer answers before the weekend, there is one step short of a login. It is a short written request to the provider, from him as the account holder, to keep everything in the account and to delete nothing, saying that his lawyer will follow. It would identify the account and say nothing else: nothing about the events, the case or his reasons. It changes nothing in the account, and it is a dated record that he asked. It is not confidential, and it is not free of risk. It is a statement by him that the account is his, the provider could pass it on if it were ever asked, and I do not know how the provider would answer. If the answer is a request to log in, or to give a password, that goes to counsel and is not acted on. A copy of whatever he sends should go to counsel the same day. It is a fallback. A letter from counsel carries more weight, and a request from the government binds. This fallback was written without his military counsel's instruction in front of me. As relayed since, that instruction is against any investigating of his own, and it covers a request of this kind. The fallback is withdrawn unless she agrees to it (section 32.5).

### 26.5 Statement 3: a second device

*As relayed: [If he] accessed or synced this messaging app on a secondary device that was not seized by law enforcement (such as a tablet or desktop computer), [it] should be left offline (in Airplane Mode or powered off) and preserved in its current state without opening it.*

**How accurate.** Right as a first step, if such a device exists. It leaves out what comes next.

1. **Why it is right.** An app that is opened while online normally brings itself up to date with the provider. If the provider has dropped his history, or drops it later, the copy on that device may be dropped as well. A device that stays offline and unopened keeps whatever it held when it last connected.
2. **What such a copy would be worth.** It is a picture of his account on the day that the device last connected. Depending on the day, it could show whether her April 30 conversation was in his list before 15:49 on May 1. That is the date that section 25.2 said was missing, and it would come from a device and not from his word. It could equally show the April 30 lines still in place.
3. **Whether there is one, only he knows.** Section 15.5 asked whether he had ever used the app in a browser, and on which computer. The same question applies to a tablet or an old phone.
4. **What to add to the statement.**
   - Tell counsel now, and let the examiner copy the device. He should make no copies or screenshots of his own.
   - Check that Wi-Fi is off as well. The forensic guide lists it separately from the cellular network (26.1).
   - Do not open the app, update it or remove it.
   - If the device has been online since May 1 with the app on it, the advice comes late and still applies. Counsel should be told how the device has been used since. It bears on the argument in section 25.2 that nobody has had access since the arrest.
5. **A computer is harder, and the examiner should say what to do with it.** If the account was ever opened in a browser, that browser may hold more than a record of visits: stored site data, cached pages and a saved session. Ordinary use can overwrite these, and a restored tab can reconnect to the site without anyone meaning it to. So he should not open the provider's site, clear the browser's history or stored data, or run a cleaning tool. Counsel should get the examiner's advice quickly on whether the computer should be disconnected, left as it is or shut down. One part of the record ages by design. Google says of Chrome: "Your history lists the pages that you've visited on Chrome in the last 90 days." Visits in April and May may already have gone from the computer's own list. They may still be in the Google account's activity (section 13.7).
6. **Keeping it safe is not keeping it from anyone.** The device must not be wiped, altered, lent or thrown away. If the government ever comes for it with a warrant, he must not get in the way (section 25.4, on *Strong*). Whether and when the defense tells the government about it is counsel's decision, under the discovery rules (26.3).
7. **Counsel may prefer that the examiner copy it where it is.** There are professional rules about a lawyer who takes possession of an item of evidence, and counsel will know them.

### 26.6 Statement 4: a login as a trap

*As relayed: Logging in creates risk that law enforcement would predict the move by (already having subpoenaed provider) waiting for user to login thereby establishing ownership of account (or this gives law enforcement too much credit).*

**How accurate.** His second thought is probably the better one. Nothing supplied suggests that anyone is waiting for a login, and the risk as described is not the one that matters. A different risk is.

1. **Ownership is not in doubt, and proving it needs no login.** He was arrested at the place the messages named, in the car they described, with the phone. The DPS report says that the number "was later confirmed" as his (section 22.6). His own case depends on the account being his: the image search, the routine and the other conversations are all his records.
2. **Waiting for a login is a different thing from asking for records.** A subpoena returns the records that exist when it is answered. To be told of a login as it happens, the government would need something more, which looks forward. The statute I know of is the one on pen registers. It defines a pen register as a device or process that records "dialing, routing, addressing, or signaling information," and says that, with exceptions, "no person may install or use a pen register or a trap and trace device without first obtaining a court order." What authority would be needed here is for counsel. I can see no purpose that such an order would serve in this case. Nothing rules it out.
3. **Nothing in the packet suggests that kind of attention.** Eleven men were arrested in three days (section 22.8). The DPS report is a draft, with a statute that does not fit, a form with the name not filled in, and another man's name on the hotel entry (section 22.9). Whether the government asked the provider for anything at all is not known (section 25.1).
4. **The real point is simpler. A login may be recorded whether or not anyone is waiting.** The provider's policy lists "IP addresses," "unique device identifiers" and "date/time stamp" among the data it collects, and one row of its table gives "2 years account activity" (26.3). That does not say that every login is logged, or for how long. The statute lets the government obtain by subpoena "records of session times and durations" and "any temporarily assigned network address" (18 U.S.C. § 2703(c)(2)). So he should assume that a login made this month could be read from the provider's records next year.
5. **What it would show is access, and not ownership.** If it is recorded, it would show that the account was opened on a certain day, after his arrest and with charges pending, and perhaps from which network. Section 25.4 sets out what the government could do with that.
6. **So the conclusion stands, and the reason should change.** He should assume that anything done with the account since May 1 can be reconstructed later. He need not assume that anyone is watching.

### 26.7 The four statements, side by side

| Statement | How accurate | What is right in it | What is wrong, or missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. A login, or a handover of credentials, risks altering metadata or app state, and a claim of tampering | Partly | He should not log in. A login changes the provider's record of the account | The seized phone cannot be affected. A look is not tampering. Credentials given to counsel's examiner are part of the proper route |
| 2. Asking the provider is premature, because providers hold only metadata | Mostly wrong for this provider | Other services work that way. The defense cannot issue a subpoena. Counsel may have reasons to hold back formal process | This provider stores message content and says for how long. That time may be nearly up, or past. A request to preserve is overdue |
| 3. A second device should go offline and stay unopened | Right | All of it, as a first step | Counsel should be told, and the examiner should copy it. Wi-Fi should be off as well. It must not be wiped or hidden |
| 4. Law enforcement may be waiting for a login, to prove ownership | Mostly wrong | A login is likely to be recorded, and records of that kind can be obtained later | Ownership is not disputed. Nobody needs to be waiting. What a login shows is access after the arrest |

### 26.8 What to take to counsel

**Today**

1. **The provider's six months** (26.4): the line from its privacy policy, the two dates, and the request that the account be preserved. Ask which of the steps in 26.4, point 7, counsel will take, and when.
2. **Whether the government has the provider's records,** or has asked for them to be preserved (section 25.7).
3. **Any email from the provider since May 1,** passed on as it is, with no link in it followed. He has since described one, about the expiry of the number (section 27.1).

**Soon**

4. **The request to put the phone in airplane mode:** who made it, when, whether he unlocked the phone, whether he gave a passcode or consent, and whether it is on the video (26.1).
5. **The phone since then:** who holds it, whether it has been kept switched on, whether Wi-Fi was off, and when its contents were copied.
6. **Any second device** on which the account was ever opened, and how it has been used since May 1 (26.5).
7. **The Google account:** any change since May 1 to its password or its list of devices, and whether the seized phone appears on that list with a recent time (26.1).
8. **Any request by the government** for a password, a passcode or consent. The answer comes from counsel.
9. **If any of the four statements came from counsel or from an examiner:** what it rests on.

**Records to add.** These add to sections 22.11 and 25.9.

1. The phone's log for the evening of May 1: the time airplane mode was switched on, and anything else done in those minutes.
2. The record of who has held the phone, and of each time it has been switched on or connected.
3. The date and method of the government's copy of the phone.
4. The provider's privacy policy and help pages as they stand now, saved with their dates by counsel or the examiner.
5. From the provider: its record of when the account became inactive, of any deletion of message content under its policy, and of account activity since April 30.

### 26.9 What this changes

1. **Counsel's advice not to open the account still stands, with better reasons than the four statements give.** The seized phone is not at risk. The provider's record is.
2. **Statement 2 is the one that could do harm.** It tells him that the provider has nothing to give and that there is time. The provider's own pages say that it stores message content, and they give a period that may be running out.
3. **Preservation now has a date on it.** Sections 14.2 and 25 called it urgent. The provider's stated period may make it a matter of days, and it may already be too late. Only the provider can say.
4. **Two corrections.** Sections 14.2 and 25.1 said that the provider does not publish how long it keeps messages. Its privacy policy gives six months for message content on inactive accounts. Notes have been added in both places.
5. **Section 25.4 assumed that there was time.** Its comparison holds as long as the same result can be had through counsel. Whether a recorded step to keep the account alive is now worth its cost is for counsel (26.4).
6. **The "sent" status at 17:03 may be better explained by the removal of the app** than by a phone that was switched off, if the phone was on and connected until the request (26.1). Section 22.6 has a note.
7. **The phone raises its own questions:** who had him handle it, whether he unlocked it, and when it was copied. Some of what it holds is cleared with age.
8. **Nothing is to be sent toward the phone from his Google account.**
9. **Section 1 has been updated, and section 7 has a new item, 15.**
10. **The order of work has one thing put in front of it:** the request to preserve the account, because it is the only item with a date on it. After that it is as before: the video, the forensic report, and the account.

## 27. Addendum 20, 25 Nov 2026: the reasons not to log in, set against the risk of losing the messages

**What this covers.** Two things supplied after section 26, recorded as relayed.

*As relayed: the number expired on July 26, with an email stating that he had 30 days to reclaim it. It was from a paid plan.*

*As relayed: state clearly and specifically the reasons not to log in to the texting account. Put simply, he is losing his mind waiting. If there is a glimmer of hope, however small, he is looking for it. He has no plan to delete, alter or modify any message or thread. Seeing, at a minimum, that the April 30 thread was removed before May 1, or before the May 1 chat began, would reassure him that it was a mistake. Waiting for lawyers who have already missed many details to see this through is uncomfortable. On the other side, seeing that the April 30 thread was still there might bring about a psychotic break. For now only the undercover log has been entered, and that has been the working position. By reasons he means such things as strife with his military defense counsel, acknowledging that he did not follow instructions, though for a good reason, the emotional toll, and the eventual record of a new login. These are to be weighed against the prospect that the messages become inaccessible when the account is deactivated.*

I read the first item to mean that a paid plan had kept the number, and that the number expired on 26 July 2026. If either reading is wrong, the dates in 27.1 change. I have not seen the email, the plan's records or the account. I could not open the provider's sites from here, so what follows about its pages rests on what a web search returns from them (section 14). Statements of law are my understanding, for counsel to confirm. This section explains counsel's advice and tests it. It does not replace it.

**Short version**

- **The answer is still not to log in, and the reason is not that looking is wrong.** He means to change nothing, and I take that as true. A look that changes nothing is not tampering (section 26.2). The reasons are about what a look can and cannot do.
- **It cannot give him what he hopes for.** A thread that begins at 15:49 on May 1 would show that the April 30 lines are not in his list now. It would not show when they went. From then on the government could point to the one person known to have been in the account since the arrest.
- **It can give him what he fears, outright.** Both days in the list would mean that the thread was never deleted. He would learn that alone, on a holiday, with nobody there to say what it does and does not mean.
- **It may well give him nothing.** After nearly seven months, with the number expired since July, an empty list or a prompt for a new number may be all that there is. That ends nothing.
- **It may not save the messages.** The provider's pages do not say that a login resets its six months. What they do say is that keeping a number takes a message sent or a call made, and not a look. If the text has already been deleted, a login saves nothing. If the deletion is months away, there is time to do this properly. A login could help only if the deletion is days away, and then only if things that I could not confirm go its way. A written request to preserve the account addresses the same risk without those costs, though it cannot promise that the provider acts in time.
- **The costs are specific:** a reinstall, on record, of a second-number texting app while he awaits trial; a login record; a changed account; a harder relationship with the lawyer who has to stand behind whatever is found; and knowledge that cannot be unlearned.
- **What he fears would not be new evidence for the government, and it would not show what he knew.** The government's log already shows the two days as one conversation, and this analysis has worked from that possibility since section 12. Both days in his list would close one possibility that helps him, and that would be a real loss. It would not show what he read or believed, which the government still has to prove.
- **What he wants can be asked for in another form:** the same look, made by an examiner or in counsel's office, and recorded from start to finish. He would see what is there. He would not be alone when he saw it, and what he saw could be used. He should ask for it in writing, with a date for an answer (27.6). I cannot promise that counsel will agree, or how soon.
- **The distress needs help now, and that does not have to wait for any lawyer.** 27.7 lists who can help. If it becomes an emergency, the Military Crisis Line is 988, then 1.

### 27.1 The new detail: the paid plan, and the expiry of the number

*As relayed: a paid plan kept the number. The number expired on July 26, and an email gave him 30 days to reclaim it.*

1. **What the provider says about its paid plan.** Its support page lists as one benefit: "Keep your TextFree number even if your account is inactive." Another of its pages says that the plan "prevents your TextFree phone number from expiring after 30 days due to inactivity." The support page adds: "Uninstalling TextFree does not cancel your subscription." Google's help page says the same of a subscription bought through the Play Store: "When you uninstall the app, your subscription won't cancel."
2. **What that explains.** The number was the same on April 30 and May 1, and it lasted through each removal of the app. It also outlived the arrest by nearly three months. The plan went on after the app came off the phone, until it was cancelled or a payment failed. I do not know which, or when.
3. **The number is gone, and a login would not bring it back.** The 30 days to reclaim it ended about 25 August. The provider's page says that if the app does not offer an expired number "when it prompts you to choose a new one," the number "is not available to use at this time." Its page on changing numbers says that to "retrieve a number you had previously" a user should contact support. So section 25.4 was right that a login would meet a prompt for a new number. By now the old number may belong to someone else.
4. **What it does to the dates in section 26.4.** That section counted the provider's six months from May 1, or from 30 days later, and said that a paid plan might change the count. The plan does not settle it. The provider's own words are that the plan keeps the number "even if your account is inactive." On those words an account can be inactive while the plan runs. So the six months may have started in May all the same.

| If the account became "inactive" | The six months end about |
|---|---|
| At the last use, on May 1 | 1 November 2026. That has passed |
| 30 days after the last use | 30 November 2026 |
| When the plan ended, if that was 30 days before the number expired | 26 December 2026 |
| When the number expired, on July 26 | 26 January 2027 |
| When the 30 days to reclaim it ran out | 25 February 2027 |

I cannot tell which row applies, or whether the provider deletes on the day. The new detail adds the later dates. It does not remove the earlier ones.
5. **What it does establish.** On July 26 the provider was still writing to him about the number. That dates the expiry. It does not show what the provider held then, or holds now.
6. **The plan and the emails are records.**
   - If the plan was bought through the Play Store, the order history there and the receipts in his mail should show when it began, each payment, and when and how it ended. The date it began helps to show how long he had had this number, though a number can be changed while a plan runs.
   - How it ended matters. If he cancelled it after May 1, that was something done about the account after the arrest. It is not a login, and it is an ordinary thing to do. Counsel should still know of it before anyone says that the account has been left alone since the arrest (section 25.2).
   - The provider's emails date the expiry. He should keep them, pass them to counsel as they are, and follow no link in them (section 26.4).
   - Nothing in the order history or the mailbox should be deleted (section 13.7).
7. **It may be argued both ways on the habit.** A plan that holds the number fits long use of one number, and breaks of more than 30 days. The short cycle of removing and reinstalling the app did not need it, because on the provider's pages a number lasts 30 days without use in any case (section 14.2). The government may say that the plan shows that removing the app did not mean giving the number up, and may use that against any reading of the removal at 17:02 as a change of mind (section 24.2). An inference of that kind has to allow for the way subscriptions work. One that renews by itself goes on until someone cancels it, whatever he intended.

### 27.2 What a look could show

| What he would see | What it would mean | What it would not do |
|---|---|---|
| An empty list, or only a prompt to choose a new number | That the history is no longer shown to him | Tell him anything about April 30, or end the waiting |
| Her thread with both days in it | That it was never deleted | Show that he read the April 30 lines on May 1 |
| Her thread beginning at 15:49 on May 1 | That the April 30 lines are not in his list now | Show when they went, or prove anything that the provider's records would not prove better |

1. **The first is a real possibility, and after this long it may be the most likely.** Nearly seven months have passed, the number has expired, and the provider warns that history "may not be available after any long period of inactivity" (section 25.1). The sources do not let me put odds on any of the three. He has since said that, in his experience, the threads remain when the number changes, which makes an empty list less likely than this point suggests (section 28.1).
2. **Between the other two, I cannot say that the one he hopes for is the likelier.** He does not recall deleting the thread. He has said that it was possibly deleted. He has also said that on the afternoon of May 1 he was worried about texting her number by accident, which suggests that her number was still in the app at about 14:00 (section 25.2). None of that settles it.
3. **Only the second can be settled by looking.** Both days in the list means that the thread was never deleted. The third still needs the provider's records to show when the lines went, and those records can be asked for without his looking (section 25.4).

### 27.3 The reasons not to log in

**What a look cannot do**

1. **It cannot prove the thing he hopes to see.** A list shows what is there today. It does not show what was there at 15:49 on May 1 (section 25.1).
2. **It could turn the best finding against him.** If the April 30 lines are gone, and the provider kept no record of when they went, the argument that they went before the arrest rests on nobody having been in the account since (section 25.2). After a login by him, the government need not prove that he removed them in November. It need only say that he could have. He intends nothing of the kind. The record would not show his intent. It would show the login. If the provider did record the time, his login changes little on this point, and nobody yet knows which it is. This point was put too strongly, and section 28.1 corrects it.
3. **It may not keep the messages from being lost.** Whether a login resets the provider's clock is not known. 27.5 sets this out.

**What it would put on the record**

4. **A reinstall.** The first thing the records would show is not that he looked at one thread. It is that, with charges pending, he reinstalled the second-number texting app that the charges are about. An install from the Play Store is normally recorded in his Google account (section 25.4). He would have a reason. The record does not carry the reason.
5. **A login,** probably with its time, the device and the network. The provider's policy gives "2 years account activity," and records of that kind can be obtained later by subpoena (section 26.6). What exactly the provider logs is not confirmed.
6. **A changed account.** There would be a prompt for a new number, and he might have to take one in order to see anything. Messages that reached the account after 17:02 on May 1 may be fetched, and the provider's record of whether they were delivered or read may change. I could not confirm how the app behaves (section 26.2).
7. **Possibly a breach of a condition.** If a condition of his bond in Texas, or an order from his command, limits what he may install or use, a reinstall could breach it. Only he and his lawyers know whether there is one. It has to be checked before anything else, because the consequence would be immediate.
8. **The risk of a message.** If the list still holds anything, it holds a police number and the numbers of women he should not contact. A message sent by accident is the thing he says he feared on May 1 (section 25.4).

**What it would do with counsel**

9. **He would have to tell counsel, and at once.** If he did not, counsel might tell the government or the court that the account has been left alone since the arrest, and the provider's record would show otherwise. That would damage counsel's standing with the court, and his own.
10. **Counsel could not vouch for how it was made.** A capture that counsel arranged comes with a witness other than the accused. One that he made alone may be harder to prove and to date, and the natural witness to it is him (section 25.4). It would not be unusable for that reason alone.
11. **It may make counsel think twice about relying on the account.** A login does not stop counsel from asking for the provider's records. But once those records would show a login in November, counsel has to weigh that before putting them forward.
12. **It weakens his position in the disagreement.** As things are, the record shows that he raised preservation, and when. If the messages are lost, that may matter. The rule on lost evidence asks, among other things, whether the loss was "the fault of or could have been prevented by the requesting party" (section 26.4). A login against advice would not decide that question. It would give the government something to say about it, and counsel something to explain.
13. **It would not end the representation.** As far as I know, detailed counsel carries on. A login against advice could strain how the two of them work together, at the point when he most needs to be heard. He would still be entitled to be kept informed about his case (27.6).

**How "for a good reason" would be heard**

14. **By counsel,** as the client doing what he was advised not to do. The reason would be understood. The act would still be the thing counsel had to deal with.
15. **By a prosecutor, if it ever became relevant.** If he testifies, he can be asked whether he has opened the account since the arrest (section 25.8), and the answer would be yes. A login against advice would prove nothing about May 1. A prosecutor might still try to use it to show how he acts under pressure, which is close to how the government reads that day (section 3). Whether that would be allowed is for counsel.
16. **By himself.** A good reason does not change what a look can show.

**What it would do to him**

17. **The result he fears would come with nobody there.** He has said what it might do to him. This week it would come on a holiday, with his lawyers out of reach, and with no one to tell him what 27.7 says about what it would and would not mean.
18. **An empty list would relieve nothing.** It would leave him where he is, with a login on record and a new fear that the text is gone.
19. **Even the result he hopes for would not end the waiting.** It would still have to be dated, and that needs the provider's records. He would go on waiting for the same lawyers, with something to explain to them.
20. **It cannot be undone.** A login may leave a record that he cannot remove and that could later be obtained. What he sees cannot be unseen. If he saw both days in the list, "possibly deleted" would no longer be an answer that he could honestly give (section 25.4).
21. **It would be a decision made in order to stop a feeling.** That is not a criticism. The distress is real, and it needs help now (27.7). The decision about the evidence is a separate thing. It can wait for a day when there is someone to ask, and the distress should not wait at all.

### 27.4 What can be said for looking

These are real, and they should be stated as plainly as the reasons against.

1. **It is his account.** I know of no rule against his looking at it, subject to any condition or order (27.3, point 7). A look that changes nothing is not tampering (section 26.2).
2. **If her thread is there, he would know at once what his list holds today.** A thread with only May 1 in it would still leave open when the earlier lines went.
3. **A thread that began at 15:49 on May 1 would mean more to him than it would prove.** He knows what he has and has not done with the account since the arrest. A fact-finder does not. It would also give him something to press counsel with.
4. **His own careful reading has already found what others missed.** As relayed, it was he who saw the two seconds (section 19.1).
5. **Waiting has a cost.** If the text is lost while he waits, that cannot be undone either.
6. **His own copy, made before a deletion, would be better than no copy.**

**Why the balance still comes down against**

1. Points 2 and 3 need something to be there, and need it to be the result he hopes for. It may be that nothing is there. If something is, I cannot say that the result he hopes for is the likelier one (27.2).
2. Points 5 and 6 are about the risk of loss. A look could do more about that risk than a written request in one row of the table in 27.5, and only if several things that are not known go its way. In the other three rows it does nothing that a request does not do better.
3. Point 4 is a reason to press counsel harder, and in writing. It is not a reason to do alone the one thing whose value depends on a witness.

### 27.5 Set against the risk of losing the messages

| If the position is | What a login by him would do now | What a written request to preserve would do |
|---|---|---|
| The text has already been deleted | Nothing for the text. All of the costs in 27.3 | Nothing for the text. It records that he asked, and it finds out |
| The text is still there, and due to go within days | It might keep the text, if a login alone resets the provider's clock, which is not known. It would let him make his own copy | It might keep the text, if the provider acts on it in time, which cannot be promised. A request made by the government binds the provider |
| The text is still there, and not due to go for weeks or months | Nothing that could not be done properly in the time left. How much time that is, nobody knows | It keeps the text if the provider acts on it, and there may be time to follow it up |
| The government already obtained the account's records | Nothing | The text is not lost. Counsel should confirm what the records cover |

1. **A login is the better course in one row at most,** and there only if things that I could not confirm go its way.
2. **On the provider's pages, keeping a number takes use, and a look is not use.** Its instruction is: "Send a text or place an outgoing phone call to a non-TextFree contact within 30 days." That is about numbers. Whether the six months for message content work in the same way, the pages I could read do not say. If they do, keeping the account alive would take a new number, and a message or a call from it. That is using the app again, and it is far more than the look he has in mind. It is a question to put to the provider through counsel, and not one for him to test. Section 28.3 takes up his suggestion of a text sent to keep the account active.
3. **The strongest argument for a look is the copy.** If the text is there and about to go, a copy made by him is worth more than nothing. It is worth less than the same copy made in front of counsel or by an examiner (27.3, points 2 and 10).
4. **So the choice is narrower than it feels.** Only in the second row is his own copy tonight set against a copy made properly some days later, with the risk that the text goes in between. I cannot promise that counsel will act within days, or that the provider will. 27.6 is about how to press for it.
5. **A request to preserve needs no login.** Section 26.4 lists the forms it can take: a letter from either of his lawyers, a request from the government, which binds, and as a last resort a short written request of his own. That last resort has since been withdrawn unless counsel agrees to it (section 32.5).
6. **If the text is lost in spite of a dated request,** the rule on lost evidence may be worth raising (section 26.4). Relief under it depends on how important the evidence is, on whether there is a substitute, and on whether the defense could have prevented the loss. Whether it would apply is for counsel.
7. **This analysis cannot tell him that waiting is free.** It is not. It can tell him that a look alone is an uncertain remedy for the thing he is afraid of losing, and that a request which needs no login can be started today.

### 27.6 The same look, with its value kept

1. **What to ask counsel for.** A capture of the account by an examiner working for counsel or, failing that, in counsel's office with counsel or a paralegal present. It would be made on a device that has never held the app, and recorded on video from before the app is installed until after it is closed. He would give written consent. Nothing would be sent and nothing deleted, and the state of the account would be noted before and after (section 25.3).
2. **It would give him what he wants.** He would see what is there, on that day.
3. **He would not be alone when he saw it.** Someone would be there to say what it means for the case.
4. **What is found could be used.** If it hurts, it can usually stay inside the defense (section 25.3).
5. **It still leaves a login on the provider's record.** The difference is that counsel arranged it and someone other than the accused can describe it. A production by the provider leaves no login at all, which is why section 25.3 puts it first. Both can be asked for.
6. **How to ask.** In writing, on one page, with the line from the provider's policy and its email about the number attached. Three requests: a letter to the provider to preserve the account; whether the government already has the provider's records; and a recorded capture. Then a named day for an answer, yes or no, with the reason if it is no.
7. **He can ask for the reason.** The Army's rules of professional conduct require a lawyer to "keep the client reasonably informed about the status of the matter" and to "promptly comply with reasonable requests from the client for information" (AR 27-26, Rule 1.4). Counsel may have a reason that he has not been told: discovery that is on its way, or a judgment that no login of any kind should be made yet. A reason he can weigh is better than an instruction he cannot.
8. **If no answer comes by that day.**
   - His Texas counsel can send a letter to the provider for the Texas case, without waiting for anyone.
   - The Army's Trial Defense Service says of its own structure: "The Senior Defense Counsel is the direct supervisor of all Trial Defense Counsel within a field office." Asking that officer for help is an ordinary step. It is better to tell his own counsel first that he means to.
   - The statute gives him two other choices. He "may be represented by civilian counsel if provided by him," or "by military counsel of his own selection if that counsel is reasonably available" (Article 38(b), UCMJ). Whether either is practical, and what it would cost in time, I cannot say.
   - *Withdrawn since, unless counsel agrees to it (section 32.5).* The short written request of his own to the provider, described in section 26.4, involves no login and stays open throughout. That was written without her instruction in front of me.
9. **If the answer is no, or there is none.** A refusal, or a day that passes without an answer, does not change the reasons in 27.3, and it is not a reason to open the account alone. If counsel refuses, he should ask for the reason and weigh it, because it may be one that this analysis cannot see. If nobody answers, the steps in point 8 are still open. The text may be at risk in the meantime, which is the reason to take them at once. The decision is his, and nothing written here can make it for him. The answer of this analysis stays the same: not alone. If it has already happened, section 25.4 says what to do: stop, change nothing, and tell counsel at once and exactly.

### 27.7 The waiting

1. **What he describes is serious in its own right.** Nearly seven months of waiting for this to be decided would wear on anyone. It is not only a legal problem, and the lawyers are not the only people who can help with it. Getting that help does not have to wait for any lawyer's answer.
2. **Who can help, and what is protected.**
   - **The Military Crisis Line.** Military OneSource describes it as "a free, confidential resource for all service members," with counselors "available 24/7, 365." The number is 988, then 1, or a text to 838255. It answers on a holiday, and it is there for a night that is too much.
   - **An emergency.** If he cannot keep himself safe, or fears that he might harm himself or anyone else, or feels that he is losing touch with what is real, that is an emergency. He should call 911 or go to the nearest emergency room, and tell someone who can stay with him.
   - **A chaplain.** The military rule protects "a confidential communication by the person to a clergy member or to a clergy member's assistant, if such communication is made either as a formal act of religion or as a matter of conscience" (M.R.E. 503). As I understand it, Texas has a rule of the same kind (Tex. R. Evid. 505).
   - **A licensed mental health professional.** He should seek that help now, and he does not need counsel's leave to do it. How he is can be said freely. The facts of the case are a different matter. The military privilege for what is said in treatment has exceptions (section 18.6), and as I understand the Texas rules there is no physician-patient privilege in a criminal case (Tex. R. Evid. 509(b)). So he can ask the clinician what is kept confidential, and ask counsel what is protected in each court. Neither question should delay urgent care. A look of any kind, including one made in counsel's office, is better left until he has that support.
3. **What is not protected.** Friends and family can be told how he is, and can be asked to keep him company over the holiday. They should not be told the facts of the case, because nothing said to them is privileged. This document is not privileged either (section 11.5). What he hopes or fears the evidence will show is better said to counsel or a chaplain than written here.
4. **What the result he fears would and would not mean.**
   - It would close one possibility that helps him: that the April 30 lines were gone before he wrote on May 1. That would be a real loss. If it were later proved from the provider's records, it would support the government's argument that the refusal was in front of him (section 12.2).
   - It would not be new to this analysis, which first assumed it in section 12.2 and has kept it in view since. The government's log already shows the two days as one conversation, and the government already argues from that.
   - It would not show what he read or believed. A thread that was in the app is not, by that fact alone, a thread that was read. On his account he was writing to several numbers that afternoon, and he does not recall seeing his own words (sections 13.4 and 19.2). The government has to prove what he believed beyond a reasonable doubt, and it has to do that from circumstances (section 23.5).
   - The core of his defense does not depend on it. The one time he was told that she was a minor, he refused. Everything he was shown afterward was an adult. Age was not mentioned on May 1 (section 11.5).
5. **What stays, whatever the account shows.** These are points for the defense. They are not assurances of an outcome, and section 23 records that accused who refused and then came back have been convicted.
   - He gave up a finished arrangement 37 seconds after he was told her age (section 22.2).
   - As relayed, his Google activity records the image search that led to an adult's profile, and it was made before the arrest (sections 11.3 and 13.3).
   - "Yes" came two seconds after the line it is said to answer (section 19.1).
   - Nothing in the government's packet states what he believed on May 1. The government has to prove it from circumstances (section 22.12).
   - The provider may still hold the text, and its page says that a data request "may include deleted messages, if they are still available." On some readings of its policy there are weeks or months left (27.1).
   - The phone may still show what was open in the minutes before 15:49, depending on when its contents were copied (sections 25.2 and 26.1).

   None of these is certain. Each is real, and none needs him to open the account.

### 27.8 What to take to counsel

**Now**

1. **The written request in 27.6, point 6,** with a day named for the answer.
2. **The provider's email about the number,** and any others from it, as they are.
3. **The paid plan:** when it began, each payment, and when and how it ended (27.1).
4. **Any condition of his bond, or order from his command,** about phones, apps or accounts (27.3, point 7).
5. **That he is finding the wait very hard, and is thinking of looking.** Counsel should hear that from him, and before the event and not after it.

**Records to add.** These add to sections 25.9 and 26.8.

1. The Play Store's order history and the receipts for the plan.
2. The provider's emails about the number: any warning, the notice of expiry, and the offer to reclaim it.
3. From the provider: when the plan ended, when the number expired, whether and when the number was given to someone else, what "inactive" means in its policy, and from what day it counted.

### 27.9 What this changes

1. **The advice not to open the account alone stands,** and the reasons for it are now in one place (27.3).
2. **The strongest reason is not the login record.** It is that a look cannot date what it finds, and that his own login would become the answer to the finding he hopes for.
3. **The risk of losing the messages is real, and a look is an uncertain remedy for it.** The provider's pages do not say that a look would keep them. A written request to preserve needs no login, though it cannot promise that the provider acts in time.
4. **The dates in section 26.4 are wider than they were.** As relayed, a paid plan kept the number until 26 July 2026. Later dates are now possible. The earlier ones are not excluded. Notes have been added to sections 14.2, 25.1 and 26.4.
5. **The plan is a new record, and it cuts both ways** (27.1).
6. **Waiting is no longer the only thing asked of him.** There is a request to make, a day to name, and people to take it to (27.6).
7. **What he fears would close one possibility that helps him.** It would not be new to this analysis, and it would not show what he read or believed (27.7).
8. **His own state matters for its own sake, and help with it should not wait.** 27.7 lists who can help and what is protected.
9. **Section 1 has been updated, and section 7 has a new item, 16.**
10. **The order of work is unchanged:** the request to preserve the account, then the video, the forensic report, and the account.

## 28. Addendum 21, 25 Nov 2026: his replies to section 27, and what a login does and does not change

**What this covers.** Replies supplied after section 27, recorded as relayed.

*As relayed: on the point that his own login would let the government say that he removed the lines then: saying is not proving, and the prosecution is presumably capable of investigating. What is it that he hopes? It is true that a look may not save the messages, unless he sends a random text to keep the account active. Accounts are recognized by the email used to log in. On logging in there will predictably be a notice that the number expired, and a prompt to pick a new one. The messages will be there, as a moment of truth. As a regular user, when he changed the number in the app, the previous messages remained.*

*As relayed: so at most, metadata is updated by a new login. But metadata would also show the time and date at which the previous messages were received.*

I have not seen the account or the provider's records. The provider's page on changing a number, as a web search returns it, does not say what happens to earlier messages, so that point rests on his experience of the app. This section corrects section 27 where he is right. It does not change counsel's advice, and it does not replace it.

**Short version**

- **He is right on three points, and section 27 is corrected.** Saying is not proving. On his experience of the app, her thread is more likely to be visible than section 27 said. The time of a message is fixed when it arrives, and a login does not change it.
- **One gap is left in the last point.** The time at which a message arrived is not the time at which it was deleted. A deletion has a recorded time only if the provider logs deletions, and nobody yet knows whether it does.
- **A login changes more than metadata in two ways.** It needs the app reinstalled, which is recorded. It will probably need a new number chosen, which changes the account.
- **A random text is a different thing from a look, and it should not be sent.** It would be a new message from a new second number, to a person he does not know, with charges pending. Whether it would restart the provider's six months is not known.
- **What he hopes for is reassurance, and a look could give him that.** Section 27 answered as if he had asked for proof. A thread that begins on May 1 would tell him a good deal. It would show a court less.
- **He should not go in expecting it.** His own statements point both ways on whether the thread was deleted (28.4).
- **So the cost of a look to the evidence is smaller than section 27 made it, and most of it depends on how the look is made:** whether it is recorded from start to finish, whether anything is sent or changed, and whether counsel is told beforehand.
- **The reason that has not got smaller is the one about him.** He expects a moment of truth, and he has said what the wrong answer might do to him. That is not something to meet alone (28.6).

### 28.1 Three corrections

1. **"Saying isn't proving."** Section 27.3, point 2, said that after a login of his own the government "need only say that he could have" removed the lines. That was too strong.
   - The government has to prove what he believed, beyond a reasonable doubt. A thread that begins at 15:49 on May 1 is evidence on that question, however it is found, and the panel weighs it. A suggestion with nothing behind it does not cancel it. As I understand it, members are told that what counsel say in argument is not evidence.
   - The government can investigate, as he says. It can get the provider's records by warrant or order (section 26.3). If the provider logs deletions, its records settle when the lines went, and his login is beside the point. If it logs what is done in a session, its records would show one login and no deletion.
   - What is left of the point is narrower. Where the provider has no record of a deletion, the best way to date one was that nobody had been in the account since the arrest (section 25.2). After a login, that can no longer be said. What replaces it is that there was one login, in November, in which nothing was changed. That needs something to show it: the provider's record of the session, a recording of the look, or his own word.
   - An investigation also brings the government the whole account, with every other conversation in it (section 25.2).
   - So a login of his own would weaken the dating of a finding. It would not defeat it. How much it weakens it depends mostly on whether the look is recorded.
2. **An empty list is less likely than section 27.2 said.** That section called an empty list a real possibility, and perhaps the most likely. He knows the app, and I do not. On his account the threads belong to the login and not to the number, and they stayed when he changed numbers. If so, the expiry of the number does not by itself remove them. Two cautions remain.
