His experience is of an account in use, and this one has been left for nearly seven months. And the provider's warning about long inactivity, and its six months, still apply (section 26.4). So her thread is more likely to be there than section 27 said. I still cannot put odds on it.
3. **The time of a message is fixed.** He is right, and 28.2 sets out what follows and what does not.

### 28.2 What the times show, and the one thing they do not

1. **What is right.** Each message carries the time at which it was sent or received. The undercover log has those times to the second (section 22.2). The provider's privacy policy lists, among what it collects, "information about those messages (date, duration, recipients, size, attachments)" (section 26.3). A login does not rewrite any of that. If both days are in her thread, the April 30 lines carry April 30 times, and nobody could suggest that they arrived later.
2. **The gap.** A deletion is not a message. It has no time of its own unless the provider records one. The question in scenario A is when the April 30 lines went (section 25.2), and the times of the messages that remain do not answer it. Whether the provider logs deletions is still unknown. It is on the list of things to ask (section 25.9).
3. **What he would see is not the provider's metadata.** The app shows him the messages that are in his list, with their times. The provider's own records of the account are on its systems. A login does not show them to him, and getting them does not need a login. They are reached with his consent, or by process (section 25.7).
4. **What follows.** If the provider logs deletions, he is right in full. Its records would date the deletion whatever he does, and a look costs little on this point. If it does not, 28.1 applies. Nobody yet knows which.
5. **One record that a login might touch.** Messages that reached the account after 17:02 on May 1 have never been opened. The provider's record of when they reached a device, or were read, may change on a login (section 26.2). The times at which the provider received them would not.

### 28.3 What a login changes beyond a login record

1. **A reinstall.** It is normally recorded in his Google account (section 27.3). He would have a reason for it. The record does not carry the reason.
2. **A new number.** He expects a notice that the number expired and a prompt to choose another, and the provider's page describes the same thing (section 27.1). If the app shows nothing until a number is chosen, then looking means taking a new second number while he awaits trial. That is a change to the account, and a prosecutor could describe it in one sentence. I could not confirm whether the app lets a user see the threads without choosing.
3. **Perhaps the provider's record of when the account was last used.** That is the change he wants, because it might restart the six months. Whether it does is not known (section 27.5).
4. **A random text would be a different kind of act.** It is not a look, and it is not metadata. It would be a new message, sent from a new second number, to a person, with these charges pending. A number picked at random belongs to someone, and he would not know to whom. If the provider's six months can be stopped only by a message, that is a step for counsel to decide on and to arrange, including who receives it. Of everything he describes, this is the one step that should be ruled out now.
5. **A condition of his bond, or an order,** may forbid the app or a new number (section 27.3, point 7). He should read his conditions before anything else.

### 28.4 What he hopes for

*As relayed: what is it that he hopes?*

Section 27 said that a look "cannot give him what he hopes for." His own words were that seeing the April 30 thread gone before the May 1 chat would give "reassurance of mistake." Section 27 answered a question about proof. He was asking about reassurance. The two are different, and a look bears on them differently.

| What he would see | What it would tell him | What it would show a court |
|---|---|---|
| Her thread beginning at 15:49 on May 1 | A good deal. He knows that he has not been in the account since the arrest. So for him the April 30 lines went by 17:02 on May 1, unless the provider itself has dropped them since | That the lines are not in his list on the day of the look. When they went still has to be shown (28.1) |
| Her thread with both days in it | That it was never deleted | Nothing that the government's log does not already show |
| Nothing | Nothing | Nothing |

1. **A look could give him the reassurance he means.** Section 27 should have said so.
2. **It would not be complete, even for him.** It would not tell him whether the lines went before 15:49 or in the hour after, and he recalls no deletion at either time (section 15.2).
3. **He should not go in expecting it.** His own statements point both ways. He has said that deleting "seemed more effective than blocking," and that the thread was "possibly deleted" (section 15.1). He has also said that on the afternoon of May 1 he feared texting her number by accident, which suggests that her number was still in his list at about 14:00 (section 25.2). And on his own answers the other conversations from April 30 were still there (section 14.3). I cannot tell him which he will find.
4. **What the other answer would mean** is in section 27.7. It would close one possibility that helps him. It would not show what he read or believed, and it would give the government nothing that its own log does not already show.

### 28.5 What decides how much a look costs

Section 27.3 set out the costs as if they were fixed. Most of them depend on how a look is made. This is not advice to make one. Counsel's advice stands, and the answer of this analysis is still not alone. It is set out because the decision is his, and because the difference is large.

| | What raises the cost | What lowers it |
|---|---|---|
| Any condition of bond, or order | Not checked | Read first. If it forbids the app or a new number, the question stops there until counsel has dealt with it |
| Counsel | Told afterward, or not told | Told beforehand in writing, with the reason, and told the result at once |
| A record of the look | Only his word for what he did | One continuous recording, from before the app is installed until after it is closed |
| What is done in the account | A message sent, a thread deleted, a setting changed | Nothing sent, nothing deleted, nothing changed |
| What is kept | Screenshots chosen afterward | The whole recording, unedited, given to counsel and to nobody else |
| Who is there | Nobody | Someone who can stay with him. That person need not see the screen or be told the facts |

1. **A message to counsel beforehand changes what the act is.** A look that counsel was told of in writing, with the provider's policy and its dates attached, is a client acting to preserve evidence after asking for help. A look that counsel learns of later is a client who went around his lawyer. The message is privileged, and it gives counsel the chance to arrange the better form.
2. **The better form is still the one in section 27.6.** It has everything in the right-hand column, and a witness who is not the accused.
3. **None of this touches the reason in 28.6.**

### 28.6 The moment of truth

1. **He calls it a moment of truth, and if he is right about the app it would be one.** Section 27 said that a look might show nothing. On his account of the app it would more likely show one answer or the other.
2. **That raises the stakes of looking alone.** He has said, in section 27 and since, what the wrong answer might do to him. Nothing in this section makes that smaller.
3. **What the wrong answer would cost is one argument, and not the case.** The refusal within 37 seconds, the adult's profile and the absence of any mention of age on May 1 would all still be there (section 27.7). No result on that screen decides the case, and none is worth his life or his health.
4. **So before any look, in any form:** someone with him; the Military Crisis Line within reach, which is 988, then 1; and section 27.7 read beforehand.
5. **The holiday is the worst time for it.** His lawyers cannot be reached, and he would have the answer for days before anyone could tell him what it means.
6. **If the feeling is that he cannot wait another night, that feeling is the thing to get help with tonight** (section 27.7). In most cases the account will show the same thing next week. The exception is the earliest date in section 27.1, and the step to take against that date tonight is the message to counsel.

### 28.7 What to take to counsel

1. **Tonight, in writing:** the line from the provider's policy, its email about the number, the dates in section 27.1, and that he is thinking of opening the account in order to preserve it. Ask for the letter to the provider and for the recorded capture in section 27.6.
2. **What he knows of the app:** that the threads stay with the login when the number changes.
3. **His conditions of bond, and any order,** so far as they touch apps, numbers or accounts.
4. **Whether the provider logs deletions and sessions.** It decides how much any login matters (28.2).

### 28.8 What this changes

1. **Three corrections to section 27.** Saying is not proving, so a login of his own would weaken the dating of a finding and would not defeat it. Her thread is more likely to be visible than section 27.2 said. The times of messages are fixed, though no time records a deletion unless the provider logs one. Notes have been added to sections 25.4, 27.2, 27.3 and 27.5.
2. **The cost of a look to the evidence is smaller than section 27.3 made it,** and most of it depends on how the look is made (28.5).
3. **A random text is ruled out.** If a message is ever needed to keep the account alive, it is counsel's step.
4. **Reassurance and proof are different things.** A look could give him the first. It could not give a court the second.
5. **The main reason is now the one about him.** It is not a reason against ever knowing. It is a reason not to find out alone.
6. **Section 1 has been updated, and item 16 in section 7 has a note.**
7. **The order of work is unchanged:** the request to preserve the account, then the video, the forensic report, and the account.

## 29. Addendum 22, 25 Nov 2026: his doubts about his lawyers, and what "wait" does and does not cover

**What this covers.** A further message supplied after section 28, recorded as relayed and in general terms.

*As relayed, in general terms: he will be straightforward about the lawyers who say not to log in and to wait. He believes that his Texas firm is stringing the process along so as to go on being paid. The civilian prosecutor has reset the pretrial hearing four times, which he thinks is likely a tactic to let the Army prosecute. The reason given for waiting is the crime lab's review of the data, and the lab likely has a backlog. His military counsel is human, and he believes that counsel could not help being affected by news coverage of an operation that "targets minors." Counsel began by questioning him hard, and moved only when the timestamps and the other threads running at the same time came up, which Google could support. Whether from experience, like the near 100 percent conviction rate that this analysis shows, or from the lack of it, never having had an innocent client where most are guilty and usually plead, counsel seems to him to have reservations about going to trial.*

I have heard neither lawyer. I have not seen the fee agreement, the Texas court's record, or anything that either lawyer has done and not told him of. So this section cannot say whether his doubts are right. It can say how each could be tested, and what does not depend on them. The Texas statute, the Army's rules and the firm's site were read in extracts returned by a web search. *United States v. Stellato* was read in the passage quoted, in a public copy of the opinion. Statements of law are my understanding, for counsel to confirm. One caution comes first: this document is not privileged, and describing here what passes between him and his lawyers may put the privilege for it at risk (29.7).

**Short version**

- **The reasons for leaving the account closed do not depend on trusting either lawyer.** They are about what a look can show, what it puts on record, and what the answer might do to him (sections 27 and 28). They stand even if both lawyers are as he fears.
- **"Do not log in" and "wait" are two different pieces of advice.** The first is what I would expect any defense lawyer to say. The second is right for some things and not for others, and that is where to press.
- **Waiting for the crime lab does not preserve the provider's records.** The lab's work, as he describes it, is on the phone's data, and the app was off the phone. The provider's records are a separate source, and the provider's own period is running. A letter asking the provider to preserve them is short and waits on nothing. Asking for it, yes or no by a named day, is a fair thing to ask. Counsel should also confirm whether anyone has already asked.
- **Delay probably does not pay the Texas firm.** Its own site says that it charges "a true flat fee that includes trial." He can read his agreement tonight. If the fee is flat, a longer case earns the firm nothing more.
- **A slow Texas case may be in his interest, and four resets are ordinary.** The Texas statute is the harder one for him, because under it a statement of her age may be enough (section 22.8). He may be right that the prosecutor is letting the Army go first. That is the two prosecutors' choice, and not his firm's. Whether the delay helps him is for his two lawyers to work out together.
- **As he describes it, his military counsel moved when shown records.** That is the most useful thing in his message. A lawyer who questions a client hard may be testing the account as a prosecutor will. One who changes position on the evidence can be moved again, by the same means.
- **Whether to go to trial is his decision.** Counsel's part is to tell him the risk. And he cannot plead guilty to Spec 1 or 2 while he maintains that he did not believe she was a minor (section 11.5).
- **Section 23 does not show a conviction rate.** Almost all of its cases are appeals, and an appeal follows a conviction. An acquittal leaves no opinion (section 23.1).
- **The way to test a doubt is a short list of questions in writing, each with an answer that can be checked** (29.5). If the concerns are still unanswered after that, the next step is the supervising counsel, a second opinion or a change of lawyer. It is not acting alone on the evidence.
- **His safety comes before the case.** The Military Crisis Line answers tonight: 988, then 1.

### 29.1 What does not depend on the lawyers

1. **Sections 27 and 28 gave reasons, and none of them was that counsel said so.** A look cannot date what it finds. It needs a reinstall and probably a new number. It may be barred by a condition of his bond. And he has said what the wrong answer might do to him. Each of those is true whatever his lawyers' motives are.
2. **If the lawyers are as he fears, the case for a written record gets stronger.** A client who cannot rely on his lawyers needs proof of what he asked for and when. A written request with a date is that proof. A look made alone works the other way. It leaves him with something to explain to the same lawyers, and to any new one.
3. **Distrust is a reason to change who helps him. It is not a reason to have nobody.**
4. **His safety tonight comes before the case.** Feeling that nobody is on his side is hard to carry, and it is worst at night and on a holiday. The Military Crisis Line answers now, at 988, then 1, or by text to 838255, and he should use it. He should ask someone he trusts to stay with him. If he cannot keep himself safe, that is an emergency: 911, or the nearest emergency room (section 27.7).

### 29.2 The Texas firm

**The fee**

1. **Whether delay pays the firm depends on the fee agreement,** which I have not seen. The firm's own site says that it offers "a true flat fee that includes trial," and that it does not charge extra if a case goes to trial. If his agreement is of that kind, the firm is paid the same whether the case ends this month or next year. Monthly payments on a flat fee are installments of a fixed sum. They are not pay for time.
2. **A flat fee carries a different risk.** It rewards finishing sooner, and not later. The thing to watch for under a flat fee is pressure toward a quick plea, and he does not describe that.
3. **If the agreement charges by the hour or by the court setting,** his suspicion has more behind it, and the questions in 29.5 matter more.

**The resets**

1. **Four resets in seven months is ordinary,** as I understand Texas practice. A felony there needs an indictment by a grand jury, and settings are commonly reset while the State waits for its lab.
2. **Delay in Texas may be in his interest, and it has costs.**
   - For it: the Texas statute is harder for him than the military charges. Under it a second-degree felony can rest on her age having been "represented to the actor," without proof of what he believed (section 22.8). If the Army goes first, the first trial is the one in which the government has to prove what he believed (section 23.5). And if the court-martial ends first, the state prosecutor may see less reason to go on. I cannot promise that.
   - Against it: the Army going first keeps both prosecutions alive (point 3). The bond conditions, the cost and the strain go on. Evidence ages.
   - Whatever he says in one case can be used in the other (section 7), so the order matters to both lawyers.
   - Which way it comes out is for his two lawyers to work out together. It is a question to put to them. The delay does not by itself show good or bad faith.
3. **He may well be right that the prosecutor is letting the Army go first.** As I understand it, each of the two can prosecute, because a state and the federal government are separate sovereigns. As I understand Army policy, a soldier who has already been tried in a state court is ordinarily not court-martialed for the same act. So for the government as a whole, the Army going first keeps both cases alive. That is a choice made by the two prosecutors' offices. His firm cannot stop it.
4. **What the firm could do to force the pace, and why it might not.** If he has not been indicted, Texas law has a deadline. A prosecution "shall be dismissed and the bail discharged" if no indictment is presented by the later of two dates: the last day of the next term of the court after his release on bail, or "the 180th day after the date of commitment or admission to bail." A court can order otherwise "for good cause shown, supported by affidavit" (Code of Criminal Procedure art. 32.01). By my count the 180th day was about 28 October 2026. I have not checked the terms of the court, which may give a later date. As I understand it, a dismissal of that kind does not stop the State from charging him again, and a motion can prompt the State to indict. So a firm that has not filed one may be serving him and not itself. It is still a fair question to ask (29.5).

**The crime lab**

1. **As a reason not to settle the defense theory yet, it is sound.** Section 7 has said the same since the first draft: get the forensic report first.
2. **As a reason to do nothing else, it has gaps.**
   - **The provider.** The app was removed from the phone before the phone was taken, and removal normally takes the app's stored messages with it (section 26.3). The lab may find none of them. The provider's records are a separate source, and the provider's own period for keeping message content is running (section 27.1). Waiting for the lab does not preserve them. Counsel should confirm whether anyone has already asked the provider for them.
   - **The copy of the phone.** The lab's report and the copy of the phone's data are two things. If the data has been copied, a defense examiner may be able to work from the copy without waiting for the lab's report. If it has not been copied in nearly seven months, counsel should know why, because some of what a phone holds is cleared with age (section 26.1).
   - **His own records.** The Google account's activity, the profile as it stands and the plan's receipts are in his hands, and not the lab's (sections 13.7, 11.5 and 27.1).
3. **It tells this analysis a little that is new.** As relayed, the reason given for waiting is the crime lab's review of the data. That suggests that a state lab has, or is to have, the phone or its data, and that no report has reached the defense. It does not establish who holds the phone, whether its data has been copied, or whether a report exists. Those are the questions in section 26.1, and counsel should confirm each. He has since said that DPS has held the phone since May 1 (section 30.2).
4. **It may matter to the court-martial as well.** The Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces has said that "an object held by a state law enforcement agency is ordinarily not in the possession, custody, or control of military authorities." It went on to list situations, taken from the federal courts, in which evidence outside the prosecution's hands is still within its control. One is where "the evidence resides in another agency but was part of a joint investigation" (*United States v. Stellato*, 74 M.J. 473 (C.A.A.F. 2015)). Both agencies called this operation joint (section 22.7). So his military counsel may be able to ask the Army's prosecutor for the phone's data while the state lab still has it. Whether that applies here is for counsel.

### 29.3 His military counsel

1. **Hard questioning at the start.** A defense lawyer has to know the worst before the prosecutor shows it in court. One way to find it is to press the client as a prosecutor will, and from the client's chair that can feel like an accusation. It is also possible that counsel did start from an assumption of guilt. Lawyers form expectations as anyone does, and I do not know what counsel brought to his case. I cannot tell from here which it was.
2. **What can be seen is what counsel did next.** As relayed, counsel's position changed when he pointed to the timestamps and to the other conversations running at the same time. A lawyer whose mind was made up whatever the evidence would not have moved. That is some reason to think that counsel responds to records. It also shows how to move counsel again: one page for each point, with the exhibit and the times (section 7, item 11).
3. **The news coverage.** It is real, and section 22.9 treats it as something to ask the panel members about. Counsel may have seen it too. But counsel does not have to believe him in order to defend him well. What he needs from counsel is work that he can check: requests made, motions filed, an examiner asked for. 29.5 lists it.
4. **Reservations about a trial.** Telling a client the risk is part of counsel's job, and here the risk is real. The case he supplied himself ended in a sentence of 16 years (section 23.2). A lawyer who never spoke of that would be failing him. Reservations are advice, and the decision is his. The Army's rules for its lawyers say: "A lawyer shall abide by the client's well-informed and lawful decisions concerning case objectives, choice of counsel, forum, pleas, whether to testify, and settlements" (AR 27-26, Rule 1.2(a)).
5. **What reservations cannot turn into.** A military judge accepts a guilty plea only if the accused admits under oath the facts that make him guilty (section 11.5). While he maintains that he did not believe she was a minor, he cannot plead guilty to Spec 1 or 2, whatever counsel thinks of the odds. So the practical question for counsel is what result short of a trial counsel has in mind, and for which specifications. Spec 3 is different, because her age is no part of it (section 21.6).
6. **Experience.** How many contested cases of this kind counsel has tried, and how they ended, is a fair question, and lawyers expect it. The Trial Defense Service also has help for its own lawyers. Its page describes a Defense Counsel Assistance Program with experts "who provide timely, case-specific advice to defense counsel in response to requests for assistance." He can ask whether it has been consulted.
7. **"Near 100 percent."** That is not what section 23 shows, and the summaries in this analysis are partly to blame.
   - Almost every case found was an appeal, and an appeal follows a conviction. Section 23.1 says: "An acquittal cannot be appealed and leaves no opinion." So the search could find a case that ended the other way only by chance, as with the one trial known from a defense firm's report.
   - In all but one of the cases in which the accused refused and came back, he knew whom he was going back to (section 23.5). That is the fact his case disputes.
   - So those cases show how courts reason once a panel has convicted. They do not give his odds, and I do not know his odds.

### 29.4 What "wait" covers, and what it does not

| Item | Is waiting right | What can be done now |
|---|---|---|
| The government's forensic report on the phone | Yes. Only the lab can produce it | Ask whether and when the phone's data was copied, and ask for a copy for a defense examiner |
| Settling the defense theory | Yes (section 7) | Nothing. This part of the advice is sound |
| The provider's records | No. They are a separate source, and the provider's period is running | A letter to preserve, and a request made with his consent (section 26.4) |
| The Google account's activity, the profile, the plan's receipts | No | Preserve them now (sections 13.7, 11.5 and 27.1) |
| The interview video | No | Watch it with counsel (section 7) |
| A defense examiner | No | Ask whether one has been requested, and when (section 25.3) |
| His own look at the account | The account stays closed. Doubts about the lawyers do not change the reasons in sections 27 and 28 | Ask counsel for a recorded capture (section 27.6) |

### 29.5 Questions with answers that can be checked

These go to the lawyers in writing, with a day named for the answers. What comes back is a better guide to the work being done than an impression of attitude.

**To the Texas firm**

1. Has he been indicted? If not, has the time in art. 32.01 run, and what is the reason for not raising it?
2. Who asked for each of the four resets, and why? The court's record should show it.
3. Has the phone's data been copied, when, and by whom? Can the defense have a copy now?
4. Will the firm send the provider a letter to preserve the account this week? Yes or no.
5. When did the firm last speak to his military counsel?

The fee question he can answer himself, from the agreement.

**To his military counsel**

1. Has the provider been asked to preserve the account, by the defense or through the trial counsel?
2. Has the defense asked the trial counsel for the phone's data, and for any records from the provider? What came back?
3. Has a forensic examiner been requested for the defense? When, and with what result? As relayed since, one had been requested, and by 5 November the government had changed its recommendation, which had been to deny it. Whether the commanding general has approved it is still to be asked (section 32.7).
4. Have the charges been referred, and has he been arraigned? The rule says: "The accused shall be brought to trial within 120 days after the earlier of" certain events, of which the first is the preferral of charges. An accused is brought to trial "at the time of arraignment" (R.C.M. 707). Charges were preferred on 24 June 2026, so by my count the 120th day was 22 October 2026. As I understand the rule, delay approved by the convening authority or a military judge does not count, and the remedy is a dismissal that may leave the government free to charge him again. Counsel will know what delay has been approved, and at whose request. Section 7 has listed this since the first draft.
5. What result short of a trial does counsel have in mind, and for which specifications?
6. Has the Senior Defense Counsel or the assistance program been consulted?

**Reading the answers.** They can help him judge the work and the reasons for it. He should look for specific actions and explanations. He should not read motives into how fast a reply comes, least of all over a holiday. If the concern about preserving the account is still unanswered after that, the next step is the supervising counsel or a second opinion (29.6).

### 29.6 If trust cannot be repaired

1. **A second opinion.** Another lawyer can review the file and say whether the work is what it should be. As I understand it, what he tells a lawyer whom he consults for advice is privileged.
2. **In Texas** he can change firms. What that costs depends on the fee agreement.
3. **In the Army** the statute lets him retain civilian counsel, or ask for a particular military counsel if that officer is reasonably available. The Senior Defense Counsel supervises his detailed counsel and can be asked for help (section 27.6).
4. **What is not a remedy** is acting alone on the evidence. It leaves the lawyers he distrusts with a new problem that is his, and it hands a new lawyer the same problem on the first day.

### 29.7 A caution about this record

1. **What he and his lawyers say to each other in confidence is privileged.** So, as I understand it, is what he tells another lawyer whom he consults in confidence. The privilege can be lost by disclosure outside such a relationship. The military rule speaks of a person who "voluntarily discloses or consents to disclosure of any significant part of the matter or communication under such circumstances that it would be inappropriate to allow the claim of privilege," and adds that the rule "does not apply if the disclosure is itself a privileged communication" (M.R.E. 510(a)). Counsel should confirm where the line falls.
2. **This document is not privileged** (section 11.5). This section describes, in general terms, what has passed between him and his lawyers. A general summary may still put the privilege at risk. He should add no further detail here, and both lawyers should be asked to look at what is already written.
3. **The same goes for what he hopes or fears the evidence will show** (section 27.7).
4. **Counsel should be told that this document now contains these things.**

### 29.8 What this changes

1. **The reasons for leaving the account closed do not rest on the lawyers.** They are in sections 27 and 28.
2. **"Wait" has been sorted.** It is right for the lab's report and for settling the theory. It is not a reason to put off the letter to the provider, the copy of the phone's data, his own records or the video.
3. **Three new details are recorded, as relayed:** the reason given in Texas for waiting is the crime lab's review of the data; the Texas hearing has been reset four times; and his military counsel changed position when shown the records.
4. **One correction to how section 23 has been read.** It does not give a conviction rate. Section 1 has a note.
5. **Two dates to ask about:** the 120th day under R.C.M. 707, which by my count was 22 October 2026, and the Texas deadline for an indictment.
6. **Whether to go to trial is his decision.** Counsel's reservations are advice.
7. **His doubts may be right or wrong.** The answers to 29.5 will help him judge.
8. **His safety comes before any of this** (29.1).
9. **Section 1 has been updated, and section 7 has a new item, 17.**
10. **The order of work is unchanged:** the request to preserve the account, then the video, the forensic report, and the account.

## 30. Addendum 23, 25 Nov 2026: what a funded examiner can and cannot do, and the "hail mary"

**What this covers.** A clarification supplied after section 29, recorded as relayed and in general terms.

*As relayed, in general terms: to be clear, his military counsel is far from bad. She is competent and fierce. What he cannot help weighing is how much can be done within the limits of the process. A forensic examiner for the defense, paid for by the government, is requested after charges are referred, and the request apparently needs the commanding general's approval. That takes time, and meanwhile the provider may delete messages under its policy. If all that the long-awaited examiner will do is review metadata and say which messages arrived when and from where, that is weak. As he understands it, metadata may or may not contain the messages themselves, and while imaging a phone takes an hour or two, working through the fragments may take months. Hence the "hail mary" of a login. The phone has been in DPS custody since May 1.*

I have not seen the request for an examiner, the phone or any copy of its data. What follows about what a phone keeps is general. One part of it rests on a forensic researcher's published account from 2021, which I read in an extract. An examiner will know what applies to his phone. Statements of law are my understanding, for counsel to confirm.

**Short version**

- **A correction to section 29.** It tested doubts about his military counsel that he does not hold. As relayed, she is competent and fierce, and the limit is the process. Section 1 has a note.
- **The limit he describes is real,** and it fits the rule as I understand it (section 25.3). It applies to one thing: an examiner paid for by the government.
- **He is largely right about the phone.** The app was removed, so an examiner will probably not find the messages on it. What the phone can show is what surrounded them, and some of that bears on his account (30.2).
- **The messages are with the provider, if it still has them, and reaching the provider needs no examiner.** A letter asking it to preserve the account needs no funding, no commanding general and no referral. Either lawyer can send it without waiting for the examiner. A letter from the defense does not bind the provider, and section 26.4 describes the other routes that need no login. The slow thing and the urgent thing are different things.
- **A recorded capture of the account does not need the funded examiner either.** It needs counsel's agreement, and a witness who is not the accused.
- **A "hail mary" is thrown when the clock has run out and nothing else is left.** Neither is established. On his own dates the provider's period may run into the new year (section 27.1). And he has not said that the two quicker steps, the letter and the recorded capture, have been asked for and refused. A date coming near, or a refusal, would not change the reasons in sections 27 and 28.
- **So the next step is one specific request to a lawyer he trusts,** on one page, with a date (30.4). If she says no, her reason is worth hearing.
- **The strongest pull toward a login is the wish to know, more than the need to preserve.** He has said as much (section 28.4). That is human. It is also the part that falls hardest on him, which is why it should not be done tonight (section 28.6).
- **His safety still comes first** (section 29.1).

### 30.1 What waits for the funded examiner, and what does not

| Step | Needs the funded examiner | Needs the commanding general | Who can do it |
|---|---|---|---|
| A letter asking the provider to preserve the account | No | No | Either lawyer (section 26.4) |
| Asking the Army's prosecutor for a binding preservation request, and whether the government already has the provider's records | No | No | His military counsel. It tells the government what the defense wants kept (section 26.4) |
| A request to the provider for his own records, with his written consent | No | No | Either lawyer. How long the provider takes is not known (section 25.7) |
| A recorded capture of the account | No | No | Counsel's office, if counsel agrees, with a witness who is not the accused (section 27.6) |
| Asking when and how DPS copied the phone, and for a copy of that data | No | No | Either lawyer (section 29.2) |
| Keeping his own Google records | No | No | Him, as counsel directs (section 13.7) |
| Examining the copy of the phone, testing the app, and reading the provider's records as an expert | Yes | Yes, as relayed | The examiner, once approved |

1. **Only the last row waits for the process he describes.** Every step that guards against the provider's deletion is in the rows above it. None of them is certain to work, and each can be started without the examiner.
2. **Who could witness a capture.** A lawyer who watches may become a witness, which is why section 27.6 suggests a paralegal. A public encyclopedia entry says that defense investigators are assigned to the regions of the Trial Defense Service. I could not confirm that from the Army's own page. She will know who is available, and whether she thinks a capture wise at all.
3. **If the request for an examiner is refused,** the rule lets it be renewed before the military judge, as I read it (section 25.3). As relayed since, by 5 November the government had changed its recommendation, which had been to deny the request, and the request was with the commanding general (section 32.7).

### 30.2 What an examiner can and cannot get from the phone

1. **Probably not the messages.** Removing an app normally removes what it stored (section 26.2). On a modern phone, data removed in that way is rarely recoverable, as I understand it. An examiner will say whether this phone is an exception.
2. **"Metadata" and "content" are different things, and they sit in different places.** Content is the words. Metadata is who wrote to whom, and when. On the phone, after the removal, the app's own files probably hold neither. At the provider both exist for as long as it keeps them, and it gives a different period for each (sections 26.3 and 26.4).
3. **What the phone can still show** is what surrounded the messages:
   - when the apps were installed and removed, to the second (section 24.7);
   - which page or app was open in the minutes before 15:49, if that record survived (section 26.1);
   - the image search and the views of the profile, with their times (section 13.7).
4. **One lead that bears on his own question.** A forensic researcher wrote in 2021 that the Google keyboard kept a cache of what the user typed, with "the application in focus, its field name (where input was sent), the timestamp of event." The same account says that this "can possibly show you data that was typed by the user on an app that is now deleted." If his phone used that keyboard, if the cache still worked in that way in 2026, and if DPS made a full copy of the phone soon enough, the copy might show what he typed on May 1, in which app and into which field. That could bear on two open questions. One is whether he typed her number in as a recipient at 15:49, which would suggest that he did not open her thread from his list (section 15.2). The other is what he typed at about 16:00 (section 25.5). The same account says that deletions are not tracked, so what was typed is not always what was sent. Every one of those conditions is uncertain, and I do not know how long such a cache lasts. A cache of that kind could also show things that do not help him. It is a lead for the examiner, and nothing should rest on it.
5. **Why the date of the copy matters now.** As relayed, DPS has had the phone since May 1. Records like these are kept for a limited time (section 26.1). If DPS copied the phone in May, they may be in the copy. If the phone has not been copied, they may be gone. When and how it was copied is a question that either lawyer can ask now. It does not wait for the examiner.
6. **Metadata is not weak for every purpose.** The provider's times could show when her line reached his phone and when his "Yes" left it, which is the two seconds behind Spec 1 (section 19.1). A list of the numbers his account wrote to around 15:49 would bear on his account of that afternoon (section 26.3).
7. **What only the provider can help with** is the thing he most wants to know: whether her April 30 thread was still in his list on May 1. Even the provider's records may not settle it. The provider may hold messages that he deleted, so the answer may need its record of deletions, if it keeps one, and tests of the app (sections 25.1 and 25.2). That is still the reason for the letter to come first. Without it there may be nothing left to examine.

### 30.3 The "hail mary," tested

1. **Is the clock at zero?** Not on what is known. The provider's six months may have run from May. In that case the text is already gone, or goes within days. They may instead run from the end of the plan, or from the expiry of the number. In that case they end between late December and late February (section 27.1). A login now would matter only in the first case, and only if the text is still there.
2. **Is nothing else left?** He has not said that she has been asked for the letter or for a recorded capture. If she has not been asked, the first step is to ask. If she has refused, her reason matters, and 30.4 asks for it.
3. **Would a login do what is hoped?** It would let him see, and copy, whatever is there. Whether it would stop a deletion is not known (section 27.5).
4. **A hail mary costs nothing if it fails.** This would cost something whether it failed or succeeded. Sections 27.3 and 28.3 list the costs to the case. Section 28.6 names the cost to him.
5. **So it is not a last play, and a date coming near would not make it one.** It is the step that feels fastest. The provider's date is not known, and that makes a request to preserve the account, sent by counsel, the urgent thing. A refusal, a delay or an approaching date does not change the reasons in sections 27 and 28. The account stays closed, and the way to it runs through counsel.

### 30.4 What to put in front of her

One page, in writing, sent tonight. She may not read it until after the holiday. It is dated when it is sent, and that is what matters. As I understand it, a note to counsel is privileged, and this document is not (section 29.7).

1. **The provider's policy,** in its own words: message content is kept "Indefinitely for active account and 6 months for inactive accounts." With it, the dates in section 27.1 and the provider's email about the number.
2. **Three requests that need no funding:** a letter to the provider to preserve the account; a question to the trial counsel, whether the government has the provider's records or has asked for them to be kept; and a recorded capture of the account, arranged through her.
3. **Two questions about the phone:** when and how DPS copied it, and whether the defense can have the copy.
4. **A day for an answer.** If the answer to any of it is no, the reason.
5. **How he is, said plainly.** She should hear from him how bad the waiting has become, including what he has said in the last day about what a wrong answer might do to him, and that he has thought of opening the account himself. A fierce lawyer would want to hear that before anything happens, and not afterward. Telling her does not replace help tonight, and help should not wait for her answer: someone he trusts with him, the Military Crisis Line at 988, then 1, and 911 or an emergency room if he cannot keep himself safe (section 29.1).

### 30.5 What this changes

1. **A correction to section 29.** His military counsel is not in doubt. The limit is the process for a government-funded examiner.
2. **That process governs one step. The urgent steps are outside it** (30.1).
3. **One more detail is recorded, as relayed:** DPS has held the phone since May 1. Sections 26.1 and 29.2 have notes.
4. **He is largely right that the phone will not yield the messages.** The provider is the source for those, if it still has them. The phone may still show what he typed and where, if it was copied fully and early, which is not known (30.2).
5. **A login is not a last play, and a date coming near would not make it one** (30.3). The reasons in sections 27 and 28 stand, and the account stays closed.
6. **Section 1 has been updated, and section 7 has a new item, 18.**
7. **The order of work is unchanged:** the request to preserve the account, then the video, the forensic report, and the account.

## 31. Addendum 24, 26 Nov 2026: a pattern of arranging, driving and not meeting, and what it would be worth

**What this covers.** Two questions supplied after section 30, recorded as relayed.

*As relayed: on the compulsion issue, where he still drives to the location although he knows that a scam is probable: if a habit can be proved with other adults (texting, driving, showing up, but not actually meeting), what value does that have?*

*As relayed: now consider the possibility that he may do the same with men on a dating app for men (texting, driving, showing up, but not actually meeting).*

The second is put as a possibility, and this section treats it as one. It records nothing about him as a fact. I have seen no record of any earlier occasion of either kind. Two decisions are used here that earlier sections did not have: *Hickerson* and *Valle*. I read the passages quoted from each in a public copy of the opinions, and not the whole of either. The other new cases, and the rules, were read in extracts returned by a web search. Statements of law are my understanding, for counsel to confirm. This is an account of what such records would be worth. It is not a suggestion about what to say, and it is open to him only if it is true.

**Short version**

- **If the records show it, this would be the first evidence from outside the day itself on the element of Spec 3 that section 21 ranked least proved:** a firm intent to go through with a sexual act. It would give Spec 2 a second answer, and one that does not depend on her age.
- **It would also weaken three inferences about her age.** The government reads his nervousness, the room he did not go to, and the thought of "just giving money and leaving" as signs that he knew she was someone he should not touch. A record of leaving adults in the same way answers that.
- **What counts is narrow.** Only an occasion on which the meeting was there to be had, and he left of his own accord. An occasion that fell through because nobody came, or because it was a scam, shows nothing about his own will, and the government will say that he meant to go through with it. An occasion that ended in a meeting cuts the other way.
- **So the pattern is mixed, on his own account,** because he has said that he used escorts for years. The questions are the proportion, and what separates the two kinds. If what separates them is that he goes in when she proves real, that is an intent with a condition, and the government will say that on May 1 the condition had been met.
- **The law's answer to a man who loses his nerve is that intent is judged when he acts.** So the pattern has to be evidence of what he intends as he drives, and not only of what he does at the door.
- **Not meaning to meet is no answer to Spec 1,** which is about the words he sent. The pattern helps there only through the inferences about her age. It probably does nothing in Texas, where the offer is the offense, and where each earlier agreement on a price could be charged as another.
- **Proving it invites the government to bring in the other occasions,** including those that ended in a meeting. Each is an act the government could use, and some could be charged.
- **Getting it before a panel is not simple.** It is unlikely to qualify as a "habit" under the rule. It has a better chance as evidence on intent, and through an evaluator.
- **On the possibility he raises:** occasions of that kind could be the cleanest of all, because money, the price and the law would be out of the comparison. The cost is of a different kind. It is a disclosure about himself, made in a public trial and perhaps in two, and it cannot be taken back. That decision is his alone. It does not have to be made now, and it can be explored first inside the privilege (31.6).
- **The government may already hold some of this.** If either kind of occasion left traces on the seized phone, they may be in the government's copy of it.
- **Nothing should be deleted, and nobody should be contacted.** The details belong with counsel, in person, and not here.

### 31.1 What the pattern would have to be

1. **A sequence, on dated occasions.** Section 17.3 said that a routine is shown by the same sequence on several dates. Here the sequence is: a meeting arranged by text, the drive, the arrival, and a departure with no meeting.
2. **Earlier occasions are of three kinds, and only one helps him.**

| Kind of occasion | What it shows | Who gains |
|---|---|---|
| He arranged it, arrived, and the meeting took place | That when he drives to a meeting, he sometimes goes through with it | The government |
| He arranged it, arrived, and it fell through for a reason outside him: nobody came, a deposit was demanded, or something at the place frightened him off | Little about his own will. It may support an inference that he meant to go through with it, perhaps on a condition. It proves no more than that | Mostly the government. It has some use on why he expected a scam (section 19.2) |
| He arranged it, arrived, the other person was there and ready, and he left | That for him arriving is not a sign that he will go through with it | Him |

3. **What marks the third kind in the records.**
   - A message from the other person that shows she was there and ready: a room number, an invitation to come up, or a question about where he was.
   - His location at the place, and for how long.
   - No payment, and no cash drawn and spent.
   - What he sent next: nothing, or an excuse.
   - What he did next: the app removed, the drive home.
   - Her reaction, if there is one. A complaint that he wasted her time is evidence that he did not meet her.
4. **Numbers matter.** One or two such occasions prove nothing. The proportion matters as well: how many of each kind, over what period. An occasion on which he texted and never set out shows nothing about what a drive means.
5. **How like May 1 they were matters.** On May 1 he wrote from the car park, asked her to come down, offered more, described his car and confirmed his lights (section 20.1). If the earlier occasions show the same, and then a departure, May 1 looks like the same sequence cut short by the arrest. If they were silent departures, the government will say that May 1 went further than he usually goes.
6. **The first kind exists, on his own account.** He has said that he used escorts for years (section 19.3). So an honest description is a mixture, and what separates the occasions on which he went in from those on which he left is the fact that decides its worth (31.3).
7. **The earlier occasion at the same hotel is one of these.** As he describes it, it belongs to the second kind (section 19.2).

### 31.2 What it would be worth, charge by charge

| Charge | What the pattern would do | Its limit |
|---|---|---|
| Spec 3 | It goes to the element that section 21.2 ranked least proved: a specific intent to engage in a sexual act, held when he did the act. It gives the argument about firmness of intent (section 20.3) facts to rest on | Intent is judged when he acts. An intent with a condition is probably still an intent. May 1 went as far as "Ya" (31.3) |
| Spec 2 | The same. It is a second answer, and it does not depend on what he believed about her age | The two answers have to be put with care: he did not know who she was, and he would not have gone through with it in any case (section 24.2) |
| Spec 1 | Nothing on the offense itself, which is the sending of words to a child and does not need a meeting. It helps only through the inferences about her age (point 1 below) | It may cut the other way. If the exchange and the drive are what he seeks, that fits a purpose to arouse or gratify |
| Texas | Probably nothing on guilt. The offense is the offer or the agreement (section 20.3) | Each earlier occasion on which a fee was agreed could be charged there as well |
| Negotiation and sentence | A good deal. A man who mostly does not go through with it is a different case from a man who does. An evaluator can say what the pattern is | It is the same record, with the same cost (31.5) |

1. **It answers three inferences about her age.** These bear on Specs 1 and 2.
   - Section 8.2 set out the government's reading of the interview: a man who thinks he is meeting an adult has little reason to hand over money and leave, so the thought fits someone he believed he should not touch. If he has left adults in the same way, the thought fits anyone.
   - The nervousness. The accused in *Puff* said that he had not been nervous in that way with adults (section 20.4). A record of the same hesitation with adults is the answer to that case.
   - The room he did not go to.
   - It does not reach the question that decides those charges, which is whether he knew whose number it was.
2. **It gives the removal of the apps a meaning.** Section 24.5 said that if earlier records show that he drove somewhere, removed the app and left without meeting anyone, then 17:02 looks like the start of the same sequence. That would be the best support the abandonment reading could have, and better still for firmness of intent.
3. **It fits the interview statement, and the cost of that statement stays.** As relayed, he told the agents that he had thought about "just giving money and leaving." With a record of earlier departures, that is no longer a claim made only after the arrest. The statement still has "she" as one person on both days (section 8.2), and he has since said that it was a general version and not what he thought (section 21.3). It cannot be right for one charge and wrong for another.
4. **It qualifies two things said earlier.** Section 21.4 said: "The better the routine is proved, the better this element is proved." That is true of a routine that ends in a paid meeting. If most of his sessions ended in the car park, the routine cuts the other way on that element. Section 17.3 said that habit could not be combined with slide 9's statement that he did not intend to meet anyone. A routine of not meeting would be the one form of habit that supports slide 9. Which routine he has is a question of fact, and the records will answer it.
5. ***Penaloza* is the model for the argument.** There the Army court held that travel "must be accompanied by evidence of a firm intent to complete the offense," and found "a fair and rational hypothesis" that the accused did not intend to pay, because he had no cash (section 20.4). A record of arriving and leaving would be offered for the same kind of hypothesis: that he did not intend to go through with it.

### 31.3 The government's answers

1. **Intent, not resolve.** Section 24.2 quoted the Army court in *Rios*, on a robber who lost his nerve: "The key is intent, not resolve. The appellant was weak and unable to carry through with his plan. His intent was, at the time, no less criminal despite his lack of will." On that reasoning, a man who means to go through with it each time he drives, and loses his nerve each time he arrives, has attempted it each time. So the pattern helps only as evidence of what he intends when he does each act the government relies on: the drive, and the messages from the car park (section 21.3). The more regular it is, the harder it is to say that he sets out with a firm intent. It can never show that directly, and an intent that was absent when he set out could still have formed at the hotel.
2. **An intent with a condition.** Section 19.3 noted the rule that an intent to do something if a condition is met is still an intent. He says that he drives although he expects a scam. If the records show that he went in whenever the woman proved to be real, the earlier departures show the condition and not a lack of intent. The government will then point to "Awesome," sent after the photo he had asked for as proof (section 22.2), and to her agreeing to come down. On its account, by 16:59 the condition had been met.
3. **May 1 itself.** His last messages moved toward a meeting, on terms he had proposed. Section 24.4 put it this way: by 17:01:20 nothing was left to arrange, and the next thing to happen was her arrival at his car. He had not left.
4. **The occasions that ended in a meeting.** Each one shows that the drive sometimes means what the government says it means.
5. **The arrest.** It came before he met anyone or left, so nobody can say what he would have done. The pattern is evidence of what was likely. The government will say that this time was different, and it will look for what made it so.
6. **A military case in which the accused did not turn up at all.** In *United States v. Hickerson*, 71 M.J. 659 (N-M. Ct. Crim. App. 2012), a Marine arranged online to meet a persona whose profile gave her age as 13. He "failed to show up at the meeting," and "testified that he never intended to actually meet" her. He was convicted of attempted enticement, and the court affirmed, holding that the arrangements he made "sufficiently demonstrated that the appellant took a substantial step toward enticement of a minor." Two things limit what that case says about his. The offense there was the persuading, which does not need an intent to carry out the act. And the members had his own admissions of a sexual interest in children, which the court called "highly probative of the appellant's intent and motive." Nothing of that kind is known here. The charge against him that works in that way is Spec 1.
7. **How courts have treated a claim that it was never going to happen.**
   - *United States v. Valle*, 807 F.3d 508 (2d Cir. 2015), was a conspiracy case. The court began: "This is a case about the line between fantasy and criminal intent." It upheld an acquittal, over a dissent. The chats said to show a real plan were "essentially indistinguishable" from the many that even the government treated as fantasy. The accused had traveled to another state and had lunch with one of the women discussed. The majority found it "impossible to conclude, without speculation," that the lunch was a step toward the crime.
   - The reasoning helps him in one way. Where the conduct said to show intent looks the same as conduct that led to nothing on other occasions, the inference of intent is weaker.
   - It is a different kind of case. The charge was an agreement and not an attempt. The lunch was with an old classmate, and his wife and child were with him. Here he drove to a hotel car park to meet someone from an ad. Section 20.4 lists the cases in which travel of that kind was held to be enough.
   - In *United States v. Gladish*, 536 F.3d 646 (7th Cir. 2008), where the accused had only talked, the court said: "The requirement of proving a substantial step serves to distinguish people who pose real threats from those who are all hot air." The government will say that a man who drives to the hotel and waits in the car park is not all hot air.

### 31.4 How it could be put before a panel

1. **As a habit, it probably fails.** The rule says that evidence of "a person's habit" may be admitted "to prove that on a particular occasion the person or organization acted in accordance with the habit or routine practice" (M.R.E. 406). A federal appeals court has listed what makes conduct a habit: "(1) the degree to which the conduct is reflexive or semi-automatic as opposed to volitional; (2) the specificity or particularity of the conduct; and (3) the regularity or numerosity of the examples of the conduct" (*United States v. Angwin*, 271 F.3d 786 (9th Cir. 2001)). Deciding whether to meet someone is a choice, and on his own account he did not always decide the same way. The rule is also about acts: it lets a habit prove that a person acted in accordance with it. On May 1 the arrest came before he either met her or left, so there is no act for a habit to prove. What is in dispute is what he intended.
2. **As character, it is limited to opinion and reputation.** An accused may offer evidence of a pertinent trait of his own. Under the federal rule, which the military rule follows as I understand it, the trait "may be proved by testimony about the person's reputation or by testimony in the form of an opinion," and not by particular occasions. It is hard to see who could give that opinion about conduct he kept to himself.
3. **As other acts offered on intent, it has a better chance.**
   - The rule bars evidence of other acts offered "to prove a person's character in order to show that on a particular occasion the person acted in accordance with the character" (M.R.E. 404(b)(1), as quoted in a 2025 opinion of the Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces). It allows such evidence for another purpose, and intent is one.
   - The rule is usually used by the government. *Hickerson* shows how: where the defense was fantasy and "intent was the sole issue in dispute," the accused's admissions about his other conduct came in against him.
   - Federal courts have often refused an accused's own evidence of what he did on other occasions. One put it flatly: "Evidence of good conduct is not admissible to negate criminal intent" (*United States v. Camejo*, 929 F.2d 610 (11th Cir. 1991)).
   - The question for the judge would be whether these occasions support an inference about his intent on May 1 that does not run through his character. Showing only that he usually leaves might not be enough. Counsel would have to state the link: that this is the same conduct with a known ending, offered on what the drive shows. The judge would also weigh the time it takes and the risk of confusion (M.R.E. 403).
   - If the government offers his history to prove his intent (section 7, item 5), the occasions that ended in the car park become part of the answer, and they are harder to keep out.
4. **Through an evaluator.**
   - Courts have said that an expert may describe a pattern of this kind. In *Gladish* the court said that a psychologist could not say what the accused intended, "but he could have testified that it was unlikely, given the defendant's psychology, that he would act on his intent." In *United States v. Hite*, 769 F.3d 1154 (D.C. Cir. 2014), where the defense was fantasy, the trial court "erred when it excluded" the defense expert's testimony "in its entirety."
   - Courts have also kept such experts out. In *United States v. Hofus*, 598 F.3d 1171 (9th Cir. 2010), the testimony was "directed solely at the propensity to actually commit the underlying sexual act, which was not before the jury," because the charge was enticement. In *United States v. Stahlman*, 934 F.3d 1199 (11th Cir. 2019), the expert was excluded under the federal rule that an expert "must not state an opinion about whether the defendant did or did not have a mental state" that is an element of the offense.
   - The *Hofus* objection has less force here. For Specs 2 and 3 the act itself is what he must have intended. Whether the military rules have the bar applied in *Stahlman* is for counsel.
   - Section 19.2 lists what offering such evidence brings with it: notice, an exception to the privilege for treatment, and an examination by the government.
5. **Through him.** He can say what he does. Every occasion is then open on cross-examination (section 21.5).
6. **Through the government's own records.** If the copy of his phone holds the earlier trips and messages, the pattern can be shown from the government's evidence, by an examiner, without his testimony. That is the form this analysis has preferred throughout (section 17.4).

### 31.5 What it opens

1. **The other occasions.** The records that show the departures show the meetings as well. The government could offer those to prove the intent he denies (section 7, item 5). Section 4 noted that the other recipients on May 1 make a mix-up plausible and cost sympathy. A longer history does the same on a larger scale.
2. **Each occasion may be an offense in itself.**
   - In the court-martial, an occasion on which he drove to meet an escort could be charged as a separate attempt. In *Baldwin* an officer was convicted of three attempts to patronize a prostitute (section 21.6). His answer to each would be the argument made here. As I understand it, the limitation period for most offenses under the Code is five years.
   - In Texas, a knowing offer or agreement to pay for sexual conduct is the offense (section 20.3). So an earlier occasion on which a fee was agreed could support a separate charge there, whether or not he met her. Whether an offer made with no intention of going through with it is within the buyer's statute is the question that section 20.3 left for his Texas counsel.
   - So anything he says about earlier occasions, in either forum, could admit Texas offenses. That is for his Texas counsel before it is said anywhere.
3. **It closes a plea to Spec 3.** A guilty plea needs an admission under oath that he intended the act (sections 11.5 and 21.5). He cannot say that he never meant to go through with it, and also plead guilty to having intended it. *Munn* ended in a plea to attempted patronizing (section 20.4). This argument and that outcome exclude each other, and which is true decides between them.
4. **It has to be the same account each time, and it has to fit his messages.** It is slide 9 again. Section 5 lists "I did not intend to meet anyone" among the claims that cost credibility, because nothing stood behind it. Records would change that. Earlier departures could have several explanations: that he arrives undecided, that he arrives meaning to go in and gives it up, or something else. The records cannot say which was his state on May 1. What he says about it has to come from what he remembers, and not from which version fits best. Section 19.2 observed that having no intention to meet fits the messages from the car park if it means that he had decided nothing, and does not fit them if it means that he had decided not to. That was an observation about fit, and so is this.
5. **"Compulsion" is the government's word too** (section 3). A compulsion to go is not a compulsion to stop. The records have to show both halves: that he goes, and that he leaves.
6. **A mental condition offered at trial has its own consequences** (section 19.2).

### 31.6 The same pattern with men, on a dating app

This takes the second question as it was put, as a possibility.

**What such occasions would add**

1. **They could be the cleanest of the third kind in 31.1.** When a paid meeting is abandoned, there are many possible causes outside him: a scam for money, the price, the police. Occasions of this kind would take those out of the comparison. No money is involved, and nothing about the meeting is unlawful. They would not exclude every outside reason. A profile can be false there too, and a man can leave for fear of being seen. Their worth would depend on what the records show.
2. **They would tend to show that the pattern is his, and not a feature of buying sex.** That would fit compulsion as he describes it (section 19.2): the going is the thing, whoever is at the other end.
3. **As described, they involve no offense that I can see, with one qualification.** As I understand it, Congress removed consensual sodomy from Article 125 in 2013, and conduct is not an offense because the two people are of the same sex. Other offenses depend on the circumstances. The qualification is marriage. The Manual defines the conduct covered by the offense of extramarital sexual conduct as certain acts "engaged in by persons of the same or opposite sex," and the offense applies where either person is married to someone else. An occasion with no meeting is not that conduct. But *Baldwin* included a conviction for an attempt at it (section 21.6). So if he or the other person was married, counsel has to weigh whether such an occasion could be charged as an attempt, with the argument made here as the answer.
4. **If everyone involved was an adult, they add to the record that his history is with adults** (section 3).
5. **They would give an evaluator more to work with** than the paid occasions alone.

**What they would cost**

1. **A disclosure that cannot be taken back.** As I understand it, a court-martial is open to the public, and what is offered there goes into the record. There may be two trials. His family and his unit could learn of it. Whether that is a price worth paying is for him and for nobody else, and it is not a decision to make in a hurry or in a document.
2. **Bias.** A panel member or a juror may hold it against him, though the law does not allow that. Counsel can question the members, and a member is to be excused who "should not sit as a member in the interest of having the court-martial free from substantial doubt as to legality, fairness, and impartiality" (R.C.M. 912(f)(1)(N), as the appeals court's digest states it). That reduces the risk. It does not remove it.
3. **Breadth reads two ways.** The defense reads it as a man who does not follow through. The government may read it as a man without restraint, which is its account of May 1 (section 3). The answer is the one already in the case: his history is with adults, and when a minor was mentioned he refused in 37 seconds.
4. **More of the account may come with it.** Offering some occasions does not make his whole private history admissible. It does give the government a reason to look at the rest, and to offer whatever cuts the other way: any meeting that did take place, which would make this pattern a mixed one too. If anyone in those conversations was under 18, or said so, counsel has to know that before anything else is decided. Other people could be drawn in, and investigators could go to them.
5. **A fight over relevance.** The setting is different: no fee, another app, men. The government may say that it shows nothing about a paid meeting with a woman, and a judge may keep it out as confusing or as taking too long (M.R.E. 403). The defense answer is that the sequence is the same, and the sequence is the point.
6. **It does not reach Spec 1,** or the question of whose number it was.

**What may already be out of his hands**

1. **If that app was on the phone on May 1, its data may be in the government's copy.** As relayed, DPS has held the phone since then (section 30.2). The scope of the warrant decides what the government may search for (section 22.11). An examiner who is looking for one thing still sees others.
2. **His orientation, whatever it is, has no bearing on guilt,** and as I understand it the government could not offer it as such. Particular messages or conduct could still be relevant in their own right, under the rules on searches and on evidence, whether or not the defense raises the subject.
3. **So counsel should hear of the possibility before the forensic report arrives.** Nothing in the report should come as a surprise to her, and she may want to look at the scope of the search.

**How to keep the choice open**

1. **Not here.** This document is not privileged (sections 11.5 and 29.7). The question is recorded as a possibility, and no detail should be added to it.
2. **Tell counsel in person.** She cannot weigh what she does not know, and what he tells her is privileged.
3. **Delete nothing:** not the account, the messages or the app. A deletion now would lose the evidence, and it would look like what section 24.3 describes.
4. **Contact nobody from those conversations.**
5. **Find out first, inside the privilege, what the records show.** What a consulting examiner finds can usually stay inside the defense (section 25.3). If the occasions are few, or mixed, the question answers itself and nothing need be disclosed.
6. **Use the other occasions first.** If the record with women carries the point, this one is not needed. It is a reserve.
7. **Work done in confidence by an evaluator engaged through counsel is generally protected.** That changes if the evaluator is called as a witness, or if evidence of a mental condition is offered (section 19.2). A clinician who treats him is under a different rule, with exceptions (sections 18.4 and 25.3). Counsel should explain both before he sees either.
8. **Nothing about this has to be decided this week.** Whatever he decides about the evidence, he does not have to carry it alone (section 27.7).

### 31.7 Records to add, through counsel

These add to sections 19.5 and 20.5, which already ask for earlier trips that ended without a meeting.

1. **For each earlier occasion, five facts:** what was arranged; whether he arrived; whether the other person was there and ready; who ended it, and how; and what he did next.
2. **The government's copy of the phone:** its location history for the months before May 1, the messages, and the record of installs and removals. Some of this is kept for a limited time, so the date of the copy matters (section 26.1).
3. **The texting provider's records of his other conversations.** They are in the same account, under the same six months (section 26.4). This is one more reason for the letter to the provider.
4. **The Google account's activity on earlier dates:** directions, hotel searches and image searches (section 13.7).
5. **Other records of where he was:** the carrier's, and the car's own, if it keeps any. Both need counsel.
6. **Bank and payment-app records:** cash drawn before a trip, and whether it was spent.
7. **The earlier occasion at the same hotel** (section 19.5).
8. **The dating app's own records,** if that is pursued. What it keeps, and for how long, I do not know.
9. **An evaluation,** arranged by counsel (section 7, item 7).

### 31.8 Questions it will draw

No answer shapes are offered.

1. "How many times did you go through with it?"
2. "What was different about the times you did?"
3. "So when she turns out to be real, you go in?"
4. "At 5:01 you confirmed that your lights were on. What had you decided?"
5. "If you never meant to meet anyone, why ask about cash?"
6. "You told the agents that you thought about giving her money and leaving. Is that what you usually do?"
7. On the second possibility: "Did you ever meet any of them?" "How old were they?" "Who else knows about this?"

### 31.9 What this changes

1. **The point about firmness of intent could become evidence.** Section 21.3 treated it as a gap in the government's proof, for counsel to press. A record of arriving and leaving would give it facts. That is new. It would be the first evidence for the defense on Spec 3 that comes from outside the day itself.
2. **Spec 2 would have a second answer. Spec 1 would not,** apart from the inferences about her age.
3. **Three inferences about her age would be weaker:** from the nervousness, from the room, and from the interview.
4. **Its worth depends on one kind of occasion,** and on how many there were beside the others. He has said that the others exist.
5. **Its cost is the other occasions,** in both forums, and a plea to Spec 3.
6. **Two earlier statements are qualified:** in section 17.3, on habit and slide 9, and in section 21.4, on the routine and Spec 3. Notes have been added.
7. **The second possibility is recorded as a possibility.** It could be the cleanest proof, and it carries the most personal cost. The decision is his, it can wait, and nothing in this analysis rests on it.
8. **There is one more reason for the letter to the provider.** His other conversations are in the same account.
9. **Section 1 has been updated, and section 7 has a new item, 19.**
10. **The order of work is unchanged:** the request to preserve the account, then the video, the forensic report, and the account.

## 32. Addendum 25, 26 Nov 2026: "Cash right?" and what was on the screen, and counsel's instruction in writing

**What this covers.** A point and a report supplied after section 31, recorded as relayed.

*As relayed: on the point made earlier about "Cash right?", which was used as a connection between the two dates. If the reference to cash came from the day before, he would not have asked, because the answer was already on the screen. It can also be viewed as confirming, or as stalling. But in general, if a thing was asked and answered, why ask what he could see on the screen, unless the thread from April 30 was not there?*

*As relayed: he came close today to logging in to the texting account, or more exactly to using the provider's support site. He stopped on rereading an email from his military counsel dated 5 November, which he supplied in full. On the additional charges that the email mentions: that prospect weighs less with him than getting in the way of her work, if the effort is genuine. Preferred charges mean nothing, as stated before, and viewing is not obstructing.*

The email is a privileged communication. It is not reproduced here, and 32.4 describes it in general terms only. I have not seen the account, the phone, his other conversations, the ad or the jail's record of his property. The provider's support pages and the rules quoted were read in extracts returned by a web search. Statements of law are my understanding, for counsel to confirm.

**Short version**

- **His point is a fair answer to the use that section 22.2 made of the message.** A man who has been told how to pay has less reason to ask than a man who has not. So the question does not, by itself, show that he knew whose number it was.
- **It does not show that the thread was gone.** By 16:51 the April 30 exchange about cash was 43 messages back, behind three photos. In the ordinary view of a thread it would have been far off the screen whether the thread had been deleted or not, and people ask again sooner than they scroll back.
- **The word that works against him is "right?"** It expects the answer. On April 30 he asked an open question, "Prefer cash or cashapp?" On May 1 he asked a closed one. The government will say that the difference is what he had learned in between. His answer is that cash is the usual answer, and his other conversations would show whether he asks in the same way of numbers that have told him nothing.
- **At its best the point is wider than one message.** Nothing he wrote on May 1 refers to April 30. He proposed a car again, as he had the day before, and he asked again about payment, which she had answered. The only words that can be read as looking back are "still" and "right?", and each has another reading.
- **So, on a tentative view, the message shows little either way about what he knew. It is still the government's on payment.** His other conversations could move that view in either direction.
- **It is an argument about what the message shows, and not a new account of what he meant.** Counsel can make it from the log, in the alternative, whether or not he testifies. What he cannot do is give two things as facts: that the message was typed into the wrong conversation (section 16.1), and that he asked her because he did not have her answer.
- **What would make it strong is the record this analysis has asked for since section 15:** what his account held for her number at 15:49.
- **He came close to the account today and stopped.** That was the right decision, and his reason for it is a good one.
- **The email changes one thing in this analysis.** Section 26.4 offered, as a last resort, a short written request of his own to the provider. Her instruction, as relayed, is against any investigating of his own, with no exceptions. A request through the provider's support site is a step of his own toward the evidence.
