That last resort is withdrawn unless she agrees to it.
- **He is right that looking is not obstructing.** Section 26.2 said the same. Her warning covers more than a look, and a charge that failed could still cost time. His own reason is the stronger one: she is doing the work, and the email shows it.
- **The email reports movement on the examiner.** As of 5 November the government had changed its recommendation, which had been to deny the request, and the commanding general's decision was hoped for in the following week. Three weeks have passed. Whether it came is the first question for her.
- **Her reading of the case and this one agree where it matters.** Even if the April 30 thread was not on his phone, the stated age and the return remain to be answered. Section 25.2 said the same. So the thread is not the whole case, whichever way it comes out, and no look at the account would end the waiting.
- **Two corrections to section 23.** As relayed from counsel, *Caroll* did involve a reverse image search, and the defense there was that he never believed she was a minor. And in *Ismaiel* the expert who agreed that the app could behave as the accused said was the State's own investigator, and not a defense expert.

### 32.1 The use made of the message, and his answer

1. **What section 22.2 said.** On April 30 he asked "Prefer cash or cashapp?" and was told "cash." At the hotel on May 1 he wrote "Cash right?" Section 22.2 said that the government could argue that "right?" asks her to confirm something he already knew, and that he could have learned it only from the April 30 conversation. That would be evidence that he knew whose number it was. Section 24.6 listed the message as one of two candidates for what the court in *Puff* called "contrary evidence in the conversation itself."
2. **That was this analysis looking ahead.** I have not seen the government make the argument. The Army report uses the message for payment: it says that he "confirmed the cash payment" (section 22.3). The passages highlighted in the packet do not include it, or the April 30 exchange about cash (section 22.1). A prosecutor may still see the link, so it is better answered now.
3. **His answer.** A man asks for what he does not have. If he had been told the day before how she wanted to be paid, and that exchange was in front of him, the question had no purpose. So the question points away from the April 30 thread, and toward a thread that was not there.
4. **What is right in it.** The link between the two messages can be read in either direction.
   - On the government's reading, he knew that he was back with the person who had told him "cash" a day earlier, and to whom he had answered "Got it." A man in that position had less reason to ask than a man who had been told nothing.
   - On his account, he was writing to a number that had told him nothing about payment that day. A question about payment before going up is then what one would expect.
   - So the fact that he asked is at least as easy to fit to his account as to the government's. That answers the inference. It is not by itself evidence for his account, because people do confirm what they have been told (32.2).
   - Section 22.2 set out the government's use of the message and gave one answer to it, the routine. This is a second answer, and it has one advantage over the first: it comes from the log, and needs no other record.

### 32.2 What the question can show, and what it cannot

1. **It cannot show what was on the screen at 16:51.** Between "cash" and "Cash right?" the log has 43 messages, three of them photos: eight more on April 30, and 35 on May 1 (section 22.2). The ordinary view of a thread shows its latest messages. In that view the April 30 exchange would have been far above the screen if the thread was whole, and absent if it had been deleted. Unless he scrolled back, which nothing shows either way, the question reads the same in both cases. How this app displays a thread is for the examiner (section 12.2). As a matter of ordinary experience, people ask again sooner than they scroll back.
2. **The time that matters for the screen is 15:49, and the question came an hour later.** If the thread was whole when he typed "Are you still around?", its last lines were her statement of her age, his refusal, "Not in rude way." and "k i get it" (section 12.2). Her answer "cash" was eight lines above the last one. Whether it was in view depends on the phone, and on whether the keyboard was up. The examiner's test of the screen would show it (section 12.2). A question put at 16:51 says little about what he saw at 15:49.
3. **The word "right?" expects the answer.** It asks for agreement with something the writer already believes. That belief came from somewhere, and there are three places it could have come from.

| Where the expectation came from | What it would mean | What would show it |
|---|---|---|
| The April 30 lines, in the thread he was writing in | The thread was whole | The provider's record of the thread, and the examiner's tests (section 25.2) |
| His memory of April 30 | The thread may have been gone, but he connected this number with the day before | Nothing direct. It is the inference the government wants drawn |
| Habit, the ad, or another conversation that day | Cash is what he expects of anyone, or what someone else had told him | His other conversations, the ad, and the conversation at about 15:18 (section 12.2) |

   His point is good against the first row. It does not reach the second, and the second is the one that hurts, because a man who remembered her terms remembered her. His account needs the third.
4. **The form changed between the two days.** On April 30, in his first conversation with this number, he asked an open question with two choices, straight after accepting the price: "Prefer cash or cashapp?" On May 1 he asked nothing about payment when the price was given. Nearly an hour later he asked a closed question that named one of the two. A prosecutor will say three things. The open question is how he asks a stranger. On May 1 he did not need to ask it. And the arrangement that day was a new one, with a second person and a new price, so that checking the terms with someone he had dealt with before was natural.
5. **The answers to that.**
   - A closed question suits the moment. At 16:51 he was parked at the hotel with the room number on his screen. The practical question was whether he had what was needed, and that is a question with a yes or a no for an answer.
   - Cash is the first of his own two choices, and it is what the agent answered. If his other conversations show that most numbers answer in the same way, "right?" needs no memory of this one. That is the answer section 22.2 gave.
   - The second choice may explain the question. A man who is used to offering a payment app has a reason to check that cash is wanted before he goes up.
   - Each of these is an inference, as the government's is. His other conversations are what would turn one of them into evidence (32.3).
6. **Confirming, or stalling.** He raises both, and each is a fair reading.
   - **Confirming** is the natural one. It is a question to her, about paying her, and it is evidence on the money element of Spec 3 (section 21.2). What he was confirming is the question in point 3.
   - **Stalling** fits what followed. He wrote "I'm here" at 16:48:28 and was sent the room number 17 seconds later. He answered "Ok" and did not go up. "Cash right?" came 22 seconds after that. It is the first of four messages, sent between 16:51 and 16:58, that came in place of going up: the others are "Im a little nervous," "Can you walk me upstairs?" and the message about the car. She answered "yes" within 16 seconds, which left nothing practical to settle, and he still did not move. A question whose answer he expected keeps a conversation going without taking it anywhere. On that reading the message belongs with sections 20 and 31.
   - The government reads the same four messages as steps toward a meeting on his own terms (section 20.2).
   - Why he asked and where the expectation came from are separate questions. A man can stall with a question whose answer he remembers. So neither reading settles what he knew, and on both he was writing to her about payment.
7. **At its best, the point is wider than one message.** On May 1 he went back over ground that he had covered with this number the day before, and he referred to none of it.
   - On April 30 he offered to come to her or to send a car. She told him "im at a hotel," and he asked which hotel. On May 1 he proposed a car again: "Can I send a Uber for you?" That is a proposal repeated. It is not a question that she had already answered, because she had not refused a car in terms.
   - On April 30 he asked how she wanted to be paid, was told "cash," and answered "Got it." On May 1 he asked about payment again. That is the one thing that had been asked and answered.
   - Nothing he wrote on May 1 refers to April 30: not to the refusal, the price of 50, the act he had named, or anything she had told him. A man who knew that he was going back to someone he had told to find a different way of life might be expected to say something about it. He wrote as a man starting again.
   - The only words that can be read as looking back are "still" and "right?" Each has another reading (sections 3 and 22.2).
   - Section 22.2 noted that his opening lines were the same on both days, and took that as support for a routine. The same lines also show a man who did not pick up where he had left off.
   - The government has answers. A routine repeats itself whoever is on the other end. He preferred the women to come to him, and so he proposed it again. May 1 was a new arrangement, with a second person. And a man going back after a refusal has reasons not to mention it. Those are fair. They explain the repetition. They do not turn it into evidence that he knew.
8. **A court has reasoned from facts of the same kind.** In *Ismaiel* the accused said that he did not know he was still writing to the person who had given her age. Of that claim the appeal court noted that he "was chatting with many different people" at the same time, which "could explain his confusion as to the identity of" the persona, and that the persona "never again identified herself" by name "or gave her age after the initial exchanges" (section 23.6). The second is true here on the log: nobody mentioned age on May 1. The first depends on records not yet seen. His timeline lists five other numbers that afternoon, and marks all of them as assumptions (section 9.4). That court was not weighing a repeated question, and I found no decision that does.
9. **Where that leaves the message.** On what he knew, my view is that it now shows little either way. That he asked is easy to fit to his account. The form of the question is easy to fit to the government's. That view is tentative, and his other conversations could move it in either direction. On payment the message is the government's, as before. This is a gain on section 22.2, and counsel can make the argument from the log alone. It is not evidence that the thread had been deleted. Only a record can show that.

### 32.3 How it sits with his earlier answers, and what would decide it

1. **This is an argument about what the message shows. It is not a new account of what he meant.** Two accounts of what he meant have been recorded. The deck, as section 4 recorded it, called the message a test for a scam (section 13.6). He has since said that "cash" stemmed from a separate conversation, and that the message was intended for someone else (section 16.1). The present point says nothing about what he meant. It takes the message as the government reads it, as a question put to her, and asks what follows.
2. **So counsel can make it in the alternative.** The argument runs: if this was a question to her, it is not one that a man who knew her terms needed to ask. It needs no evidence from him, and it withdraws nothing that he has said.
3. **What cannot be done is to give both as facts.**
   - If he testifies that the message was typed into the wrong conversation, then on his own evidence it was not a question to her. It then shows nothing about what he knew of her, in either direction.
   - If he testifies that he asked her because he did not have her answer, the message was meant for her.
   - A witness cannot say both. Section 16.8 said that "meant for someone else" should be weighed hard before it is used. This is one more thing to weigh: given as his own evidence, it would sit badly beside an argument that counsel can otherwise make freely.
   - The other reading in section 16.1 is different. On it the subject came from another conversation, and the question was still put to her. That sits with the present point without difficulty, and it concedes payment.
4. **If he testifies,** he will be asked what he meant by the message, and the answer has to be what he remembers. If he does not remember, that is the answer (section 13.6). No answer shapes are offered.
5. **What would decide it.**

| Record | If it shows | Then |
|---|---|---|
| What his account held for her number at 15:49 (section 25.2) | No April 30 lines | The first row of the table in 32.2 falls away, and the asking is easier to fit to his account |
| The same record | The April 30 lines | The answer was in the thread he was writing in, and the government's reading gains |
| His other conversations | The same closed question, put to numbers that had told him nothing | The form of the question is habit |
| The same conversations | The open question to each new number, and no question to a number that had already answered | The form on May 1 stands out |
| The conversation at about 15:18 on May 1 (section 12.2) | Cash discussed | A source for the expectation that is not April 30 |
| The ad (section 22.1) | A line about payment | The same |
| The jail's record of his money, and his bank records for 16:13 to 16:23 (sections 16.1 and 22.5) | Cash on him, in about the amount | He came ready to pay in cash, and the question was a check |
| The same records | Little or no cash | The question was a real one, or a stall. That also bears on Spec 3 (section 21.3) |

6. **The Ontario case shows the method.** In *Premanathan* the judge tested an account of a wrong recipient against the form of the accused's other conversations. They began with the service or the rate, and the one in question began "U do car call?" (section 23.5). A court asked what "Cash right?" shows would compare it with his other conversations in the same way.
7. **A correction to section 23, found while checking this point.** Sections 23.5 and 25.2 said that an expert in *Ismaiel* testified that the app could behave as the accused described, and section 23.6 called him a defense expert. On the opinion's words, as a web search returns them, he was the State's own investigator, who had been "designated an expert" on cases of this kind and on the use of the app. He testified that when a user deletes a thread, the other user "could still post a message to the thread, which would 'reinitiate' the conversation," and that the user who had deleted it "would not have access to the prior messages in the thread." That is the third case in section 15.2, described by a prosecution witness for a different app. It matters here in one way. The evidence of how that app treats a deleted thread came from the prosecution's side. The witnesses who ran the undercover system here can be asked what this app does. They may not know, which is a reason to have the examiner's answer first.
8. **What the same records cost.** His other conversations are other occasions (section 9.4), and section 31.5 sets out what they bring with them. Whether to rely on them is a decision for counsel, with the records in hand.

### 32.4 The email, in general terms

*As relayed, in general terms: the email is dated 5 November and answers four points that he had raised. It reports a conference with the government that morning. The government had at first recommended that the defense request for a forensic examiner be denied, and after the conference it changed its recommendation. Counsel hoped that the commanding general would approve the request in the following week. An examiner's review of the evidence does not by itself delay the case. The email repeats, as something discussed several times, that he is not to reinstall anything or to investigate on his own, under any circumstances. It extends that to the Texas case, where investigating is his Texas lawyer's job, and it warns that the government could prefer additional charges if he does. It gives her view of* Caroll *and names three facts that she regards as worse in his case. It notes his inclination to testify and leaves that decision for later. It encourages him to keep working with the providers he is seeing, and offers help if his command gets in the way of his appointments.*

1. **It is a privileged communication, and supplying it here is the kind of disclosure that section 29.7 warned about.** A summary in general terms was already a risk. The full text of a lawyer's advice is a larger one. It is not reproduced in this document. She should be told that it was supplied here, and nothing more from either lawyer should be (sections 11.5 and 29.7).
2. **It was written nearly three weeks before sections 26 to 30.** Those sections were written without its wording. Where they differ from it, her instruction governs, and 32.5 makes one change for that reason.
3. **What it shows about her work.** Section 29.3 said to judge the work by acts that can be checked. The email reports one: a conference with the government, and a recommendation changed in the defense's favor. It answers his questions one by one. It takes up the case he gave her. It takes his explanation about the missing thread seriously, and tells him what that explanation leaves unanswered. It attends to how he is. That bears out his own correction in section 30.

### 32.5 Today: the account, the support site, and one change to this analysis

1. **He came close and stopped.** He had a strong pull toward it and did not act on it. The decision was his, made on a holiday, with nobody watching. Sections 27 and 28 gave reasons for leaving the account closed that do not depend on any lawyer. He has added one of his own, and it is a good one: she is working on this, and a step of his own could get in the way.
2. **What the support site is.** The provider's help pages sit on a hosted support site that has its own "Sign in" and a form headed "Submit a request." As a web search returns it, the form asks for an email address, a subject, the reason for the request and "the phone number linked to your account." The provider's contact page says that someone who is not logged in, or does not have the app, can write to its support address by email. So a request made there need not be a login to the app. I could not open the pages, and I do not know what signing in to the support site involves.
3. **It would still be a step of his own toward the evidence.**
   - It would be a statement by him to a third party about the account, with none of the protection of a note to counsel (section 26.4).
   - It would ask for the number, which has expired, and for details of the account. An agent who answered might ask him to sign in, or might do something to the account. The provider's pages send users to support to get back a number they had before (section 27.1), and they describe a request to delete an account's data (section 25.1). He would not control what was done.
   - It is investigating on his own, on any ordinary reading of her instruction.
4. **So one thing in this analysis changes.** Section 26.4 offered a last resort for the case in which no lawyer could be reached: a short written request of his own to the provider, to keep everything and delete nothing. Sections 27.5 and 27.6 repeated it. That was written without her instruction in front of me. As relayed, the instruction has no exceptions, and a request of that kind falls within it. **The last resort is withdrawn unless she agrees to it.** What is left of it is a question for her: will she send such a letter, or ask the trial counsel to, and if not, does she object to two lines from him?
5. **What is still his to do, inside the instruction.**
   - **Write to her.** A note to his own lawyer is not investigating. If the page described in section 30.4 has gone to her, the thing to do is to wait for her answer. If it has not, it can go today. Her office is closed for the holiday, and the note carries today's date.
   - **Add three things to it.** That he came close today, said plainly (section 30.4). The questions in 32.7. And the point about "Cash right?", on one page, if she wants it. As relayed, the email thanks him for the material he had sent and says that it goes into his file, so that is a channel that works.
   - **Ask the Texas firm for the letter as well.** As relayed, the email says that investigating in the Texas case is his Texas lawyer's job. A letter from that firm asking the provider to keep the account is within that (section 29.5).
   - **Keep what he has.** The provider's emails, the receipts for the plan and his Google activity, untouched, with no link followed (sections 13.7 and 27.1).
   - **Leave the rest alone.** The account, the support site, the ad site, the profile, and every number from that afternoon.
6. **The nearest date.** One reading of the provider's policy puts the end of its six months at about 30 November, which is Monday (section 27.1). On other readings the date has passed, or is weeks or months away. Nobody knows which.
   - That is the reason for the note to go today, and to name the date.
   - I cannot say that a request to the support desk, sent today, would be read before Monday, or that a support agent can stop a deletion that runs by itself. If both were so, it would have an advantage over a lawyer's letter sent on Monday. That is uncertain, and it has to be set against point 3 and against a written instruction from a lawyer he trusts.
   - He weighed it today and decided to stay inside the instruction. On what is known, that was sound.
7. **How he is.** As relayed, he has providers. Coming close today is worth telling them, and her. The Military Crisis Line is there in the meantime, at 988, then 1 (section 27.7).

### 32.6 Whether viewing is obstructing, and what a charge costs

1. **He is right about a look.** As I understand the offenses set out in sections 24.3 and 25.4, each needs an intent to obstruct the proceedings or to impair evidence, and most need something to be altered, destroyed or concealed. Section 26.2 said that a look at his own account, made without that intent and changing nothing, meets none of them. That stands.
2. **Her warning is wider than a look.** As relayed, it covers reinstalling anything and any investigating of his own, in either case. Some things that those words cover could be charged, or could become evidence against him: a message sent from a new second number, contact with anyone from that afternoon or with the owner of the profile, a return to the ad site, a deletion. A login is also more than a look, because it needs a reinstall and probably a new number (section 28.3).
3. **Her instruction is advice.** Departing from it is not by itself a breach of any order or condition. A condition of his bond, or an order from his command, would be a separate thing, and only he and his lawyers know whether there is one (section 27.3).
4. **To say that "preferred charges mean nothing" is right about proof and wrong about cost.** A charge is an accusation, and the government would still have to prove it. He knows better than most what an accusation costs before it is tested.
   - **Time.** The rule on joining charges says: "Additional charges may be joined with other charges for a single trial at any time before arraignment if all necessary procedural requirements concerning the additional charges have been complied with." It goes on: "After arraignment of the accused upon charges, no additional charges may be referred to the same trial without consent of the accused" (R.C.M. 601(e)(2)). I do not know whether he has been arraigned. Either way, a new charge could mean more process and more time, whether it joined this trial or was dealt with apart from it. How much is for counsel. The waiting is what he finds hardest.
   - **Evidence.** What an accused does about evidence after his arrest can be offered to show a guilty mind, whether or not it is charged (section 24.3). In *Kerns* the obstruction charge failed. In *Puff* the deletion of the app was counted as evidence that the accused knew the age.
   - **Restraint.** Decisions about restraint before trial are made before any conviction, on findings that need less than proof beyond a reasonable doubt. A defense firm's page sets out the rule on confinement before trial as requiring probable cause and, among other things, that it is foreseeable that the accused "will engage in serious criminal misconduct" and that lesser forms of restraint are inadequate. It says that the rule defines that phrase to include obstruction of justice (R.C.M. 305(h)(2)(B)). I did not see the rule's own page. In Texas, after a hearing, a bond can be revoked if a magistrate finds "by a preponderance of the evidence" that a condition of a certain kind was violated (Code of Criminal Procedure art. 17.40). Each of those needs a finding, and an allegation alone is not enough for either. I do not suggest that a look at his own account would lead to either.
   - **Position.** A new charge, however weak, is something the government can offer to drop.
5. **His own ordering is the right one.** The risk of a charge from a look alone is small. The cost to her work is not.
   - The examiner she has asked for is to examine the evidence as it stands. A login changes the provider's side of it before the examiner sees it (section 26.2).
   - Whatever she tells the government about the account rests on his having left it alone (section 27.3).
   - If a step of his own is ever taken, the thing to do is what section 25.4 says: stop, change nothing, and tell her at once. A near miss is worth telling her too. She can only plan around what she knows.
6. **The condition he attaches: "if they are genuinely making efforts."** The email is evidence that she is (32.4). The way to go on testing it is the one in section 29.5: questions with answers that can be checked.

### 32.7 What the email adds to the analysis

**The examiner**

1. **The slow step was already under way.** Sections 29.4 and 30.1 left open whether an examiner had been requested. As relayed, by 5 November the request had been made, the government had changed its recommendation, which had been to deny it, and the decision was with the commanding general.
2. **The decision was hoped for in the week of 9 November, and it is now 26 November.** Whether it came, and who the examiner is, is the first question for her.
3. **If the examiner has been approved, the questions that sections 25 to 30 collected can go to the examiner:** what the provider holds and for how long, whether it logs deletions, and how a capture of the account should be made. Putting the provider's six months in front of the examiner, through her, is the quickest proper route.
4. **The provider is still not covered.** As relayed, the email describes the examiner's work as a review of evidence in the government's file. The provider's records may not be in that file (section 29.2). So the email does not answer who has asked the provider to keep the account. That is still the question to put.
5. **Referral.** He has said that a request of this kind is made after charges are referred (section 30). If so, the charges had been referred by 5 November. The email does not say so, and it does not say whether he has been arraigned or when a trial is expected. The fourth question for her in section 29.5 stands.

***Caroll***

1. **What is public is unchanged.** The Army's release names the site and says nothing of a search, or of the defense (section 23.2).
2. **What counsel adds.** As relayed, the case did involve a reverse image search. It did not involve a refusal. The defense was that he never believed the person was a minor, because the app was one for fantasy and not one tied to commercial sex.
3. **That changes section 23.** *Caroll* shares at least two of the five features, and not one: a stated age and a search. It also shares the claim at the center of his own case, that he did not believe she was a minor. A panel in the same court, two months ago, heard that claim in a case with an image search in it, and convicted. The sentence was 16 years.
4. **What that does and does not show.** It shows that such a defense can fail before a panel at Fort Hood. It does not show why. I do not know what the search found, which side relied on it, or how the panel was instructed. And the facts were different: there the persona was a child from the first message, the sexual messages went on for days, and nothing suggests a refusal (section 23.2).
5. **What to ask her,** since she knows that trial and this analysis does not: what the search found, how each side used it, whether he testified, and how the panel was instructed on belief. In *Felder* the accused's own searches were used as proof of what he believed (section 23.5). The instruction matters most (section 23.5, point 4).

**The facts she names as worse in his case**

1. **As relayed, there are three:** that the agent said she was underage, that he went back, and that the app he was using is tied to commercial sex. The first two are the two that section 1 has carried since the first draft. The first is common to both cases on what is public, because the detective in *Caroll* posed as a girl of 14 (section 23.2). I take her point to be that where the setting is one for fantasy, a stated age can be argued to be part of it, and that here the argument is harder.
2. **The third cuts in more than one direction.** I take it to refer to the setting: an ad on a site for commercial sex, answered from a second-number app.
   - On Spec 3 it is the government's (section 21).
   - On Specs 1 and 2 it makes one argument harder than it was in *Caroll*: that a stated age was part of a fantasy. His refusal shows that on April 30 he did not take it as one. What he believed on May 1 is a separate question, and it is the one the charges turn on (section 1).
   - It also supports the other half of his account. The site is one for adults, his search of the ad's photo led to an adult, and the pictures he was sent were of adults. The acquittals reported in section 23.5 all rested on photographs of adults, though that argument has not won an appeal.
3. **Her point about the missing thread is this analysis's point too.** As relayed, even if the earlier chat was not on his phone when he went back, the stated age and the app he was using remain to be answered. Section 25.2 put it this way: "The deletion takes the exchange off his screen. It does not take it out of his memory."
4. **What follows for him.** He has put a great deal on the thread, and has called a look at it a moment of truth (section 28.6). His lawyer and this analysis say the same thing from two sides. If the thread was gone, there is still a case to answer. If it was there, his best facts are still his (section 27.7). So no look at the account would end the waiting, and none would decide the case.

**Testifying.** As relayed, she has noted his inclination and left the decision for later. That is where sections 5 and 29.3 leave it. The decision is his, and it comes after the video and the forensic report.

### 32.8 Records to add, through counsel, and questions the point will draw

**Records.** These add to sections 22.11 and 25.9.

1. **His other conversations:** how he asks about payment, at what point, in which form, and whether he asks again of a number that has already answered (32.3).
2. **The conversation at about 15:18 on May 1:** whether payment was discussed in it (section 12.2).
3. **The ad:** whether it said anything about payment (section 22.1).
4. **The jail's record of his money,** and his bank records for the stop between 16:13 and 16:23 (sections 16.1 and 22.5).
5. **The screen at 15:49 and at 16:51:** what the app showed, and how far back the April 30 lines were (section 12.2).
6. **The record of the trial in *Caroll*:** what the search found, how it was used, and the instruction on belief (32.7).

**Questions.** No answer shapes are offered. Each answer has to be what he remembers. The second arises only if he himself gives evidence about whom the message was for.

1. "On April 30 you asked her 'Prefer cash or cashapp?' and she said cash. The next day you wrote 'Cash right?' What were you checking?"
2. "Was 'Cash right?' meant for her, or for someone else?"
3. "On April 30 you asked 'Prefer cash or cashapp?' as soon as the price was agreed. On May 1 she gave you a price and you did not ask. Why not?"
4. "She had just sent you the room number. Why ask about money if you did not mean to pay?"
5. "How much cash did you have with you?"

### 32.9 What this changes

1. **"Cash right?" does less for the government than section 22.2 suggested.** That he asked is at least as easy to fit to his account as to the government's, and nothing he wrote on May 1 refers to April 30. On a tentative view the message shows little either way about what he knew. It is still the government's on payment. Sections 1, 6, 22.2 and 24.6 have notes.
2. **It is not evidence that the thread was gone.** The exchange about cash was 43 messages back by 16:51 in either case, and "right?" expects the answer. The record of what his account held at 15:49 is still what decides it.
3. **It is an argument for counsel, in the alternative, and not a new account of what he meant.** He cannot give it as fact together with a message typed into the wrong conversation. Sections 13.6 and 16.1 have notes.
4. **The last resort in section 26.4 is withdrawn unless counsel agrees to it.** Her instruction, as relayed, covers a request of his own to the provider. Sections 26.4, 27.5 and 27.6 have notes, placed where the request was described.
5. **Looking is not obstructing, and the instruction stands for reasons that are not about charges.** A charge that failed could still cost time. The stronger reason is his own: her work.
6. **The examiner.** As relayed, the request had been made by 5 November, and the government had changed its recommendation, which had been to deny it. Whether the commanding general approved it is the first question for her. Sections 29.5 and 30.1 have notes.
7. **Two corrections to section 23.** *Caroll* involved a reverse image search and a defense that he never believed she was a minor, as relayed from counsel. The expert in *Ismaiel* was the State's own investigator. Sections 1, 23, 23.2, 23.5, 23.6 and 25.2 have notes.
8. **His counsel's reading and this one agree on what is hardest:** the stated age and the return. Both say that the thread, whichever way it comes out, is not the whole case.
9. **Section 1 has been updated, and section 7 has a new item, 20.**
10. **The order of work is unchanged:** the request to preserve the account, then the video, the forensic report, and the account.
